# Sūrah Yā-Sīn (Chapter 36) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:1

> Yâ-Sĩn

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Detached Letters Begin a Sūrah of the Heart**

Yā-Sīn opens the sūrah with two detached Arabic letters, yāʾ and sīn, without a verb, without a predicate, without any immediate gloss. The Qur'an uses this device at the start of twenty-nine sūrahs, and the earliest listeners recognized it as a distinct mode of speech. These openings are called al-muqaṭṭaʿāt, the disconnected letters, and they arrive as sound before sense. The sūrah known as the heart of the Qur'an begins not with an argument but with a sound that suspends argument, and that suspension is part of its rhetoric. A hearer who expects a proposition first meets two letters whose sense he cannot fix, which forces attention onto the speaker rather than the sentence. The name Yā-Sīn became the popular title of the chapter precisely because no other chapter opens with this exact pair, and the pair itself became a marker that the message to follow carries weight beyond ordinary discourse.

**Why the Meaning Is Left With God**

The dominant position among the Companions and early exegetes is that the precise intent of Yā Sīn belongs to divine knowledge. Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd, and a group of Successors treated the disconnected letters as part of the mutashābih, the ambiguous whose interpretation is deferred. This is not an evasion but a theological claim about language. The Qur'an elsewhere describes itself as containing verses that are decisive and others that are ambiguous, and it praises those who say *“We believe in it; all of it is from our Lord”* (3:7). To leave Yā Sīn without a fixed translation preserves that posture. It tells the reader that revelation includes what is plain and what is withheld, and that submission covers both. Al-Ṭabarī records this view first and gives it precedence, while still collecting other opinions after it. The wisdom is pedagogical: if every opening were immediately transparent, the reader would mistake understanding for mastery.

**Yā Sīn as a Name and a Call**

A second line of interpretation reads Yā Sīn as an address. In Arabic yā is the vocative particle, O, and sīn is taken as a contraction of insān, human being, or as an affectionate diminutive. On this reading the phrase means O man, or O little human, spoken tenderly to the Prophet Muḥammad. The appeal of this reading is contextual: the very next verses say *“You are truly one of the messengers upon the Straight Path”* (36:3-4), so an opening call to the man who is then confirmed as messenger forms a coherent sequence. Other reports present Yā Sīn as one of the Prophet's own names, given by God in the Qur'an alongside Muḥammad and Aḥmad. One report attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib lists seven names said to be given to the Prophet in the Qur'an, including Yā Sīn and Ṭā Hā, though the chain of that report is debated among hadith critics and is often cited without a graded number. Whether as name or call, this reading personalizes the opening: the sūrah of resurrection, signs, and town messengers begins by naming its immediate addressee.

**The Disconnected Letters Tie This Sūrah to Its Argument**

The disconnected letters are not ornamental. In almost every sūrah that opens with them, the text that follows immediately mentions revelation, the Book, or the Qur'an itself, as if the letters were a reminder of the raw material of the message. Here verse two follows with an oath: *“By the Quran, rich in wisdom!”* (36:2). The sequence mirrors Ṭā Hā, Yā Sīn, Ṣād, Qāf — each time letters built from the same alphabet the Arabs used for poetry and dispute become the building blocks of a book they cannot match. The sūrah later argues from signs in dead earth, gardens, night and day, sun and moon, ships and cattle. All those signs are external. The opening sign is internal to language: these are your letters, yet you cannot produce their like. The Qur'an elsewhere makes that challenge explicit: *“If you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sūrah like it”* (2:23). Yā Sīn places the reader inside that challenge before any proof is offered.

**How the Ear Hears Before the Mind Decides**

Sound matters in Yā Sīn. The sūrah is recited more than any other in gatherings for the sick, the dying, and the dead, and its cadence is part of why. The long ī in Yā and Sīn stretches the mouth and slows the breath, creating a pause that the reciter must respect. Early rhetoricians noted that sūrahs opening with two or three letters often have a distinct musical profile that returns in their verse endings. In Yā Sīn the fāṣilah, the verse ending, frequently returns to īn, īm, ūn sounds that echo the opening. This is not accidental decoration. The sūrah's central themes are warning, remembrance, and return, and those themes are carried by a rhythm that keeps returning. The listener hears return in sound before he is told about return in meaning. That is why the opening with two letters works: it teaches by listening, not only by defining. The Prophet's own recitation was described as measured and clear, letter by letter, and openings like Yā Sīn give that measure a starting point.

**An Opening That Establishes Authority Without Explanation**

Yā Sīn claims authority precisely by not explaining itself. A human author anxious to be understood would begin with a thesis. This text begins with what its first audience could not fully parse and then immediately swears by the wisdom of the Qur'an that follows. The oath in 36:2 functions as a seal on the opaque opening: you may not know what Yā Sīn means, but you are told what kind of book follows it. That order reverses ordinary persuasion, which explains first and then asks for trust. Here trust is asked first, explanation follows across eighty-three verses. The structure mirrors how signs work in the rest of the sūrah. Dead earth looks inert until God gives it life, *“producing grain from it for them to eat”* (36:33). Night looks permanent until daylight is stripped away. The sun appears to wander until its fixed term is declared. Each sign begins as something taken for granted or not understood, then is reframed. Yā Sīn does the same with language itself. Two letters that look too small to matter become the doorway to a discourse about life, death, and return, and the doorway remains partly closed, so the reader enters with humility.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:2

> By the Quran, rich in wisdom

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath That Answers the Opening**

The sūrah opened with two unexplained letters, and the immediate oath by the Qur'an gives those letters a home. Arabic oaths work by invoking something weighty as witness, and here the witness is the book itself. *“By the Quran, rich in wisdom!”* (36:2) is not a decorative flourish. It tells the listener that what follows Yā Sīn is not a puzzle for its own sake but a book whose character is ḥakīm. The word translated as rich in wisdom carries three strands that early lexicographers recorded: wise in itself, firmly constructed so falsehood cannot enter it, and wisdom-giving to those who receive it. All three are active at once. The verse that says *“falsehood cannot approach it from before it or behind it”* (41:42) explains the second strand, while the description of the Qur'an as a book that guides to the straight path explains the third. Placing the oath directly after the disconnected letters also answers a silent question: if Yā Sīn is obscure, the Qur'an as a whole is not obscure in purpose; its wisdom is accessible even when its openings are not fully decoded.

**What Ḥakīm Means for This Sūrah**

Ḥakīm in this verse is usually rendered wise, but the commentators note it can also mean muḥkam, made firm, or muḥkim, that which makes wise. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both mention that the Qur'an is called ḥakīm in several places, including *“Alif, Lām, Rā. These are the verses of the wise Book”* (10:1) and *“By the clear Book”* (43:2) followed by its description as wise. The firmness reading points to structure: verses are set in place, guarded, not open to tampering. The wisdom-giving reading points to effect: this book produces ḥikmah, sound judgment, in its reader. Yā Sīn needs both meanings. The sūrah will move through stories, natural signs, resurrection arguments, and eschatological scenes. What holds those diverse materials together is not topic but wisdom — a way of connecting dead earth to raised bodies, sun and moon to accountability. Calling the Qur'an ḥakīm at the start promises that the transitions to come are not random; they follow a wise arrangement that the reader will only see after traversing the whole chapter.

**Why an Oath by the Qur'an Itself**

Oaths in the Qur'an usually invoke created things — sun, moon, fig, olive — to point to the Creator. An oath by the Qur'an is rarer and more self-referential. It occurs in Sūrah Ṣād, *“By the Qur'an containing the Reminder”* (38:1), and in other openings. The logic is that the Qur'an is the most immediate evidence of what it claims. The Arabs who first heard it were judges of speech; they knew poetry, oratory, and soothsaying. Swearing by the Qur'an puts that literary experience on the stand. If the book is wise, firmly made, and wisdom-giving, then its claim about the messenger in the next verse stands on something the audience can test. The sūrah later says *“We have not taught him poetry, nor is it fitting for him”* (36:69), separating revelation from familiar genres. The oath in 36:2 begins that separation. Poetry can be wise in moments; this book is characterized as wisdom throughout, not incidentally.

**The Relation Between Wisdom and Warning**

Wisdom in the Qur'anic vocabulary is not cleverness but putting each thing in its proper place. That definition matters because the sūrah will warn a people described as heedless. Heedlessness, ghaflah, is the opposite of wisdom; it is living as if signs were not signs. By swearing that the Qur'an is rich in wisdom, the verse establishes the standard against which heedlessness will be measured. The people are not blamed for lacking information; they are blamed for missing the point of information they already have. Earth that produces grain, gardens of palms, night stripped of day — all these are mentioned later as signs, but their sign-character only appears to a mind seeking wisdom. A heedless eye sees agriculture, not a sign. The oath therefore does more than praise the book; it defines the failure it will diagnose. To ignore a wise book is not a neutral choice; it is a refusal of the very faculty that makes a human responsible.

**How This Oath Functions in Recitation and Memory**

Those who memorize Yā Sīn often note how verse two anchors the opening. Yā Sīn alone could be forgotten or misheard as a name; *“By the Quran, rich in wisdom!”* (36:2) fixes it as scripture. In congregational recitation, the pause after verse one and the lift into the oath create a small drama: silence, then attestation. Classical reciters prolong the oath slightly, giving ḥakīm its full weight. This is also why Yā Sīn is called the heart of the Qur'an in a well-known report, *“Everything has a heart, and the heart of the Qur'an is Yā Sīn”* recorded in Sunan al-Dārimī and Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī, though its chain is graded as weak by many critics; the sentiment it expresses is that this sūrah concentrates themes found elsewhere. An oath at the start that names the Qur'an's central quality — wisdom — supports that concentration. The reader is told at the threshold what kind of book he has entered, so the stories and arguments that follow are received not as scattered topics but as facets of one wise address.

**Wisdom as a Guard Against Distortion**

A book described as ḥakīm is also protected from distortion, and that protection matters for a community receiving revelation after a long gap without a warner. The Qur'an says *“Indeed, it is We who sent down the Reminder and indeed, We will be its guardian”* (15:9). The oath by the wise Qur'an therefore carries a promise: this wisdom will remain intact for those who come later. Unlike earlier scriptures that were altered, this one is firmly made, muḥkam, and its firmness is part of its wisdom. The early Muslims understood this quickly; they preserved the text letter by letter, including the disconnected letters themselves, even when their meaning was debated. To swear by the Qur'an is thus to swear by something that will remain available as proof. The sūrah's later arguments about resurrection rely on that availability. If the book that warns about the Last Day were itself subject to loss, its warning would be uncertain. By calling it rich in wisdom at the outset, the sūrah assures its hearers that its guidance will stay clear and its structure will stay firm, so the choice it presents remains meaningful for every generation that hears it.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:3

> You ˹O Prophet˺ are truly one of the messengers

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Direct Confirmation After an Oath**

The oath in the previous verse required an answer, and this verse provides it. *“You are truly one of the messengers”* (36:3) is addressed to the Prophet Muḥammad, and the particle of emphasis plus the oath together form one of the strongest affirmations in the Qur'an. The structure is oath, then statement: By the wise Qur'an, you are among the messengers. In Arabic rhetoric that pairing is deliberate. The truth of the second clause is guaranteed by the weight of the first. If the Qur'an is wise and firmly constructed, then its testimony about its bearer is trustworthy. The sūrah thus moves in three beats: obscure letters, a book described as wise, a man described as sent. The progression trains the listener to accept that guidance comes through a human messenger carrying a wise book that begins with what humans cannot fully explain. All three elements — mystery, book, man — are necessary for the sūrah's later arguments about resurrection, which will also require trust beyond immediate sight.

**What “One of the Messengers” Implies**

Saying one of the messengers rather than the messenger places Muḥammad within a lineage. The Qur'an repeatedly stresses that he is not an innovation among messengers: *“Say, I am not something original among the messengers”* (46:9). The lineage includes Nūḥ, Ibrāhīm, Mūsā, ʿĪsā and many others, and the sūrah will soon give an example of earlier messengers sent to a town. By saying one of, the verse does two things. It dignifies the Prophet by joining him to a known, honored company, and it normalizes opposition to him. If previous messengers were rejected and mocked, then rejection of this messenger is not evidence against him; it is evidence that his community follows an old pattern. The verse also quietly answers a Meccan objection that a messenger should be an angel. If he is one of the messengers, and all messengers before him were men who ate food and walked in markets, then his humanity is not a defect. The Qur'an states that principle elsewhere: *“We never sent before you any but men to whom We revealed”* (16:43).

**Why the Second Person Matters**

The address is in the second person singular, you. The sūrah does not say Muḥammad is a messenger in the third person; it speaks to him directly. That direct address has a pastoral function. The Prophet faced denial, mockery, and the accusation that he was a poet or possessed. A verse that says you are truly among the messengers is consolation as well as doctrine. It tells him that his status does not depend on public acceptance. The same second-person reassurance appears throughout the Meccan period: *“So do not let their words grieve you”* (36:76) near the end of this same sūrah. The shift from third-person report to second-person address is a known device in Qur'anic consolation. It moves the Prophet from being talked about to being talked to. For the listener, the effect is to overhear a private confirmation, which paradoxically makes it more credible than a public proclamation.

**The Connection to Yā Sīn as a Name**

If Yā Sīn is read as a name or vocative for the Prophet, as some early reports suggest, then verse three becomes an explanation of verse one. Yā Sīn, you are among the messengers. The name calls, the next verses describe. Al-Qushayrī and others who took Yā Sīn as an affectionate address saw this as natural: O little human, you are among those sent. Even if one does not adopt that linguistic derivation, the narrative flow supports it. The sūrah's title became Yā Sīn because its opening is unique, and its message is about messengership, so the title and theme converge. In popular devotion, people named their children Yā Sīn hoping to share in that convergence, though scholars cautioned that the primary meaning remains with the disconnected letters whose exact sense is with God. Both readings can coexist: the letters retain their mystery, and the address retains its intimacy.

**How This Verse Frames the Town Story to Come**

The affirmation that you are among the messengers prepares for verses thirteen onward, where *“the residents of a town, when the messengers came to them”* (36:13) are described. The Prophet's own experience of rejection is placed alongside an older town's rejection, so his community sees their behavior in a mirror. The town received two messengers, then a third, and still said *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15). The Quraysh said the same about Muḥammad. The parallel is not accidental. By first confirming that you are truly one of the messengers, the sūrah establishes the standard case, then shows the repeated pattern of denial. The reader who accepts the first affirmation is led to see the town's deniers as unjust, and by extension to see Meccan denial as unjust. The verse is therefore not only theology but strategy: it secures the premise before presenting the example that will test the audience's consistency.

**A Verse That Defines Prophethood as Service, Not Privilege**

Being one of the messengers in Qur'anic language does not mean being elevated above accountability; it means being entrusted with a clear delivery. The next verses define that duty as only to deliver the message clearly (36:17). Prophethood is thus framed as responsibility rather than status. This matters for the sūrah's later critique of those who take gods hoping to be helped (36:74-75). True help comes from God, not from intermediaries, and the messenger does not claim intercession as his own power. He delivers. The confirmation in 36:3 is therefore modest in one sense: you are one among many, not a new kind of being. It is monumental in another: among all humans, you are among those chosen to carry the wise Qur'an. The tension between modesty and magnitude is the Qur'anic portrait of prophethood, and this verse holds both together in a single line.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:4

> upon the Straight Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Path Described in Three Words**

*“Upon the Straight Path”* (36:4) is a complete sentence fragment that continues the affirmation before it: you are among the messengers, upon the straight path. The brevity is eloquent. The Qur'an does not say you follow the straight path or you guide to it here; it says you are upon it, as one stands on a road. The preposition ʿalā, upon, conveys firmness and elevation, as if the path itself carries the walker. The same construction appears in the opening sūrah, where believers pray *“Guide us to the Straight Path”* (1:6), and the answer across the Book is that messengers are already upon it and invite others onto it. The straightness, istiqāmah, is not merely moral uprightness but absence of crookedness, deviation, or hidden trap. A straight path is the shortest distance between two points — in this case, between the human condition and divine pleasure — and therefore the most merciful route.

**Why Straightness Is the Chosen Image**

The Qur'an could have described the Prophet as truthful, wise, or chosen. It chooses a spatial image. Path imagery works because it captures both direction and movement. A path can be seen, followed, left, or blocked. The sūrah later describes barriers placed before and behind those who refuse to see (36:9), which is the inverse of being upon a path: instead of an open road ahead, there is a wall. The straight path also implies that other paths exist and are not straight. The sūrah will later warn *“Did I not command you, O Children of Adam, not to follow Satan, for he is truly your sworn enemy”* (36:60) and then *“but to worship Me alone? This is the Straight Path”* (36:61). The same term returns at the end as at the beginning, forming an inclusio. What begins as a description of the messenger's location ends as an invitation to the audience to stand where he stands. The image thus carries the whole sūrah.

**The Straight Path Is Not an Abstraction**

In early Arabic usage ṣirāṭ meant a broad, clear road, often a Roman road, raised and visible. It was not a narrow track through brush but a public way. Translating it as straight path preserves straightness but can lose breadth. The Qur'an's straight path is wide enough for a community, not a tightrope for individuals. It accommodates the signs that follow — grain, gardens, night, sun, moon, ships, cattle — all of which are on that path as evidence, not distractions from it. To be upon the straight path is therefore to see creation correctly. The dead earth that is given life (36:33) is not a detour from theology; it is on the path, because resurrection is the path's destination. The sun traveling for its fixed term (36:38) is not astronomy for its own sake; it is a milestone on the same road, showing that movement has a term. The path metaphor integrates nature, history, and eschatology.

**Messengership and Path Are Linked**

The verse links two ideas: being among the messengers and being upon the straight path. The link matters because it defines what messengers do. They do not invent a path; they are placed upon one and call others to it. The Qur'an says of the Prophet elsewhere *“You guide to a straight path”* (42:52), which complements rather than contradicts being upon it. One cannot guide to a road he does not himself walk. The town messengers later say *“Our duty is only to deliver the message clearly”* (36:17). Their clarity is a function of their location: because they are upon the straight path, their call is straight. A crooked caller cannot issue a straight call. This is why the sūrah emphasizes that its messengers ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21). Guidance is not only content but position. The straight path authenticates the messenger, and the messenger makes the straight path visible.

**A Short Verse That Carries a Theological Weight**

Three words in Arabic, four in English, yet this verse carries a theological weight that exegetes have expanded. Is the straight path the Qur'an itself, Islam as a way of life, or the Prophet's own character? The commentators answer that it is all three, because the Qur'an describes itself as guiding to the straight path, Islam is called the straight path in hadith, and the Prophet's conduct was described by ʿĀʾishah as the Qur'an walking. The hadith *“I have left you upon a clear white path, its night is like its day”* recorded in Sunan Ibn Mājah 43 and graded ḥasan by some, uses the same imagery of a path that remains visible even at night, linking back to the sūrah's sign of night stripped of daylight (36:37). The brevity of 36:4 allows that breadth. By not specifying, it includes. The messenger is upon the straight path in every sense: doctrinally, morally, practically. There is no gap between what he says and where he stands.

**How the Reader Is Meant to Respond**

A path invites walking. The Qur'an does not present the straight path as a theory to be admired but as a place to be occupied. The opening prayer *“Guide us to the Straight Path”* (1:6) is answered here with a living example: here is a man upon it. The sūrah will end by telling its audience that worshiping God alone is the straight path (36:61). Between those two points, the reader is shown why other paths fail: they rely on intercessors who cannot help, they deny resurrection despite daily revivals of earth, they mock messengers despite needing them. The straight path is thus not one option among many; it is the only route that accounts for all the signs. To be upon it is to be consistent with reality. To leave it is to be inconsistent with the sun, moon, grain, and ships that all travel in fixed courses. The shortness of the verse mirrors the simplicity of the choice it presents.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:5

> ˹This is˺ a revelation from the Almighty, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Revelation Attributed to Two Names**

*“A revelation from the Almighty, Most Merciful”* (36:5) identifies the source of what the messenger carries. The Qur'an is not described here as his speech but as tanzīl, a sending down, from al-ʿAzīz al-Raḥīm. The pairing of Almighty and Most Merciful appears repeatedly in the Qur'an, especially in Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ where each story of a rejected messenger ends with *“your Lord is truly the Almighty, Most Merciful”* (26:9). The pairing is deliberate. Almighty conveys power that cannot be resisted, dominance that prevails; Most Merciful conveys gentleness that gives respite and forgives. Revelation comes from both, so it carries both qualities. It cannot be defeated, and it is not sent to destroy without warning. The sūrah's later threats of a single blast (36:29, 36:49) show might, while its promises of forgiveness and honorable reward (36:11) show mercy. The double name at the start tells the reader that both will appear.

**Why Tanzīl Matters More Than Kitāb Here**

The word tanzīl, revelation, emphasizes descent. The Qur'an uses kitāb, book, when it wants to stress completeness and record, and tanzīl when it wants to stress origin and movement from above to below. Here tanzīl fits the argument: a people whose forefathers were not warned (36:6) are now receiving something that has come down to them. The descent is not random; it is from the Almighty, so it cannot be blocked, and from the Most Merciful, so its descent is a gift. Some commentators note that tanzīl can be read as the object of an implied verb — We sent it down as a revelation — or as a predicate — It is a revelation. Both readings lead to the same point: the Qur'an's presence among the Arabs is an act of God, not a product of local culture. The sūrah later says *“We have not taught him poetry, nor is it fitting for him”* (36:69), reinforcing that this descent is not a human composition elevated by eloquence but a distinct kind of speech with a distinct source.

**Almighty and Merciful as a Balance in Daʿwah**

The juxtaposition of might and mercy shapes how warning works. If revelation came only from the Almighty, warning would be pure threat; if only from the Merciful, warning would be easy to dismiss. Together they create the conditions for real choice. The Almighty can enforce the consequences about which the book warns; the Most Merciful gives time to respond before they arrive. The sūrah illustrates that balance in its stories. The town that rejected its messengers was seized with a single mighty blast (36:29) — might. Yet the believing man from the farthest end of the city was told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) and wished his people knew how his Lord had forgiven him (36:27) — mercy. The same balance appears in the natural signs: the sun runs for a fixed term set by the Almighty, All-Knowing (36:38), yet the earth that was dead is revived to produce grain for them to eat (36:33), an act of mercy. Naming the source as Almighty, Most Merciful at the outset prepares the reader to see both aspects in every sign.

**The Connection to the Prophet's Role**

Verse five is grammatically linked to verse three. You are among the messengers upon the straight path, a revelation from the Almighty, Most Merciful. The revelation is what places him upon that path and qualifies him as messenger. This answers a potential objection: how does a man become a messenger? Not by self-appointment but by receiving tanzīl. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“God has sent down to you a reminder, a Messenger reciting to you the clear signs”* (65:10-11), where reminder and messenger are paired as both sent down. Some exegetes therefore read tanzīl in 36:5 as referring not only to the book but to the Prophet himself as one sent down in function, though the majority view is that it refers to the Qur'an. Either way, the source is the same. The Prophet does not speak from his own desire; he speaks because something has been sent to him from beyond himself. That is the Qur'anic definition of prophethood, and it is established within the first five verses of this sūrah.

**A Verse That Frames the Warning to Come**

The next verse explains the purpose of this revelation: *“so that you may warn a people whose forefathers were not warned”* (36:6). Purpose clauses need a source, and verse five supplies it. The warning is not the Prophet's personal anxiety about his people; it is the merciful act of the Almighty who sends a warning before judgment. The Qur'an states this principle as a universal rule: *“We never punish until We have sent a messenger”* (17:15) and *“Your Lord would never destroy towns until He sends a messenger to their mother city”* (28:59). By naming the source as Most Merciful, the sūrah presents warning itself as mercy. To be warned is better than to be seized without warning. The people described as heedless in 36:6 are therefore not without excuse and not without opportunity. They have received a book whose very descent is an act of mercy from a source whose might makes the warning credible. The two names together make the warning both kind and serious.

**How Early Hearers Heard the Two Names**

For the first audience, al-ʿAzīz was a name they knew from tribal power — the mighty one whose protection was sought — and al-Raḥīm was a name they associated with close, familial mercy, the womb, raḥm. Hearing both as source of the same book would have been striking. A mighty king does not usually speak gently to a heedless people; a merciful relative does not usually have the power to enforce consequences. The Qur'an joins them. In recitation, the two names are often given equal weight, with a slight lengthening on Raḥīm that softens the ending after the strength of ʿAzīz. That phonetic pairing mirrors the theological pairing that runs through Yā Sīn: blast and forgiveness, sealing mouths and offering fruits, dead earth and living grain. The verse is short, but it sets the tonal range for everything that follows, from threat to invitation, and tells the listener that both tones come from one source.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:6

> so that you may warn a people whose forefathers were not warned, and so are heedless

**Expanded Commentary**

**Warning a People Without a Recent Warner**

*“So that you may warn a people whose forefathers were not warned, and so are heedless”* (36:6) gives the immediate purpose of the revelation described in the previous verse. The people are the Arabs, and the commentators explain that no messenger had come to them in the generations immediately before Muḥammad with a book in their own language. The Qur'an states this elsewhere: *“We have not given them any books that they study, nor have We sent them a warner before you”* (34:44). That does not mean the Arabs had never received guidance at all — Ismāʿīl lived among them, Ibrāhīm built the House — but the pure monotheism had been obscured and replaced by idol worship, so from the perspective of living memory they were without a warner. The verse thus presents the Prophet's mission as both new and expected: new because no one in their fathers' time had warned them, expected because every people eventually receives a warner. The Qur'an states the rule plainly: *“There is no community but a warner has passed among them”* (35:24).

**What Heedlessness Means in This Sūrah**

Ghaflah, heedlessness, is not ignorance of facts but inattention to what facts mean. The sūrah will illustrate heedlessness with signs that are present yet unseen: dead earth that comes alive, gardens, springs, night and day, sun and moon, ships, cattle. All are visible, but their character as signs is missed. A heedless person sees grain but does not see resurrection in it; sees night but does not see a covering that will one day be removed. The verse therefore diagnoses a moral state, not an intellectual one. The forefathers were not warned, so their descendants inherited a culture where signs were not read as signs. That inheritance does not excuse them, but it explains why warning is needed. The Prophet is sent to interrupt a habit of not noticing. The sūrah later says *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46), which shows heedlessness hardening into avoidance. First they do not see, then they refuse to see.

**Why the Past Matters for Responsibility**

Mentioning forefathers raises a question about fairness. If their fathers were not warned, are the children blamed for their fathers' lack? The Qur'an answers that each soul is responsible for its own response once warning arrives. *“No bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another”* (6:164). The purpose clause so that you may warn makes the present generation the first accountable generation in a new cycle. The principle that God does not destroy towns until a messenger has been sent to their mother city (28:59) means that punishment only comes after warning, not before. By sending a messenger now, God establishes the condition for accountability. The ominous tone noted by some exegetes comes from this: once a warner has come, the excuse of never having been warned is removed. The sūrah's later threats of a single blast that seizes people while they dispute (36:49) are only just after the warning has been given and rejected.

**A Reading That Changes the Meaning Slightly**

Some early readers proposed a different vocalization: not that their forefathers were not warned, but that they were warned of the same thing their descendants are now warned of. In that reading the mā in mā undhira ābāʾuhum is not negative but relative: that of which their forefathers were warned. The difference is small but the implication is large. If the forefathers were warned, then heedlessness is continuity of rejection rather than result of absence. Both readings have been transmitted, and grammarians allow both. Al-Ṭabarī records the majority as the negative — not warned — and notes the alternative without dismissing it. The existence of the alternative shows how seriously early readers took the question of inherited responsibility. Either way, the present warning is decisive: whether the fathers were unwarned or warned and ignored, the children are now being addressed directly and cannot claim surprise.

**How This Verse Connects to the Sūrah's Structure**

Verse six is the hinge between the opening affirmation of prophethood and the description of those who will not believe. The next verse will describe a judicial outcome that follows heedlessness, where the sentence becomes binding on most. The heedlessness of verse six becomes the cause whose consequence is then stated. Warning is offered, but most will not benefit. That sequence mirrors other Meccan sūrahs where a universal invitation is followed by a division of humanity into those who accept and those who do not. The sūrah will later give a parable of a town where messengers came and most rejected, and one man believed and was killed, then entered Paradise (36:20-27). That parable is a narrative version of verses six and seven: warning comes, most are heedless, some respond, and the outcome differs. By stating purpose first — so that you may warn — the sūrah frames all later stories as instances of that purpose being fulfilled, whether accepted or rejected.

**Mercy in the Act of Warning Itself**

To warn is itself an act of mercy, and the previous verse named the source as Most Merciful. The Arabs could have been left in heedlessness until the blast seized them without notice, but they were not. They were given a messenger from among themselves, speaking their language, reciting a book described as rich in wisdom. The Qur'an says *“We have made it easy in your language so that they may be mindful”* (44:58). Ease of language is part of mercy. Heedlessness is cured not by harshness but by clarity, and the sūrah's clarity is remarkable: short sentences, vivid images, repeated refrains. Even the harsh images — shackles, barriers, sealed mouths — are warnings given before the events they describe, so that they need never happen to the listener. A heedless people is not insulted here; it is addressed. The address itself is the mercy that the heedlessness had made necessary.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:7

> The decree ˹of torment˺ has already been justified against most of them, for they will never believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Judicial Sentence After Warning**

This verse moves from warning to judgment. After stating that a people whose forefathers were not warned are now being warned, the text says the word, the decree, has become binding against most of them. The Arabic al-qawl, the word, here is understood by most exegetes as the word of punishment, the divine sentence that those who persistently reject guidance will be inhabitants of the Fire. The Qur'an says elsewhere *“Likewise the Word of your Lord has come due against those who disbelieve, that they are inhabitants of the Fire”* (40:6) and *“But the Word from Me will come true: I will surely fill Hell with jinn and men all together”* (32:13). The sense is not that God arbitrarily condemns people before they act, but that His prior threat — if you turn away, this will be your end — has been confirmed by their actual turning away. The word was spoken as warning; their response made it come due as judgment. Most will not believe, not because belief was made impossible, but because they have chosen a pattern that now defines them.

**Why “Most” and Not “All”**

The verse says most of them, aktharuhum, not all. That single word keeps the door open. Even in a context of widespread rejection, the Qur'an preserves the possibility of faith. The sūrah will illustrate that possibility immediately: a town rejects its messengers, yet a man comes from the farthest end of the city and believes (36:20). One believer among a town shows that most does not mean all. The same precision appears elsewhere: *“Most of them do not believe”* (2:100) often follows a description of a majority, leaving room for a minority. Theologically, this matters because it avoids determinism. The decree is justified against those who fulfill its condition — persistent disbelief — but the condition is not forced. The verse also consoles the Prophet. If most do not believe, his task is not failure; it is the same ratio previous messengers faced. The Qur'an tells him *“If you obey most of those on earth, they will lead you away from God's path”* (6:116), indicating that majority is not a measure of truth.

**The Word as Both Threat and Proof**

Qawl in Qur'anic usage can mean speech, decree, or promise. Here it is the threat of punishment that has become true. Some commentators also read it as the Qur'an itself: the Qur'an is a proof either for you or against you, as a well-known hadith states. On that reading, the book that came as mercy becomes a witness against those who ignore it. The idea appears in the sūrah's later verse *“On this Day We will seal their mouths, their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify”* (36:65). First the word testifies, then the limbs testify. The progression is from external evidence to internal evidence. The word that came down as *“a revelation from the Almighty, Most Merciful”* (36:5) now stands as evidence that mercy was offered and refused. This is why the verse follows the mention of heedlessness (36:6). Heedlessness is not a neutral state; it allows a just decree to become binding because the warning that could have prevented it was present and ignored.

**How the Decree Becomes Justified**

The verb ḥaqqa means to become true, binding, incumbent. It is not simply said; it becomes real through fulfillment. The Qur'an uses the same verb for promises and threats that materialize. The threat *“I shall fill Hell”* (32:13) becomes ḥaqq when people choose the path that leads there. Exegetes debate whether this becoming-true refers to this life or the next, and the answer is both. In this life, the decree manifests as sealing, as barriers, as inability to benefit from signs. In the next, it manifests as actual punishment. The sūrah describes both stages: shackles and barriers (36:8-9) in the present, and burning in Hell (36:64) in the future. The present state is a foretaste that confirms the future state is coming. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the word becomes due because they do not believe, not the reverse. Their disbelief is the cause, the decree becoming due is the consequence. This preserves moral responsibility while acknowledging divine foreknowledge.

**A Verse That Explains the Prophet's Experience**

For the Prophet, this verse explained why his clearest arguments met denial. The Meccans were not lacking intelligence; they were fulfilling a pattern the Qur'an describes elsewhere as hearts covered and ears made heavy. The sūrah will later say *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46). That turning away is the human side; the decree becoming justified is the divine side. Both are true at once. The Qur'an holds divine decree and human choice together without collapsing one into the other. The verse does not say God prevented them from believing; it says the decree has been justified against them because they will not believe. The future tense *“they will never believe”* (36:7) is a statement of knowledge, not compulsion. God knows who will persist, and His word reflects that knowledge. For the believer reading today, the verse is a warning not to become part of the most by repeatedly ignoring reminders until not believing becomes a settled trait.

**The Relation to Mercy Despite Judgment**

Even as it announces judgment, the verse is placed within a sūrah that repeatedly offers mercy. The same chapter that says the decree is justified against most also says *“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and are in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him. So give them good news of forgiveness and an honourable reward”* (36:11). The two verses are a pair: one describes those who will not benefit, the other those who will. The Qur'an's method is to present both outcomes side by side so the listener can choose. The decree being justified is not the final word of the sūrah; resurrection, forgiveness, gardens, and peace from the Merciful Lord (36:58) are also part of its final word. The verse is therefore not meant to produce despair but clarity. Most will not believe, but you may still be among those who do, and the invitation to be among them is given immediately after.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:8

> ˹It is as if˺ We have put shackles around their necks up to their chins, so their heads are forced up

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Image of Restraint That Becomes Character**

This verse gives a physical image for a spiritual condition. *“We have put shackles around their necks up to their chins, so their heads are forced up”* (36:8) describes aghlāl, iron collars with cuffs that bind the hands to the neck, lifting the chin and preventing the head from bowing. The picture is of a person who cannot lower his head, cannot look down, cannot extend his hands to do good. The Qur'an uses the same term in *“We have not shackled your hand to your neck”* (17:29) as a warning against miserliness, and in *“they will be dragged, with shackles and chains around their necks”* (40:71) for the Hereafter. Here the image is placed in the present, as the state of those against whom the decree has become justified (36:7). They are not yet in the Fire, but they already live as if shackled, because their rejection has become a habit that restrains them. The shackles are not imposed arbitrarily; they are the form their own choices have taken.

**Why the Chin Is Forced Up**

The detail that heads are forced up, muqmaḥūn, is rare and evocative. Lexicographers explain it as a camel whose head is lifted because its nose-rein is pulled tight, or a man whose chin is pushed up by a collar so he cannot see the ground in front of him. Both convey inability to look where one should. A person whose head is forced up cannot see the path at his feet, cannot bow in prayer, cannot look with humility. Some exegetes read it as arrogance: they walk with chins raised, refusing to lower themselves to truth. Others read it as helplessness: they would like to lower their heads but cannot because the collar prevents it. Both meanings converge. Arrogance chosen repeatedly becomes a collar that then prevents humility. The sūrah later says *“Had We willed, We could have transfigured them on the spot, so they could neither progress forward nor turn back”* (36:67), another image of immobilization. The shackles of 36:8 are the moral equivalent of that physical freezing.

**Shackles in This Life and the Next**

Commentators differ on whether the shackles are in this world or the next, and the strongest view is that they are both, with this world as metaphor and the next as literal. In this life, the shackles are the love of status, wealth, and ancestral custom that bind hands from giving and necks from bowing. The disbelievers who said *“Why should we feed those whom Allah could have fed if He wanted to?”* (36:47) show hands shackled from charity. In the Hereafter, the metaphor becomes iron. The Qur'an often does this: a moral state in this life becomes a physical state in the next. The barrier before and behind them in the next verse (36:9) follows the same pattern. The progression from shackles around necks to barriers around sight shows encirclement. First the hands and neck are bound, then the eyes are veiled. The person is enclosed from acting and from seeing, which is the Qur'anic definition of a sealed heart.

**The Relation to Miserliness and Generosity**

The link to 17:29, *“Do not shackle your hand to your neck”* (17:29), is not incidental. That verse warns against withholding charity. The sūrah later condemns those who refuse to donate from what Allah has provided (36:47). Shackled hands cannot give. The image therefore connects theology to economics. A community that refuses to feed the poor because it rationalizes that God could feed them if He wanted (36:47) is a community whose hands are bound to their necks by their own reasoning. The Qur'an presents generosity as a sign of a heart that is not shackled. The believer who follows the Reminder and is in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him (36:11) is promised forgiveness and an honorable reward, and part of that awe is shown in giving. The shackles are thus both cause and symbol of a refusal to be open-handed, and open-handedness is presented as liberation.

**How This Verse Was Understood in Early Reports**

Some reports link verses eight and nine to a specific incident in Mecca where a leader of Quraysh intended to harm the Prophet while he prayed, and his hand became stuck to his neck, or he was blinded to the Prophet's presence. Al-Ṭabarī and others mention such reports, but they also stress that the verses have a general meaning beyond any single occasion. The general meaning is that those who set out to harm truth find themselves restrained, not by an external force alone but by the very act of opposition. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“We will turn their hearts and eyes away, as they did not believe the first time”* (6:110). The first refusal makes the second harder. The shackles image captures that hardening. It is not that God prevents belief arbitrarily; it is that repeated refusal creates a collar that the refuser then wears. The image is harsh, but it is also just, because it shows consequence as continuation rather than as unrelated punishment.

**A Condition That Is Not Final for Everyone**

Although the verse describes those against whom the decree has been justified, the sūrah does not present shackles as inevitable for all. The same chapter that describes necks bound up to chins also describes faces that will be busy enjoying themselves in Paradise (36:55), reclining on couches (36:56), greeted with peace from the Merciful Lord (36:58). The contrast is intentional. One group has heads forced up and cannot bow; the other has been given shade and ease and bows willingly in gratitude. The reader is placed between those two images and asked which posture he prefers. The shackles are not described to entertain with vivid language but to warn that a moral choice has a physical outcome, and that outcome can be avoided while there is time. The very next verse adds barriers before and behind, completing the enclosure, but even then the Qur'an continues to call, to warn, and to promise forgiveness for those who will listen.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:9

> and have placed a barrier before them and a barrier behind them and covered them ˹all˺ up, so they fail to see ˹the truth˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Encircled From Every Side**

Verse eight described shackles around necks that force heads up; this verse adds barriers in front and behind and a covering over all. *“We have placed a barrier before them and a barrier behind them and covered them up, so they fail to see”* (36:9) completes the image of total enclosure. A person whose hands are bound to his neck and whose sight is blocked front and back cannot move purposefully. The barrier, sadd, is a dam or wall that blocks passage. Placing one before and one behind means neither advance nor retreat is possible. The covering, aghshaynāhum, is a veil that prevents seeing at all. The result is *“so they fail to see the truth”* (36:9). The truth is present — the sūrah will list signs in earth, night, sun, moon — but the veiled eye does not register them as signs. The verse thus describes not absence of evidence but incapacity to read evidence, and that incapacity follows the moral state described before it.

**Why Barriers Before and Behind**

Exegetes offer several readings of what the two barriers represent. Some say the barrier before them is the delusion of this world that distracts, and the barrier behind is denial of the Hereafter that makes them think there is no consequence. Others say the barrier before is what is coming — punishment, resurrection — that they refuse to anticipate, and the barrier behind is what has passed — destroyed nations, previous warnings — that they refuse to remember. Both readings point to the same condition: a person cut off from past and future, trapped in a present that feels permanent. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Have they not considered what is before them and what is behind them of heaven and earth? If We willed, We could cause the earth to swallow them”* (34:9). The sūrah's later warning *“Beware of what is ahead of you and what is behind you so you may be shown mercy”* (36:45) echoes the same pairing. To be told to beware of what is before and behind implies that barriers have been placed because that warning was ignored.

**Blindness That Follows Rejection**

The Qur'an distinguishes between blindness of the eye and blindness of the heart. *“It is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts in the chests that go blind”* (22:46). The covering here is of that second kind. The disbelievers can see the sun, moon, grain, and ships, but they do not see them as signs. The verse says they fail to see the truth, not that they fail to see at all. The addition *“the truth”* in the translation clarifies that what is missed is not sensory data but its meaning. This is why the sūrah repeatedly says *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46). First they fail to see, then they turn away, which deepens the failure. The covering is both punishment and consequence: punishment because it is attributed to God — We placed, We covered — and consequence because it follows their choice to ignore earlier signs. The divine act and human act are not in competition; the divine act is the fixing of the human act into a state.

**The Incident Reports and the General Meaning**

Some early reports attach this verse to a specific attempt to harm the Prophet. One narration says Abū Jahl took a rock to strike the Prophet while he prayed, and his hand froze to his neck, or he heard the Prophet but could not see him, or a camel appeared before him. Similar stories are reported for other leaders. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī mention these occasions but treat them as examples of the verse's meaning, not its limit. The general meaning is that anyone who approaches truth with hostility finds a barrier. The Qur'an says *“We will turn their hearts and eyes away, as they refused to believe at first”* (6:110). The first refusal creates a barrier that makes the second encounter harder. The physical barrier that prevented a rock from reaching the Prophet is a visible instance of the invisible barrier that prevents guidance from reaching a hostile heart. Both are real, but the second is more consequential because it persists beyond a single incident.

**How Barriers Relate to Guidance**

If barriers are placed, can guidance ever reach? The Qur'an answers that barriers are not permanent for those who seek removal. The same sūrah that describes barriers also says *“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and are in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him”* (36:11). Those who follow the Reminder have not had barriers removed by accident; they have turned toward the Reminder, and turning removes barriers. The Prophet is told *“So do not let their words grieve you. Indeed, We fully know what they conceal and what they reveal”* (36:76), which implies that his duty is to deliver, not to remove barriers by force. Removal is God's act in response to a human turning. The image of barriers before and behind therefore does not teach fatalism; it teaches that persistence in rejection has a cumulative effect that eventually feels like enclosure, and that enclosure is a warning to turn before it becomes complete.

**A Verse That Mirrors the Human Condition**

Everyone experiences moments of not seeing what is before and behind. A person absorbed in a dispute, as described later — *“a single Blast, which will seize them while they are entrenched in worldly disputes”* (36:49) — does not see the larger reality. The verse universalizes that experience. Shackles and barriers are not exotic punishments for ancient deniers; they are the form that heedlessness takes when it hardens. The sūrah's signs — dead earth revived, night stripped of day, sun on a fixed course, moon in phases, ships carried, cattle subjected — are all attempts to remove those barriers by pointing to what is before and behind. Grain comes from earth that was dead, which is behind, and points to resurrection which is before. Night is stripped to show day, which is before. The barriers of verse nine are thus answered by the signs that follow, which are invitations to look again. The verse is harsh, but its placement before a long series of signs shows its purpose is to make the reader ask whether he too is veiled, and to seek the removal of the veil while there is time.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:10

> It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**Equality of Warning and Non-Warning**

*“It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe”* (36:10) is a verse that resembles *“It is the same for them whether you warn them or not, they will not believe”* (2:6), which opens Sūrah al-Baqarah's description of those who disbelieve. The repetition across sūrahs shows a consistent Qur'anic pattern: after warning has been given and rejected, persistence in rejection reaches a point where further warning does not change the outcome. The verse is not a statement about the Prophet's ability but about the audience's settled disposition. The same sun that softens wax hardens clay; the same reminder that awakens one heart confirms another's turning away. The equality described — same whether warned or not — is not because warning lacks power, but because the hearers have placed barriers before and behind themselves (36:9) and wear shackles that force heads up (36:8). Warning cannot benefit a person who has decided not to be warned, not because the warning is defective but because benefit requires receptivity.

**Why This Verse Does Not Cancel the Duty to Warn**

A reader might think that if warning makes no difference, the Prophet should stop warning. The Qur'an explicitly rejects that conclusion. The Prophet is told *“Your duty is only to deliver the message clearly”* (36:17) through the town messengers, and elsewhere *“So remind, for you are only a reminder, you are not a controller over them”* (88:21-22). The duty to warn is independent of its immediate success. The verse describes a fact about this group — they will not believe — but it does not permit the messenger to abandon them, because among the most there may still be some who will believe, and because warning itself establishes proof. The Qur'an says *“We never punish until We have sent a messenger”* (17:15). Warning must be given so that on the Day when mouths are sealed and hands speak (36:65), no one can claim they were not told. The equality of warning and non-warning for this group is therefore a statement of their state, not a cancellation of the Prophet's mission.

**The Difference Between Knowledge and Compulsion**

The verse says they will never believe, which is a statement of divine knowledge about their future choice. It does not say they are forced not to believe. The Qur'an maintains both divine foreknowledge and human responsibility. *“God knows what is in your hearts”* (33:51) and *“God does not wrong people in the least, but people wrong themselves”* (10:44). The knowledge that they will not believe follows their persistent refusal; it does not cause it. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the meaning is that they have become such that warning does not benefit them because they have rejected the signs repeatedly. The sūrah later says *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46). Turning away is an act; being described as those who will never believe is the description of those who have made turning away a habit. The verse is thus a diagnosis, not a sentence that prevents cure. The cure remains available, but the patient refuses it.

**How This Verse Relates to the Two Audiences**

The sūrah immediately divides its audience in the next verse, which describes those who actually benefit from warning — people who follow the Reminder and hold awe for the unseen Most Compassionate. Verse ten and eleven are a pair. One describes those for whom warning is the same as no warning; the other describes those for whom warning is effective. The pairing is a common Qur'anic technique: mention of those who do not benefit is followed by mention of those who do, so the listener is invited to place himself in the second group. The Prophet is consoled by this division. He is not failing; he is addressing two kinds of hearts, and his words are reaching the kind that is alive. The sūrah later says *“to warn whoever is truly alive”* (36:70), implying that some hearts are dead and warning cannot revive them unless God revives them as He revives dead earth (36:33). The equality of warning and non-warning is therefore not a universal statement about all people, but about a specific group whose hearts have become dead through heedlessness.

**A Warning Against Becoming Like Them**

For the contemporary reader, the verse functions as a warning against becoming someone for whom warning makes no difference. How does a person become like that? The sūrah shows the steps: heedlessness (36:6), then justification of the decree through persistent disbelief (36:7), then shackles (36:8), then barriers (36:9), then equality of warning and non-warning (36:10). Each step is a deepening. The progression is not inevitable; it is chosen at each stage. The Qur'an says *“God does not change the condition of a people until they change what is in themselves”* (13:11). The verse is placed early to alert the reader that spiritual states have momentum. If reminders are consistently ignored, a point arrives where reminders no longer register. That point is described here, but it is described as a warning while there is still time to avoid it. The very fact that the reader is reading this verse means he is not yet among those for whom warning and non-warning are the same, because he is still being warned and can still respond.

**The Pastoral Purpose for the Messenger**

Finally, the verse has a pastoral purpose for the Prophet. He grieved over his people's rejection, and the Qur'an repeatedly tells him not to let their words grieve him (36:76). Knowing that some will never believe, despite warning, protects the messenger from self-blame. His success is not measured by universal acceptance but by clear delivery. The town messengers said *“Our Lord knows that we have truly been sent to you”* (36:16) and *“Our duty is only to deliver the message clearly”* (36:17). Their town mostly rejected, yet their duty was fulfilled. The same is true for Muḥammad. The verse that says warning makes no difference for some is therefore not a statement of failure but of realism. It allows the Prophet to continue warning those who will benefit — those who heed the Reminder and hold reverent fear of the unseen — and to leave the result of the others to God, who knows what they conceal and what they reveal (36:76).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:11

> You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and are in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him. So give them good news of forgiveness and an honourable reward

**Expanded Commentary**

**Who Actually Benefits From Warning**

After describing those for whom warning makes no difference (36:10), this verse identifies those for whom warning does work. *“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and are in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him”* (36:11) defines effective warning by two traits. First, following the Reminder, al-dhikr, which is a name for the Qur'an itself, as in *“We have sent down the Reminder and We will preserve it”* (15:9). To follow the Reminder is to let the Qur'an shape action, not merely to hear it. Second, being in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him, which is خشية بالغيب, a reverent fear that persists even when God is unseen. The same phrase appears in *“who feared the Most Compassionate without seeing Him and came with a penitent heart”* (50:33). The two traits together describe a person whose outward behavior follows revelation and whose inward state is aware of God even in private. Warning benefits such a person because he is already oriented toward guidance; the reminder reminds him of what he already cares about.

**The Reminder as a Name for the Qur'an**

Calling the Qur'an the Reminder, al-dhikr, is significant. Dhikr means remembrance, mention, reminder. The Qur'an presents itself not as new information about a previously unknown deity but as a reminder of what the human already knows at a deeper level — that he was created, that he will return, that signs surround him. The sūrah's signs — dead earth revived, night stripped of day, sun and moon in orbits — are reminders because they point to what was forgotten through heedlessness (36:6). To follow the Reminder is therefore to remember. The Prophet is told he can only warn those who follow it, which implies that following is a choice that precedes benefit. The Qur'an says *“This is only a Reminder and a clear Qur'an, to warn whoever is truly alive”* (36:69-70). The truly alive are those who follow the Reminder; the dead are those for whom warning is the same as no warning (36:10). The name al-dhikr thus ties warning to life.

**Awe Without Seeing**

The second trait, awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him, describes faith in the unseen, which is the foundation of Qur'anic piety. *“This Book, there is no doubt in it, guidance for those who are mindful of God, who believe in the unseen”* (2:2-3). To be in awe of al-Raḥmān without seeing Him is to let the name Most Compassionate shape conduct even when no human is watching. The choice of al-Raḥmān here, rather than al-ʿAzīz or al-Jabbār, is deliberate. Awe of the Almighty might be fear of power; awe of the Most Compassionate is reverence that includes love and gratitude. The person described fears disappointing mercy, not just power. That kind of fear produces the generosity the sūrah later calls for, as opposed to those who refuse to donate (36:47). It also produces the humility that the shackled necks of 36:8 lack. A head forced up cannot bow; a heart in awe without seeing bows willingly.

**Good News of Forgiveness and Honourable Reward**

The command *“So give them good news of forgiveness and an honourable reward”* (36:11) follows the description of those who benefit. Forgiveness, maghfirah, comes first, because even the person who follows the Reminder and fears the unseen still needs forgiveness. No one is without fault. The honourable reward, ajr karīm, is Paradise, described later as busy enjoyment, shade, couches, fruits, and peace (36:55-58). The adjective honourable, karīm, indicates a reward that is generous, dignified, and given by a generous host. The same phrase appears in *“For them is forgiveness and an honourable reward”* (67:12) for those who fear their Lord unseen. The pairing of forgiveness and honourable reward shows the balance of mercy: sins are covered, then goodness is honoured. The Prophet is told to give good news, bashshir, which is the opposite of warning, indhār. Warning and good news are the two modes of prophetic speech, and they are directed to different audiences. Warning is for all, but good news is specifically for those who follow and fear.

**Why Only Those Who Follow Can Be Warned**

The phrase you can only warn, innamā tundhiru, limits the effective scope of warning, not the duty to warn. The Prophet must warn all, but only those who follow the Reminder will actually be warned in the sense of benefiting. This is similar to *“You cannot make the dead hear, nor can you make the deaf hear the call when they turn away”* (27:80). The limitation is in the hearer, not the speaker. The sūrah illustrates this with the town story: messengers came, most rejected, but one man from the farthest end of the city came rushing and believed (36:20). He was among those who follow the Reminder and fear unseen, even though the Reminder had just reached him. His quick response shows that following is not about duration but orientation. Some hear once and follow; others hear repeatedly and do not. The verse therefore encourages the Prophet to focus his hope on those who show the two traits, without abandoning the general call.

**A Verse That Defines the Successful Listener**

For the reader today, this verse defines what successful listening looks like. It is not about intelligence or lineage but about two practical commitments: letting the Qur'an guide action, and being mindful of the Most Compassionate when alone. The first is public, the second private. Together they form integrity. The reward promised — forgiveness and an honourable reward — is not described as earned but as given, which preserves grace. The next verse will speak of resurrection and of a record that preserves what people send ahead and what traces they leave, indicating that following the Reminder leaves marks that are preserved. The good news of forgiveness and honourable reward is thus not abstract; it is tied to a record that preserves what was sent forth and what was left behind. The verse invites the reader to become someone for whom warning is not the same as no warning, by choosing to follow and to be in awe without seeing.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:12

> It is certainly We Who resurrect the dead, and write what they send forth and what they leave behind. Everything is listed by Us in a perfect Record

**Expanded Commentary**

**Resurrection and Record Together**

This verse pairs two divine acts that the sūrah's audience might have thought separate: giving life to the dead and keeping a record. *“It is certainly We who resurrect the dead, and write what they send forth and what they leave behind”* (36:12) says both are God's work, and both are certain. The first clause answers the central doubt the sūrah will address — *“Who will give life to decayed bones?”* (36:78) — by asserting resurrection as fact. The second clause answers a hidden doubt — that deeds are forgotten — by asserting that what is sent forth and what is left behind is written. The pairing is logical: if dead are to be raised, there must be a record by which they are judged; if a record is kept, there must be a raising for the record to matter. The verse that says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged in the least, nor will you be rewarded except for what you used to do”* (36:54) depends on both. Resurrection without record would be purposeless; record without resurrection would be inert.

**What Is Sent Forth and What Is Left Behind**

Mā qaddamū wa āthārahum, what they send forth and their traces, has been explained in several ways. What they send forth is what they do in life that goes ahead to the next world — prayers, charity, good or evil deeds. What they leave behind, āthār, literally footprints or traces, is what remains after them. One occasion cited is that the tribe of Banū Salimah lived far from the Prophet's mosque and wanted to move closer, and the Prophet told them their footprints walking to prayer are recorded, so they should stay. The hadith *“When the son of Adam dies, his deeds end except for three: ongoing charity, beneficial knowledge, or a righteous child who prays for him”* — recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1631 — illustrates what is left behind. A well dug, a book taught, a child raised well, continues to produce traces after death. Conversely, a harmful innovation or a bad example also leaves traces. The verse therefore expands accountability beyond the moment of action to its ongoing effects. A person is responsible not only for what he did but for what his deeds set in motion.

**The Perfect Record, Imām Mubīn**

*“Everything is listed by Us in a perfect Record”* (36:12) uses imām mubīn, a clear registry or leading record. Imām means leader, something followed, and mubīn means clear. Some exegetes take it as the Preserved Tablet, al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ, in which everything is inscribed from beginning to end, as in *“No calamity strikes on earth or in yourselves but it is in a Book before We bring it into being”* (57:22). Others take it as the record of deeds that will be given to each person, as in *“Every person's deeds We have fastened to his neck, and on the Day of Resurrection We will bring out for him a book which he will find wide open”* (17:13). Both are true at different levels: a universal record that encompasses all, and individual records that are derived from it. The sūrah later says *“This is the Hell you were warned of. Burn in it today for your disbelief”* (36:63-64) and *“On this Day We will seal their mouths, their hands will speak”* (36:65), showing that the record is not only written but also testified by limbs. The perfect record is thus both preserved and made manifest.

**Why Resurrection Is Mentioned Here**

Placing resurrection right after the division between those who do not benefit from warning and those who do (36:10-11) is strategic. Those who do not benefit often deny resurrection; those who benefit prepare for it. The verse reminds both that resurrection is coming regardless of belief. The sūrah will later argue for resurrection from natural signs: dead earth revived with grain (36:33), fire from green trees (36:80), creation of heavens and earth (36:81). Here it is simply asserted: We resurrect the dead. The assertion comes with the pronoun of majesty, We, which appears throughout this sūrah for acts only God can do — We placed barriers, We resurrect, We carried ancestors in the Ark. The use of We emphasizes that resurrection is not a natural process that happens by itself but a divine act. The same We that writes what is sent forth is the We that will raise. The two acts share an agent, so trust in one supports trust in the other.

**Traces as an Encouragement and a Warning**

The idea that traces are written is both encouragement and warning. Encouragement, because a small act that continues — teaching a verse, planting a tree, building a place of prayer — keeps being recorded after the actor is gone. Warning, because a harmful trace also continues. The town story that follows shows both: the messengers' message left a trace in the believing man who came rushing (36:20), and the town's rejection left a trace in its destruction by a single blast (36:29). The reader is invited to think about what traces he is leaving now. The sūrah later says *“Had We willed, We could have transfigured them on the spot”* (36:67), which would cut off traces immediately, but God allows time — *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44) — so that traces can accumulate and repentance remains possible. The record is perfect, but the time to add to it is limited.

**A Verse That Connects to the Sūrah's End**

The final verse of the sūrah says *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is the authority over all things, and to Whom you will all be returned”* (36:83). Return to Him is the same as resurrection of the dead, and authority over all things includes authority to keep a perfect record. Verse twelve thus anticipates the ending. It also anticipates the scene of judgment where *“no soul will be wronged in the least”* (36:54) because everything is listed. The perfect record guarantees justice; resurrection guarantees that justice will be experienced. Together they answer the heedlessness diagnosed earlier. A heedless person lives as if deeds vanish and death is the end. This verse says deeds are written and death is not the end. The Qur'an says *“Whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it”* (99:7-8). That seeing requires both a record that preserves atoms and a resurrection that allows seeing. This verse asserts both in one line.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:13

> Give them an example ˹O Prophet˺ of the residents of a town, when the messengers came to them

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Tell a Story**

This verse shifts from doctrine to narrative. *“Give them an example of the residents of a town, when the messengers came to them”* (36:13) is addressed to the Prophet, commanding him to present a mathal, a parable or example, drawn from a town. The Arabic mathal is not fiction; it is a case that illuminates a principle. The principle here is how communities respond to messengers, and the town is presented as a mirror for Mecca. The Quraysh who rejected Muḥammad are told to look at another town that did the same. The story that follows — two messengers, then a third, then a believing man, then a single blast — condenses the prophetic experience into a short drama. By calling it an example, the verse signals that the details of name and place matter less than the pattern. Whether the town is Antioch, as many commentators say, or another city, the pattern of rejection, reinforcement, and consequence is what the audience is meant to retain.

**Why a Town, Not a Nation**

The word qaryah, town, suggests a settled, identifiable community with markets, walls, and a sense of collective identity. Towns in the Qur'an often represent self-sufficiency that breeds arrogance. *“We never sent a warner to a town but its affluent said, We disbelieve in what you have been sent with”* (34:34). A town is small enough that messengers can walk its streets, yet large enough that its rejection is communal. The sūrah's earlier description of barriers before and behind (36:9) fits a town whose gates are closed to truth. The town in this example receives messengers, not a book directly, which matches the Meccan situation where a messenger walks among them. The verse also echoes the principle *“We never destroy towns until We send a messenger to their mother city”* (28:59). A town is destroyed only after warning, so telling its story is itself an act of mercy to the next town — Mecca — which is still being warned.

**Messengers as a Test**

*“When the messengers came to them”* (36:13) presents arrival as a test. The messengers come; the town must respond. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Every community has a messenger, and when their messenger comes, judgment is passed between them with justice”* (10:47). The coming of messengers initiates accountability. The sūrah does not say the town was already doomed; it says messengers came, implying opportunity. The later verse *“Oh pity, such beings! No messenger ever came to them without being mocked”* (36:30) shows that mocking is the usual response, not the exception. By framing the story as an example for the Prophet to give, the verse also defines his role: he is to give examples, not just rules. Stories carry what arguments alone cannot — the emotional weight of a man rushing from the farthest end of the city (36:20) and a community extinguished at once (36:29). The example is therefore pedagogical, not merely historical.

**The Identity of the Town and Its Messengers**

Most classical commentators identify the town as Antioch, Anṭākiyah, and the messengers as apostles sent by ʿĪsā, Jesus, after his ascension. In that reading, God says We sent them because Jesus was commanded by God to send them. Others say the messengers are prophets in their own right, not disciples, because the Qur'an calls Muḥammad a mursal in 36:3 and calls other prophets mursalūn in many places. The identification with Antioch cannot be verified from the Qur'an itself, and Ibn Kathīr notes the uncertainty. What matters for the sūrah's argument is not the town's name but its behavior, which matches Mecca's: denial of human messengers, accusation of lying, threat of stoning. The story's anonymity makes it universal. Any town that receives messengers and reacts this way can see itself in it. The Prophet is told to give them an example, and the example is left open enough to fit his audience.

**How Example Functions in Qur'anic Persuasion**

The Qur'an uses mathal frequently to move from abstract to concrete. *“God presents an example: a servant owned and unable to do a thing, and one We have provided with good provision”* (16:75) is one such. Examples work because they bypass defensiveness. A listener who would argue against a direct accusation — you are rejecting your messenger — may listen to a story about another town. Once he judges that town's rejection as unjust, he has judged his own. This is the rhetorical strategy of Nathan with David: tell a story about another man's lamb, then say you are the man.
