Yā Sīn does the same. It tells of a town that said *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15), which is exactly what the Quraysh said about Muḥammad. The listener who condemns the town's excuse condemns his own. The command to give an example is thus a command to use indirect persuasion, which is often more effective than direct confrontation.

**An Example That Prepares for the Believer**

The town story is not only about rejection; it contains a model of belief. A man comes from the farthest end of the city, rushing, advising his people to follow the messengers (36:20). He is the counterpoint to the town. By introducing the story as an example of residents of a town when messengers came, the verse sets up both the majority and the minority response. The majority rejects; one man believes. That division mirrors the earlier division between those for whom warning is the same as no warning (36:10) and those who follow the Reminder (36:11). The example therefore does not end in despair. It shows that even in a town that as a whole rejects, an individual can break away, follow the messengers, and be told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26). The command to give an example is thus also a command to give hope: the pattern of rejection is common, but the possibility of individual faith remains open, even when it costs the believer his life.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:14

> We sent them two messengers, but they rejected both. So We reinforced ˹the two˺ with a third, and they declared, “We have indeed been sent to you ˹as messengers˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Messengers, Then a Third**

This verse details the town's test. *“We sent them two messengers, but they rejected both. So We reinforced the two with a third, and they declared, We have indeed been sent to you as messengers”* (36:14) shows escalation on both sides. The town receives two, rejects both, and instead of being left to its rejection, it receives a third as reinforcement. The Arabic ʿazaznā, We reinforced, implies strengthening with might and numbers. The sequence demonstrates divine patience. One messenger could have been enough to establish proof; two provide corroboration; three make denial harder to justify. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“We sent messengers in succession”* (23:44) and that communities who denied them were seized. Here succession is within a single town. The declaration *“We have indeed been sent to you as messengers”* (36:14) is simple and direct, without elaborate argument. Messengers do not begin with philosophy; they begin with identity: we have been sent to you. The content of the message is implied in the sending.

**Why Two, Then Three**

The number matters for legal and psychological reasons. In Islamic law, testimony of two is stronger than one; two witnesses establish a claim. Sending two messengers provides that corroboration. When both are rejected, a third is added, not to provide new content but to reinforce the same content. The pattern shows that rejection was not due to lack of clarity or number but to unwillingness. The town's later excuse — *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15) — would have applied to one as well as three; increasing the number does not change the excuse, which reveals that the excuse is pretext. The sūrah's own context supports this: Muḥammad is one messenger among many (36:3), yet his single presence is enough to establish proof. If one is enough, three is abundance. The town's persistence in rejection despite abundance makes its eventual seizure by a single blast (36:29) proportionate. Abundance of warning precedes swiftness of judgment.

**We Sent Them: Divine Attribution**

The verse attributes sending to God: We sent them two messengers. Even if the messengers are understood as apostles of Jesus, their sending is ultimately God's act, because Jesus himself was sent by God. This attribution matters because it elevates the town's rejection from a dispute with men to a dispute with God. To reject those God sent is to reject God. The Qur'an says *“Whoever obeys the messenger has obeyed God”* (4:80). The town thinks it is rejecting humans like itself, but the verse frames it as rejecting divine sending. The same framing applies to Mecca. The Quraysh said Muḥammad was a man like them, but the Qur'an says *“We have sent you as a messenger”* (4:79). The pronoun We, used for divine acts throughout Yā Sīn — We placed barriers, We resurrect, We carried ancestors — places this sending among acts only God does. The town's story is thus not about interpersonal disagreement but about response to divine initiative.

**The Declaration as Clear Proclamation**

The messengers' statement *“We have indeed been sent to you as messengers”* (36:14) is a model of clear proclamation, balāgh mubīn, which they later define as their only duty (36:17). Clear proclamation does not mean elaborate rhetoric; it means unambiguous claim. We have been sent to you. The clarity is in identity and address: we are sent, and you are the recipients. No ambiguity, no hidden agenda. The sūrah emphasizes that true messengers ask no reward (36:21) and are rightly guided, so their claim is supported by character. The town's response will be to call them liars (36:15) and threaten stoning (36:18), which are attacks on character, not refutations of message. The messengers' simple declaration exposes that. When a clear claim is met with insult and threat rather than argument, the inadequacy lies with the respondents, not the claimants. The verse thus teaches how to recognize prophetic speech: it is clear, direct, and about being sent.

**Reinforcement as Mercy and Proof**

Sending a third messenger after two have been rejected can be read as mercy — another chance — and as proof — additional witness. Both are true. Mercy, because the town is given more time and more voices before judgment; proof, because the town can no longer say it did not understand or did not have enough evidence. The Qur'an says *“We never punish until We have sent a messenger”* (17:15), and here three are sent. The reinforcement also shows that divine guidance does not withdraw after first rejection. The sūrah later says *“Had We willed, We could have blinded their eyes”* (36:66) and *“transfigured them on the spot”* (36:67), but God does not. Instead, He reinforces messengers. That patience is part of the name Most Merciful mentioned at the sūrah's start (36:5). Even when reinforcement is rejected, the fact that it was given will be part of the record that is kept in a perfect registry (36:12). The town's traces include both its rejection and the abundance of warning it received.

**A Pattern That Repeats in Every Generation**

The pattern of two, then three, is not limited to this town. The Qur'an describes communities that received successive messengers and denied them: *“Every time a messenger came to a community, they called him a liar”* (23:44). The story is therefore paradigmatic. Any community that receives guidance, rejects it, receives more guidance, and still rejects, is following the town's path. The verse invites Mecca to see itself: it received one messenger, Muḥammad, who is one of many (36:3), and his message is reinforced by the Qur'an itself, which is rich in wisdom (36:2), and by natural signs that follow. If a town that received three human messengers was seized, what about a town that receives a messenger plus a wise book plus signs in earth and heaven? The escalation in warning implies escalation in responsibility. The verse is short, but it establishes a principle of cumulative proof that runs through the entire sūrah and through the Qur'an as a whole.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:15

> The people replied, “You are only humans like us, and the Most Compassionate has not revealed anything. You are simply lying!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Humanity Objection**

The town's reply is the classic objection to messengers: *“You are only humans like us, and the Most Compassionate has not revealed anything. You are simply lying”* (36:15). The objection has two parts. First, you are humans like us, so you cannot be messengers; messengers should be angels or superhuman. Second, the Most Compassionate has not revealed anything, so there is no message to carry. Both parts aim to disqualify the messengers without engaging their message. The Qur'an records this objection repeatedly: *“They said, Has God sent a human as messenger?”* (17:94), *“What ails this messenger who eats food and walks in markets? Why was not an angel sent down with him?”* (25:7). The objection assumes that humanity and messengership are incompatible, while the Qur'an asserts they are inseparable. *“We never sent before you except men to whom We revealed”* (16:43). A human messenger is necessary because he can be an example; an angel cannot model human patience, charity, or repentance.

**Why the Name Most Compassionate Appears Here**

The town says al-Raḥmān, the Most Compassionate, has not revealed anything. The choice of name is pointed. Al-Raḥmān is the name that conveys universal mercy, the mercy that sends rain, gives life to dead earth, and provides ships and cattle. To say the Most Compassionate has not revealed anything is to deny that mercy includes guidance. It is to accept mercy in the form of grain and gardens but reject mercy in the form of warning. The sūrah later shows that al-Raḥmān's mercy includes both provision and warning: *“Glory be to the One Who created all things in pairs”* (36:36) is mercy in creation, and *“Peace will be their greeting from the Merciful Lord”* (36:58) is mercy in the next life. The town wants mercy without message, provision without accountability. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“They know the outward aspect of worldly life, but of the Hereafter they are heedless”* (30:7). Recognizing mercy in nature but denying it in revelation is a selective acknowledgment that the sūrah critiques.

**Accusation of Lying as a Defense**

*“You are simply lying”* (36:15) is the town's conclusion. It does not say we disagree or we need more evidence; it says you are lying. Accusing messengers of lying is easier than refuting them, because lying attacks motive, not content. If messengers are liars, their message need not be examined. The Qur'an notes that this accusation was made against many messengers: *“They said, You are but liars”* (26:185) to Shuʿayb, and similar to others. The accusation also protects the accusers' self-image. If messengers are liars, then rejecting them is not rejection of God but rejection of impostors, which feels righteous. The messengers' response in the next verse — *“Our Lord knows that we have truly been sent to you”* (36:16) — appeals to God's knowledge rather than to self-defense, showing that their concern is not reputation but truth. The town's accusation of lying will be mirrored by the Meccans who called Muḥammad a poet, magician, or possessed, all attempts to label rather than listen.

**The Link to Mecca's Objection**

The Quraysh made the same two claims about Muḥammad. They said he was a man like them who ate food and walked in markets, and they said the Qur'an was not revelation but something he invented or was taught by a human. The sūrah was revealed in Mecca precisely when those objections were loudest. By attributing the same objections to an earlier town, the Qur'an shows Mecca that its arguments are not original; they are the standard repertoire of denial. *“Nothing is said to you but what was said to messengers before you”* (41:43). The town's story thus functions as a mirror. If Mecca sees the town's objection as weak — and any listener would, because three messengers saying the same thing are unlikely to be lying together — then it must see its own objection as weak. The verse is therefore not only history but argument by analogy. The same objection, made by different people in different times, has the same flaw: it dismisses human messengers while benefiting from human mercy in every other domain.

**How the Qur'an Answers the Humanity Objection Elsewhere**

The Qur'an answers the humanity objection in several ways. First, it says an angel messenger would still appear as a man to be understandable: *“Had We made him an angel, We would have made him appear as a man”* (6:9). Second, it says human messengers are a mercy because they can be followed: *“There has come to you a messenger from among yourselves, grievous to him is what you suffer”* (9:128). Third, it says the message, not the messenger's nature, is the point: *“Say, I am only a man like you, it is revealed to me that your God is One God”* (18:110). The difference between messenger and ordinary human is not biology but revelation. The town focuses on shared humanity — like us — and ignores the distinction — revelation. That selective focus is the essence of heedlessness (36:6): seeing what is similar and missing what is distinctive. The sūrah later says *“Follow those who ask no reward and are rightly guided”* (36:21), giving criteria for distinguishing true messengers from impostors that do not depend on superhuman nature.

**A Verse That Exposes a Permanent Temptation**

The temptation to say messengers are only humans like us is permanent. Every generation prefers a messenger who is not like them — an angel, a superhuman, a distant historical figure — because a messenger like them is accountable and imitable. If the messenger is human like us, then his guidance applies to us; we cannot say his piety was due to angelic nature. The town's objection is therefore an attempt to make guidance inapplicable. The Qur'an counters by insisting that guidance comes through humans precisely so it is applicable. The final verse of the sūrah says *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things, and to Whom you will all be returned”* (36:83). Return to God will be as humans, not angels, and judgment will be of human deeds recorded in a perfect registry (36:12). A human messenger is fitting for a human return. The town's denial of revelation from the Most Compassionate is thus a denial of the very mercy that would prepare them for that return.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:16

> The messengers responded, “Our Lord knows that we have truly been sent to you

**Expanded Commentary**

**Appeal to Divine Knowledge**

The messengers answer the accusation of lying not with self-praise but with appeal to God's knowledge. *“Our Lord knows that we have truly been sent to you”* (36:16) places the dispute before a higher judge. The town said you are lying (36:15); the messengers say God knows we are truthful. The shift from human argument to divine witness is characteristic of prophetic speech. Messengers do not need to prove sincerity by elaborate oaths; they invoke the One who sent them. The Qur'an records similar appeals: *“God is sufficient as witness between me and you”* (13:43) and *“Say, God suffices as witness between me and you”* (29:52). The appeal also shows confidence. A liar would avoid invoking God as witness because divine witness would expose him; a truthful person welcomes it. The messengers' calm reliance on God's knowledge contrasts with the town's agitated accusation, and that contrast itself is evidence. Truthful speech is often quieter than false accusation, and the sūrah lets the quiet answer stand without embellishment.

**Why the Response Is Shorter Than the Accusation**

The town's accusation had three claims: you are humans like us, the Most Compassionate revealed nothing, you are lying. The messengers' reply addresses only the last, but by addressing it they address all. If God knows they have been sent, then they are not merely humans like us in the relevant sense — they are humans with a sending — and the Most Compassionate has revealed, because sending implies message. The brevity is rhetorical. Messengers do not need to refute every detail; they need to re-anchor the discussion in divine sending. The sūrah later defines their duty as only clear delivery (36:17), not exhaustive debate. This economy of speech is part of their credibility. Those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21) do not waste words defending ego; they state the fact of being sent and leave judgment to God. The Qur'an praises such restraint elsewhere: *“When the ignorant address them, they say Peace”* (25:63).

**Our Lord Knows as a Theological Claim**

Saying Our Lord knows is not only a claim about this incident but about God's attribute of knowledge. The sūrah emphasizes divine knowledge throughout: *“That is the design of the Almighty, All-Knowing”* (36:38) for the sun's course, *“He has perfect knowledge of every created being”* (36:79) for resurrection, *“We fully know what they conceal and what they reveal”* (36:76) for the Prophet's consolation. Knowledge is what makes judgment just. If God knows that messengers were sent, He also knows how they were received, and He records both in a perfect registry (36:12). The messengers' appeal to divine knowledge therefore anticipates the final accounting where *“no soul will be wronged in the least”* (36:54). The town thinks its rejection is a private matter between humans; the messengers say it is known to the Lord who knows all traces. That reframes the dispute from social to cosmic.

**Truly Sent: Emphasis and Assurance**

The Arabic la-innā ilaykum la-mursalūn contains double emphasis: indeed, truly, we have been sent to you. The emphasis responds to the town's emphatic denial. When one side says you are simply lying, the other says we are truly sent. The Qur'an uses such emphatic particles when truth is contested. The same emphasis appears in the sūrah's opening: *“You are truly one of the messengers”* (36:3) where the oath and particle together affirm Muḥammad's messengership against denial. The town messengers' emphatic claim mirrors that opening, linking their experience to his. Both are truly sent, both are denied, both appeal to divine knowledge. The parallel is intentional. Mecca hearing this story would recognize the same emphasis that was used for its own messenger, and would understand that denial of one sent is denial of the pattern of sending itself. The emphasis does not coerce belief; it clarifies that the claim is serious and not made lightly.

**How This Verse Models Prophetic Conduct**

The messengers' conduct here models how to respond to accusation. They do not return insult, they do not threaten, they do not invoke their own status. They say Our Lord knows. That response contains humility — we are servants whose Lord knows — and dignity — we are sent by that Lord. It also contains patience. They have been rejected twice as two, and now as three, yet they still speak calmly. The believing man who later rushes from the farthest end of the city will say *“Why should I not worship the One who originated me, and to Whom you will be returned”* (36:22), showing the same orientation toward the Creator rather than toward the crowd. The sūrah thus presents a community of those who appeal to God: messengers appeal to His knowledge that they were sent, the believer appeals to His origination and return. Both appeals bypass the town's power structure and place accountability directly with God, which is the essence of tawḥīd.

**A Verse That Prepares for Clear Delivery**

Verse sixteen is incomplete without seventeen. The messengers say Our Lord knows we have been sent, and then *“Our duty is only to deliver the message clearly”* (36:17). Knowledge and duty are paired. God knows they were sent; their duty is to deliver clearly. The first is God's concern, the second theirs. This division protects messengers from despair when their message is rejected. They are responsible for clarity, not for conversion. The Qur'an says to Muḥammad *“You do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills”* (28:56). The town messengers embody that principle. They have delivered clearly to a town that called them liars, and they still affirm that God knows they were sent. Their confidence is not in the town's response but in divine knowledge and in having fulfilled their duty. That confidence is what allows them to continue despite threat of stoning (36:18). The verse is short, but it holds a theology of prophetic mission that sustains messengers in every generation.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:17

> And our duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Defining the Limits of Messengership**

*“Our duty is only to deliver the message clearly”* (36:17) defines prophetic responsibility with precision. The messengers do not claim power to guide, to compel, or to punish. Their duty is balāgh mubīn, clear delivery. The Qur'an repeats this definition for Muḥammad and all messengers: *“The messenger's duty is only to deliver clearly”* (5:99, 29:18, 64:12). Clear delivery has two components: clarity of content — the message is understandable — and clarity of manner — the delivery is open, not hidden or coercive. The town messengers have delivered in both senses. They declared *“We have indeed been sent to you as messengers”* (36:14) and appealed to God's knowledge (36:16). Their message is clear; the town's rejection is not due to obscurity. This definition protects both messenger and audience. The messenger is protected from blame for others' disbelief; the audience is protected from compulsion, because belief must be chosen after clear delivery, not forced.

**Why Clarity Is Enough**

The Qur'an presents clarity as sufficient to establish proof. *“We have made it clear”* appears as a refrain after signs are given. If a message is clear and still rejected, the rejection is willful, not excusable by confusion. The town said *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15), which is not a response to message content but a dismissal of messengers. Clear delivery exposes that dismissal. The sūrah's later signs — dead earth revived (36:33), night stripped of day (36:37), sun on fixed course (36:38) — are also clear, yet *“Whenever a sign comes to them, they turn away from it”* (36:46). Clarity does not guarantee acceptance, but it guarantees justice. On the Day when *“no soul will be wronged in the least”* (36:54), no one can say the message was unclear. The messengers' statement therefore anticipates final judgment. Their duty was to deliver clearly; they did; the record in a perfect registry (36:12) preserves both delivery and response.

**Clear Delivery Versus Persuasion**

Modern readers sometimes confuse delivery with persuasion, thinking a messenger must convince. The Qur'anic definition separates them. Delivery is the messenger's act; persuasion, guidance, is God's act. *“You do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills”* (28:56). The town messengers embody this separation. They have delivered clearly to a town that threatens stoning (36:18), yet they do not resort to threats in return. They state their duty and leave result to God. This model shaped the Prophet's own conduct in Mecca, where he was told *“So remind, for you are only a reminder, you are not a controller over them”* (88:21-22). The distinction also explains why the sūrah pairs this verse with the next, where the town threatens punishment. The messengers offer clear words; the town offers stones. The contrast between word and stone is the contrast between guidance and coercion. Messengers deliver words clearly; tyrants deliver stones violently. The reader is asked which method is more legitimate.

**The Meaning of Mubīn, Clear**

Mubīn means clear, manifest, making clear. It describes both the message and the delivery. The message is clear in itself — its monotheism, its warning, its promise — and the delivery makes it clear to others. The Qur'an calls itself a clear book, *“a clear Qur'an”* (36:69), and says it was made easy in the Prophet's language so people may be mindful (44:58). Clarity does not mean simplistic; it means accessible to those who seek. The sūrah's arguments about resurrection from green trees producing fire (36:80) and from creation of heavens and earth (36:81) are clear in logic but require reflection. Clear delivery invites reflection; it does not force assent. The town's failure is not that it found the message unclear but that it found it unwelcome. It says *“We see you as a bad omen”* (36:18), shifting from content to superstition. Clear delivery cannot cure superstition unless the hearer is willing to examine his own omens, which the messengers point out lie within himself (36:19).

**How This Verse Consoles the Prophet**

For Muḥammad, whose people said *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15) in different wording, this verse is consolation. His duty is only clear delivery, not conversion of all. The sūrah opens by affirming he is truly among messengers upon straight path (36:3-4) and that his revelation is from Almighty, Most Merciful (36:5). His delivery is therefore clear by virtue of source and path. If most do not believe (36:7), that does not diminish clarity. The verse also links to the later assurance *“So do not let their words grieve you. Indeed, We fully know what they conceal and what they reveal”* (36:76). God knows both the clarity of delivery and the concealment of rejection. The messenger's duty is done; God's knowledge encompasses the rest. This division allows the Prophet to continue warning those who follow Reminder (36:11) without being crushed by those who do not. Clear delivery is a sustainable mission because its success is measured by fidelity, not numbers.

**A Principle for All Callers to Truth**

Beyond prophethood, this verse establishes a principle for anyone who calls to truth: duty is clear delivery, not control of outcome. A caller who understands this will not resort to coercion, deception, or reward-seeking, because those compromise clarity. The believing man who comes from farthest end of city says *“Follow those who ask no reward and are rightly guided”* (36:21), linking lack of reward-seeking to guidance. Clear delivery is free of ulterior motive. It says what it means, attributes knowledge to God, and leaves choice to hearer. The sūrah's final verse says authority over all things is in God's Hands and to Him all will be returned (36:83). If authority is His, then delivery is ours and judgment His. That division is liberating. It frees the caller from arrogance — I must make them believe — and from despair — they did not believe, so I failed. Duty is only to deliver clearly, and clarity itself is an act of mercy.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:18

> The people replied, “We definitely see you as a bad omen for us. If you do not desist, we will certainly stone you ˹to death˺ and you will be touched with a painful punishment from us.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Argument to Superstition and Threat**

The town's response escalates from denial to superstition to violence. *“We definitely see you as a bad omen for us. If you do not desist, we will certainly stone you to death and you will be touched with a painful punishment from us”* (36:18) shows how rejection hardens. First they said you are humans like us and liars (36:15). When messengers responded that God knows they were sent (36:16) and their duty is clear delivery (36:17), the town abandons argument and resorts to ṭiyarah, seeing bad omens. Ṭiyarah in Arab culture was interpreting bird flight or other signs as portending evil, then attributing that evil to persons. The Qur'an records similar accusations: Pharaoh's people said to Mūsā *“We see you and those with you as a bad omen”* (27:47), and earlier communities said the same. The move from you are liars to you are bad omens is a move from rational to superstitious rejection, which is harder to counter because it does not engage content at all.

**Why Bad Omen Accusation Is Convenient**

Calling messengers bad omens is convenient because it reverses causality. Instead of we are being warned because we are doing wrong, it says we are suffering because you arrived. The town attributes its misfortunes — drought, illness, economic hardship, whatever they experienced — to the presence of messengers rather than to its own deeds. The messengers' reply in the next verse exposes this: *“Your bad omen lies within yourselves”* (36:19). The Qur'an says elsewhere *“When good comes to them, they say This is from God, but when evil befalls them, they say This is from you”* (4:78), and *“No affliction befalls except by God's permission”* (64:11). Bad omen thinking allows a community to avoid self-examination. If messengers are the cause of bad luck, then removing them will restore luck. Hence the threat that follows: if you do not desist, we will stone you. Superstition leads to violence because it identifies a scapegoat whose removal promises relief.

**Stoning as a Communal Punishment**

The threat *“we will certainly stone you to death”* (36:18) is rajm, stoning, a public, communal form of execution that involves the whole town. It is not assassination but collective rejection made physical. Stoning appears elsewhere as threat against messengers: *“If you do not desist, O Ibrāhīm, I will surely stone you”* (19:46) says his father, and the people of Shuʿayb say *“Were it not for your clan, we would have stoned you”* (11:91). The threat is meant to intimidate through numbers. A single person can be ignored; a town that stones together enforces conformity. The addition *“and you will be touched with a painful punishment from us”* (36:18) suggests torture before or alongside stoning, indicating that the town claims authority to punish. It says punishment will be from us, as if it possesses the right to chastise. The messengers, who said their Lord knows they were sent (36:16), face a town that says punishment will be from us. Two authorities are contrasted: divine sending versus human threat.

**How Threat Reveals Lack of Argument**

When a community threatens stoning after being unable to refute clear delivery, it reveals that its position lacks argument. The messengers said our duty is only clear delivery (36:17). The town does not say your delivery is unclear; it says you are bad omens and we will stone you. The shift from words to stones is admission of intellectual defeat. The sūrah later says *“Oh pity, such beings! No messenger ever came to them without being mocked”* (36:30). Mockery and stoning are two forms of the same refusal to engage. The Qur'an presents this pattern to Mecca, which also moved from mockery to threat of expulsion and assassination against Muḥammad. The town's story warns that communities that begin by calling messengers liars often end by threatening violence, because lying accusation does not remove the messenger's presence, so stronger measures are sought. The progression is predictable once the first step is taken.

**The Psychological Mechanism of Omen Thinking**

Omen thinking, ṭiyarah, is addressed in hadith. The Prophet said *“There is no ṭiyarah, but the best is good optimism”* and *“Ṭiyarah is shirk”* in some narrations, recorded in Sunan Abī Dāwūd 3910 and graded authentic by many, indicating that attributing causation to omens compromises reliance on God. The psychological mechanism is that humans seek cause for misfortune, and when true cause — own deeds — is uncomfortable, they project cause onto outsiders. The town's misfortune, if any, likely came from its own injustice or natural cycles, but attributing it to messengers is easier. The Qur'an says *“Whatever affliction befalls you is because of what your hands have earned, and He pardons much”* (42:30). The messengers' reply that your omen lies within yourselves (36:19) points to that principle. It tells the town that its bad omen is its own deeds that have become a collar around its neck (36:8) and barriers before and behind (36:9). The town externalizes what is internal.

**A Verse That Shows the Cost of Delivering Clearly**

Finally, this verse shows the cost of clear delivery. Messengers who deliver clearly are threatened with stoning and painful punishment. The believing man who supports them will be killed and then told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26). Clear delivery is not safe in a hostile town. Yet the sūrah presents it as the only duty, regardless of cost. The Prophet Muḥammad faced similar threats, and the sūrah's inclusion of this threat is consolation: you are not the first to be threatened for delivering clearly, and threat does not indicate failure. The verse also sets up the contrast with the next world, where those who threatened stoning will themselves face a painful punishment not from a town but from God, and those threatened will be in Paradise saying *“If only my people knew how my Lord has forgiven me”* (36:26-27). The threat from us in this verse is answered by forgiveness from My Lord in that later verse. Two punishments and two mercies are contrasted, and the reader is asked which he prefers.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:19

> The messengers said, “Your bad omen lies within yourselves. Are you saying this because you are reminded ˹of the truth˺? In fact, you are a transgressing people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Your Omen Is Within Yourselves**

The messengers' reply reframes the town's superstition. *“Your bad omen lies within yourselves”* (36:19) — ṭāʾirukum maʿakum — says the cause of misfortune is not the messengers but the town's own deeds. The Arabic ṭāʾir, literally bird, was used for omen because Arabs divined by birds, but it came to mean fortune, destiny, or omen. The Qur'an uses it elsewhere: *“For every person We have fastened his omen to his neck”* (17:13), meaning his deeds and their consequences are attached to him. The messengers say your bird, your omen, is with you, not with us. It is your choices that produce your outcomes. The same idea appears in *“Whatever affliction befalls you is from what your hands have earned”* (42:30). By locating omen within selves, the messengers dismantle scapegoating. The town wants to stone messengers to remove bad luck; the messengers say bad luck, if any, will remain because its source is internal. This is the reflexive nature of deeds emphasized throughout the Qur'an: miserliness harms the miserly (47:38), deception deceives the deceiver (2:9), plotting plots against the plotter (52:42).

**Are You Saying This Because You Are Reminded**

The next phrase *“Are you saying this because you are reminded of the truth?”* (36:19) exposes the town's motive. The town's accusation of bad omen comes precisely when it is reminded. Reminder, dhikr, is what messengers bring, and the town's hostility is triggered by reminder, not by harm. The rhetorical question implies: you call us bad omens only because we reminded you, so your accusation is not about omens at all but about dislike of being reminded. The Qur'an says *“When God alone is mentioned, the hearts of those who do not believe in Hereafter shrink with aversion”* (39:45). Aversion to reminder is the real cause; omen language is cover. Some readings of the Arabic render the phrase as *“Even if you are reminded, you still say this?”* or *“Because you are reminded?”* All point to same: reminder is the trigger, not misfortune. The town is not suffering because messengers came; it is accusing messengers of bringing bad luck because it does not want to be reminded.

**You Are a Transgressing People**

The final clause *“In fact, you are a transgressing people”* (36:19) names the condition. Mus rifūn, transgressing, prodigal, exceeding limits. The word isrāf means crossing bounds, whether in wealth, power, or denial. The town exceeds limits by threatening stoning for clear delivery, by calling mercy bad omen, by attributing to messengers what belongs to itself. The Qur'an uses musrif for those who exceed in denial: *“Thus We reward the transgressor, the disbeliever”* (20:127) and Pharaoh is called *“indeed a transgressor”* (10:83). Transgression is not just sin but excess. The town does not merely disbelieve; it threatens painful punishment from itself (36:18), claiming authority that belongs to God. That excess is isrāf. The messengers' diagnosis moves from specific — your omen is with you — to general — you are transgressing people. Specific misattribution is symptom; transgression is disease. By naming it, they offer the town a chance to see itself accurately, which is first step to change.

**How This Verse Corrects Superstition**

This verse is one of the Qur'an's clearest corrections of superstition. It does not merely say omens are false; it says the true omen is one's own deeds. The Prophet taught *“There is no contagion without God's permission, no bad omen, no hāmah, no Ṣafar”* in a hadith recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5707, rejecting pre-Islamic superstitions that attributed causation to omens. The messengers' reply does same: no, your fortune is not determined by our arrival; it is determined by your response to reminder. This teaching liberates. If omen lies within self, then change of self changes fortune. The town can stop transgressing and its omen changes. The sūrah later says *“Had We willed, We could have blinded their eyes”* (36:66) but God does not; He allows time for change. The correction of superstition is therefore not only theological but practical: it returns agency to people. You are not victims of messengers' bad luck; you are agents whose transgression produces consequences, and agency can be redirected.

**The Relation to Mecca's Own Omen Thinking**

Mecca also practiced ṭiyarah. They saw the Prophet's message as bringing division, and they attributed drought and hardship to his presence or to his followers. The Qur'an says *“If good befalls them, they say This is from God, but if evil befalls them, they say This is from you”* (4:78) to the Prophet. By telling the story of a town that said messengers are bad omens, Yā Sīn tells Mecca that its own omen thinking is not new and not valid. The verse invites self-reflection: when you say Islam brings bad luck, is it because you have been reminded of truth you prefer to forget? The question *“Are you saying this because you are reminded?”* (36:19) is thus directed beyond the town to every listener who feels irritation when reminded of accountability, resurrection, or generosity. Irritation at reminder is often mislabeled as bad omen, bad timing, or bad messenger, when its source is internal resistance to being reminded.

**A Diagnosis That Still Offers a Way Out**

Although the verse ends with a harsh label — transgressing people — it is still part of clear delivery, not condemnation for its own sake. Naming transgression is necessary for cure. The believing man who comes next will say *“Why should I not worship the One who originated me, and to Whom you will be returned”* (36:22), offering an alternative to transgression: worship of the Originator and preparation for return. The messengers' diagnosis thus prepares for his invitation. They say where the problem lies — within yourselves — and what it is — transgression — so that the solution — worship and return — makes sense. The verse does not threaten stoning in return; it states fact. That restraint is part of their credibility as those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21). They correct superstition without resorting to superstition, and they name transgression without transgressing themselves. The reader is left with a clear choice: continue to externalize omen and remain transgressing, or internalize responsibility and turn.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:20

> Then from the farthest end of the city a man came, rushing. He advised, “O my people! Follow the messengers

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Man From the Farthest End**

After the town's threat of stoning (36:18) and the messengers' correction (36:19), a new figure appears: *“Then from the farthest end of the city a man came, rushing”* (36:20). The detail farthest end, aqṣā al-madīnah, is not incidental. It suggests distance, marginality, and effort. This man is not from the town center where power resides; he is from the outskirts, yet he rushes, yasʿā, running with urgency. The verb saʿā implies striving, hurrying for a purpose. The Qur'an uses it for those who strive for Hereafter: *“Whoever desires the Hereafter and strives for it”* (17:19). His rushing contrasts with the town's static barriers before and behind (36:9). While the town is enclosed, he moves. While the town threatens, he advises. His appearance breaks the deadlock between messengers and town, showing that even in a rejecting community, an individual can respond. The sūrah earlier said *“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder”* (36:11), and here is one who does.

**Why His Identity Matters Less Than His Action**

Classical sources name him Ḥabīb al-Najjār, the carpenter, and say he worshiped God in seclusion or that his son was healed by messengers. Some reports attribute to the Prophet a saying that the foremost are three who did not disbelieve for a blink: ʿAlī, the companion of Yā Sīn, and the believer of Pharaoh's people. These identifications are transmitted with varying chains and are not essential to the Qur'anic point. The Qur'an does not name him, which universalizes him. He is a man, rajul, from farthest end, who came rushing. Any person from any margin can be that man. The anonymity invites the listener to imagine himself as that rushing man, rather than as part of the threatening crowd. The sūrah's earlier division between those for whom warning is same as no warning (36:10) and those who follow Reminder (36:11) is now embodied: the crowd is the first, the rushing man is the second. The story moves from principle to person.

**Follow the Messengers: The Core Advice**

His advice is simple: *“O my people! Follow the messengers”* (36:20). He says my people, qawmī, claiming kinship even as he disagrees, showing that his counsel comes from belonging, not enmity. He does not say follow me; he says follow the messengers, directing attention away from himself to those who were sent. That self-effacement is mark of sincerity. He is not seeking leadership; he is pointing to those who ask no reward (36:21). The command to follow, ittabiʿū, is the same used for following the Reminder (36:11) and following the straight path. Following messengers means accepting their message that God alone is to be worshiped and that return is to Him (36:22). The rushing man's advice is thus not tribal but theological. He asks his people to follow those who call to worship the Originator, not those who threaten stoning. The contrast between follow the messengers and we will stone you (36:18) is stark: one invites, the other coerces.

**Rushing as a Sign of Faith**

Rushing, coming quickly, is praised in the Qur'an when it is toward good. *“Race to forgiveness from your Lord”* (3:133) and *“Hasten to remembrance of God”* (62:9) use similar urgency. The man rushes because he senses time is short — messengers are threatened, town is about to overstep, and opportunity to support truth is fleeting. His rushing is physical expression of inner conviction. The sūrah later describes those who will rush from graves to their Lord when trumpet is blown (36:51), using same root. One rushing is in this life toward messengers; the other rushing is in next life toward judgment. The parallel suggests that how one rushes now determines how one will rush then. The man from farthest end rushes toward guidance now, so his later rushing will be toward mercy, as he is told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26). The town that does not rush now will rush then in terror.

**A Marginal Voice With Central Truth**

The fact that he comes from farthest end suggests that truth often comes from margins, not centers of power. The town center, where leaders threaten, is the place of barriers; the farthest end, where this man lives, is place from which truth can still come. The Qur'an often notes that early believers were not elites: *“The arrogant among his people said, We see you only as a man like us, and we see only the lowest among us following you”* (11:27). Marginality is not disqualification. The sūrah earlier described shackles around necks up to chins (36:8) — a central, visible restraint — and barriers before and behind (36:9). The man from farthest end is outside that enclosure, or at least not yet fully enclosed, so he can still see and move. His rushing is thus also a sign that enclosure is not total; even in a town that seems sealed, a path from farthest end remains open for one who seeks.

**How This Verse Prepares for His Martyrdom**

This verse begins the man's intervention, which will end with his killing and entry into Paradise (36:26). By noting that he came rushing, the verse anticipates his fate. One who rushes to support messengers in a town that threatens stoning risks stoning himself. The sūrah does not hide that cost. It shows that following messengers can be dangerous, but that danger is followed by forgiveness and honor (36:27). The man's rushing is therefore both courage and faith. He knows his people — he calls them my people — and knows they threaten, yet he rushes anyway. His example answers the question of what it means to follow the Reminder and fear the Most Compassionate unseen (36:11) when following is costly. It means rushing from farthest end, advising even those who may kill you, and trusting that return is to the One who originated you (36:22). The verse is short, but it holds a complete portrait of a believer who acts alone against a crowd and whose single act is recorded in a perfect registry (36:12).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:21

> Follow those who ask no reward of you, and are ˹rightly˺ guided

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Criteria for True Guides**

The rushing man continues: *“Follow those who ask no reward of you, and are rightly guided”* (36:21). He gives his people two tests to distinguish true messengers from impostors. First, they ask no reward, ajr, from you. Second, they are rightly guided, muhtadūn. The first is about motive, the second about content. A person who seeks no worldly gain — money, status, power — is more likely to be sincere; a person who is rightly guided has something worth following. The Qur'an emphasizes the first criterion repeatedly. Nūḥ says *“I ask you no reward for it; my reward is only with God”* (10:72), and similar statements come from Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ, Shuʿayb (26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180). Muḥammad is told to say *“I ask you no reward for it”* (6:90, 38:86, 42:23). The absence of reward-seeking is a sign that the call is for God, not self. The town messengers have asked nothing; they have only delivered clearly (36:17) despite threat of stoning (36:18).

**Why No Reward Matters**

Asking no reward matters because it removes conflict of interest. If messengers sought payment, their message could be dismissed as self-serving. By asking nothing, they leave the town with no excuse. The town cannot say they want our wealth; they want only that we worship the One who originated us (36:22). The criterion also protects the community from exploitation. False leaders often seek reward — money, obedience, deference — while true guides seek God's pleasure. The believing man's own behavior matches the criterion: he rushes from farthest end, not for payment, but out of concern for his people. He says my people, indicating kinship, not superiority. His advice to follow those who ask no reward is thus self-consistent: he himself asks no reward for advising. The sūrah later contrasts those who take gods besides God hoping to be helped (36:74) with messengers who seek no help from people. One seeks help from false gods; the other seeks no help from people, only from God.

**Rightly Guided as Second Test**

The second criterion, rightly guided, means they follow guidance themselves and call to it. Guidance in Qur'anic usage includes correct belief, worship of God alone, and moral uprightness. The messengers are guided because they worship the One who originated them and to whom return is (36:22), they do not attribute bad omens to others (36:19), and they deliver clearly without transgression. The town is transgressing (36:19), so it is not guided. The contrast is between those who exceed limits and those who are upon straight path (36:4). The rushing man tells his people to follow the guided, not the transgressing. This is practical wisdom: follow those whose lives show guidance. The sūrah earlier said messengers are upon straight path (36:4); now the believer says they are rightly guided, linking his testimony to the opening affirmation. His statement is thus not new doctrine but recognition of what was already affirmed.

**How This Verse Applies Beyond the Town**

The two criteria — no reward, rightly guided — are universal tests for any caller to truth, in any time. A caller who demands payment, status, or blind loyalty fails first test; a caller whose life contradicts his call fails second. The Qur'an says *“Follow the way of those who turn to Me”* (31:15), indicating that following is about turning, not lineage. The believing man's advice is therefore timeless. It tells listeners to evaluate callers by motive and by guidance, not by numbers, power, or eloquence. The town's leaders who threaten stoning seek to preserve power — a reward — and they are transgressing, not guided. The messengers seek no reward and are guided. The choice should be clear, yet the town will kill the man who gives this clear criterion, showing how far transgression can go when guidance is rejected. The verse thus contains both advice and warning: advice to follow the right criteria, warning that those who give such advice may be persecuted.

**The Connection to the Prophet's Own Mission**

For Mecca, this verse directly describes Muḥammad. He asked no reward; his reward was with God. He was rightly guided, upon straight path (36:4), delivering a revelation from Almighty, Most Merciful (36:5). The Quraysh accused him of seeking leadership or wealth, but his life showed otherwise. He lived simply, gave charity, and endured hardship. The town's believing man, by giving criteria that fit Muḥammad, indirectly validates him. The Meccan listener who accepts that one should follow those who ask no reward and are guided must then ask whether Muḥammad fits. The sūrah does not force answer; it lets story do work. The believing man's criteria also answer the earlier objection that messengers are only humans like us (36:15). Yes, they are humans, but humans who ask no reward and are guided are not like those who seek reward and transgress. Shared humanity does not mean shared character. The distinction is moral, not biological.

**A Verse That Defines Integrity in Daʿwah**

Finally, this verse defines integrity in calling to God. Integrity is not eloquence but absence of self-seeking and presence of guidance. The man from farthest end embodies both: he rushes without reward and he calls to worship the Originator. His integrity costs him his life, but it gains him Paradise and forgiveness (36:27). The sūrah records his words so that later callers can measure themselves by them. Do I ask reward? Am I guided? Those two questions are sufficient to purify intention. The verse is short — ten words in Arabic — but it holds a complete ethic of religious leadership. It says follow those who do not use you and who know where they are going. The town that kills such a caller chooses to follow those who use it and who are lost. The reader, hearing this, is invited to choose the other path, to follow those who ask no reward and are rightly guided, even if they come from farthest end and even if following them is unpopular.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:22

> And why should I not worship the One Who has originated me, and to Whom you will be returned

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Is an Argument**

The believing man asks: *“Why should I not worship the One who has originated me, and to Whom you will be returned”* (36:22). The question form is rhetorical; it asserts that there is no reason not to worship the Originator. The verb faṭara, originated, is stronger than khalaqa, created. Faṭara implies bringing into existence for first time, splitting non-existence to produce existence, and is linked to fiṭrah, primordial nature. The Qur'an says *“Set your face to religion as a ḥanīf, in the primordial nature upon which God originated mankind”* (30:30). To be originated is to be given a nature that inclines to worship. The man argues from origination: the One who originated me deserves worship. The second clause — to Whom you will be returned — argues from destiny. Return to God is inevitable, so worship should be directed to the One to whom return is. The two clauses together cover beginning and end, origin and return, and leave no room for other gods. If origin is from Him and return is to Him, worship in between should be for Him.

**Why He Says Me and You**

He says originated me, faṭaranī, but you will be returned, turjaʿūn. The shift from first person singular to second person plural is deliberate. He speaks of his own origination as personal experience — I was originated — but of return as collective certainty — you, all of you, will be returned. This includes his audience in the destiny he affirms, making his argument inclusive, not accusatory. He does not say you were originated, which might sound preachy; he says I was originated, which is humble and verifiable. Everyone knows he was originated; his existence is evidence. Then he says you will be returned, which is warning but also invitation. The Qur'an uses similar shifts: *“He is the One who created you from dust, then from a sperm-drop”* (40:67) moves from general to specific. The believing man's shift from me to you shows he is not separating himself as superior; he includes himself in origination and includes his people in return, so the call to worship is for all.

**Worship as Recognition of Origination**

Worship, ʿibādah, in Qur'anic language is not merely ritual but recognition of lordship. To worship the Originator is to acknowledge that existence is gift, not self-produced. The man asks why should I not worship, implying that not worshiping would be strange, contrary to reason. The Qur'an says *“How can you disbelieve in God when you were dead and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die, then give you life again, then to Him you will be returned?”* (2:28). The argument from origination to worship is foundational. The sūrah later asks *“Do they not see that We singlehandedly created for them cattle which are under their control?”* (36:71) and *“Do people not see that We have created them from a sperm-drop?”* (36:77), both arguing from creation to gratitude. The believing man's question is the personal version of those arguments. He has been originated; therefore he worships. His people have also been originated; why do they not?

**Return as Motivation**

*“To Whom you will be returned”* (36:22) introduces accountability. Origination alone might produce gratitude, but return produces urgency. If return is to God, then deeds done in between will be judged. The sūrah emphasizes return throughout: *“Everything is listed in a perfect Record”* (36:12), *“They will all be brought before Us”* (36:32), *“You will be rewarded except for what you used to do”* (36:54), *“To Him you will all be returned”* (36:83). Return is not annihilation but meeting. The believing man reminds his people that their return is to the same One who originated them, so their origin and destiny are linked. This link undermines idol worship, which he will address next: *“How could I take besides Him other gods whose intercession would not benefit me nor could they save me?”* (36:23). If return is to the Originator, intercession from others is irrelevant. The argument is tight: origin from Him, return to Him, therefore worship of Him alone.

**How This Verse Connects to Fiṭrah**

The word faṭara connects to fiṭrah, primordial nature, mentioned in 30:30. The Qur'an says God originated mankind upon a primordial nature that inclines to tawḥīd, and that there is no altering God's creation. The believing man's question appeals to that nature. Why should I not worship the One who originated me upon a nature that inclines to Him? To not worship would be to go against fiṭrah, to alter creation. The sūrah later says *“Did I not command you, O Children of Adam, not to follow Satan, for he is truly your sworn enemy, but to worship Me alone? This is the Straight Path”* (36:60-61). Worshiping the Originator is the straight path because it aligns with fiṭrah. The man's question is thus not only theological but anthropological: it asks his people to be true to their own origination. The Qur'an says *“We have certainly created man in best stature, then We return him to lowest of low, except those who believe and do righteous deeds”* (95:4-6). Worship preserves best stature; refusal returns to lowest.

**A Personal Creed That Becomes Universal**

Although spoken by one man in one town, this verse became a creed quoted by believers in many contexts. It is personal — why should I not worship — yet universal — to Whom you will be returned. It models how to argue for faith without arrogance: start from own origination, include others in return, and let question do work. The man does not say you must worship because I say so; he says why should I not worship, inviting listeners to ask same of themselves. His question is still asked today whenever someone reflects on existence. Why should I not worship the One who originated me? The sūrah answers with signs: dead earth revived (36:33), night stripped of day (36:37), sun and moon in orbits (36:38-40), ships and cattle (36:41-42, 71-72). All are acts of origination and sustenance that point to the Originator. The believing man's short question opens the door to that long catalogue of signs, and his fate — entering Paradise and wishing his people knew (36:26-27) — shows where that question leads when answered sincerely.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:23

> How could I take besides Him other gods whose intercession would not be of any benefit to me, nor could they save me if the Most Compassionate intended to harm me

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why Other Gods Cannot Intercede**

The believing man continues his argument: *“How could I take besides Him other gods whose intercession would not be of any benefit to me, nor could they save me if the Most Compassionate intended to harm me?”* (36:23). He has argued from origination and return (36:22); now he argues from powerlessness of idols. Intercession, shafāʿah, in Arab belief was that idols or lesser deities could plead for their worshipers before the high God. The Qur'an corrects this: intercession belongs entirely to God, and none can intercede except by His permission. *“Who is there who can intercede with Him except by His permission?”* (2:255) and *“On that Day intercession will not benefit except one to whom the Most Compassionate has given permission”* (20:109). If the Most Compassionate intends harm — as consequence of disbelief — no idol's intercession can prevent it, because idols have no independent power. The man's question exposes the futility: why worship what cannot help?

**The Choice of Most Compassionate Again**

He says if al-Raḥmān, the Most Compassionate, intended harm for me. The use of Most Compassionate for harm seems paradoxical, but it is deliberate. Even harm that comes from God is within His compassion, because it is just and may lead to repentance, or because His compassion is the standard against which harm is measured. The town earlier said the Most Compassionate revealed nothing (36:15), denying His merciful guidance. The believing man now says even if the Most Compassionate intended harm, idols could not save. He thus affirms both that al-Raḥmān does reveal and that His will is decisive. The sūrah uses al-Raḥmān for both mercy and decisive will: *“This must be what the Most Compassionate warned us of”* (36:52) on the Day of Resurrection. The name that promises mercy also warns, because mercy includes justice. The man's argument holds both: I fear the Most Compassionate's just harm, and I know idols cannot prevent it, so I will not take them.

**Intercession That Does Not Benefit**

The Qur'an distinguishes between intercession that is permitted and intercession that benefits. Idols' intercession neither benefits nor saves. *“They worship besides God what neither harms nor benefits them, and they say These are our intercessors with God”* (10:18). The verse says their intercession would not avail me at all, lā tughni ʿannī shafāʿatuhum shayʾā. The phrase shayʾā, anything at all, emphasizes total uselessness. Not a little, not in some cases — nothing. Nor would they save me, yunqidhūn, rescue. Intercession and rescue are two hopes people place in idols: plead for me, or pull me out. Both are denied. The sūrah later says *“They cannot help the pagans, even though they serve the idols as dedicated guards”* (36:75). The idols cannot help, and the pagans end up guarding what cannot guard them. The believing man's question anticipates that later critique. Why take what cannot help as helper?

**A Question That Answers Itself**

*“How could I take besides Him other gods?”* (36:23) is istifhām inkārī, a rhetorical question that denies its own possibility. The form implies: I could not, it would be irrational. The man does not say it is forbidden, though it is; he says it would be irrational given what idols cannot do. This is effective persuasion for a crowd that thinks in terms of benefit and harm. He appeals to their own logic: you take gods hoping to be helped (36:74), but they cannot help, so why take them? The question also shows his personal stake: would not benefit me, nor save me. He is not speaking abstract theology but about his own fate if al-Raḥmān intended harm. That personalization makes argument concrete. He is about to be killed by his people; he knows harm may come, but he trusts that idols cannot save him from divine will, so he will not turn to them even under threat. His monotheism is tested by threat and holds.

**How This Verse Connects to the Sūrah's Critique of Idols**

The sūrah's later verses say *“Still they have taken other gods besides Allah, hoping to be helped by them. They cannot help the pagans, even though they serve the idols as dedicated guards”* (36:74-75). Verse twenty-three is the early, personal version of that critique. The believing man, facing his people, says idols cannot intercede or save. The sūrah, addressing its audience, says idols cannot help even though they are guarded. Both point to reversal: worshipers think they are protected by idols, but in fact they end up protecting idols. The man's question also anticipates the sealing of mouths and testimony of hands and feet (36:65). On that Day, intercession from idols will be absent, and limbs will testify to what was done. The only intercession that benefits is by God's permission, and permission is given to those whose word is pleasing to Him (20:109). Idols, having no word pleasing to God, will have no permission. The man's logic is thus eschatological as well as present: if idols cannot help now when harm is threatened by people, how will they help then when harm is decreed by God?

**A Creed That Remains Relevant**

The question remains relevant because humans still take besides God other things hoping for intercession or rescue — wealth, status, connections, ideologies — that cannot benefit when divine will decrees otherwise. The believing man's criterion is practical: does this thing benefit me or save me if the Most Compassionate intends otherwise? If not, why take it as god? The Qur'an says *“If God touches you with harm, none can remove it but He, and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty”* (10:107). The man's argument is that monotheism is not only true but rational. To worship the Originator to Whom return is (36:22) and to reject what cannot intercede or save (36:23) is to align belief with reality. His question, asked while rushing from farthest end of city, becomes a timeless test for any object of devotion: can it intercede? Can it save? If not, it is not god. That clarity is why his words are preserved, and why he is told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) immediately after.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:24

> Indeed, I would then be clearly astray

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Consequence He Accepts for Himself**

The believing man concludes his argument about idols with a self-judgment: *“Indeed, I would then be clearly astray”* (36:24). If he took besides God other gods whose intercession does not benefit and who cannot save (36:23), he would be in ḍalāl mubīn, manifest error. The phrase ḍalāl mubīn appears throughout the Qur'an for error that is obvious, evident, not hidden. *“Whoever takes Satan as protector instead of God has certainly incurred manifest loss”* (4:119) and *“Indeed, the wrongdoers are in manifest error”* (19:38) use similar language. By saying I would be clearly astray, the man shows he applies to himself the same standard he asks of his people. He does not say you are astray, though they are; he says I would be astray if I did what you do. That humility makes his warning more acceptable. He places himself under judgment first, which is characteristic of sincere callers. The Qur'an says *“Why do you say what you do not do? It is most hateful to God that you say what you do not do”* (61:2-3). He says what he does, and says what he would be if he did otherwise.

**Why Manifest Error, Not Just Error**

Mubīn, manifest, means clear, evident, making clear. Error that is manifest is error that is obvious to anyone who reflects, and error that makes its own evidence clear. Taking powerless idols as gods while acknowledging that the Most Compassionate's will is decisive is manifestly inconsistent. The inconsistency is visible: you hope idols will help, yet you admit they cannot help if al-Raḥmān intends harm. That contradiction is manifest. Some readings interpret mubīn as active — error that makes manifest, that reveals — meaning the error itself shows its own falsehood. Others read it as passive — error that is made manifest, obvious. Both fit. The man's argument has made the error manifest by asking how idols could benefit or save. Once that question is asked, the error of taking them is obvious. The sūrah later says *“Why should we feed those whom Allah could have fed if He wanted to? You are clearly astray”* (36:47), where disbelievers call believers clearly astray, reversing the label. The believing man's use here is the correct application: taking powerless intercessors is clear straying.

**A Short Verse With a Complete Theology**

Four words in Arabic, six in English, yet this verse contains a complete theology of error. Error is not merely mistaken opinion; it is taking a path that leads away from origin and return. The man earlier said *“Why should I not worship the One who originated me, and to Whom you will be returned”* (36:22). Worshiping the Originator is guidance; worshiping others is error. The error is clear because origin and return are clear. If you were originated by Him and will be returned to Him, worshiping others is deviation from both start and end. The verse also shows that error is not neutral; it has consequence. The sūrah says *“This is the Hell you were warned of. Burn in it today for your disbelief”* (36:63-64). Manifest error in this life leads to manifest punishment in next, just as manifest guidance leads to *“Peace from Merciful Lord”* (36:58). The brevity of the verse mirrors the simplicity of choice: worship the One who can harm and benefit, or be clearly astray by worshiping what cannot.

**How This Verse Functions in the Narrative**

In the narrative, this verse is the man's final self-warning before his public declaration of faith: *“I do believe in your Lord, so listen to me”* (36:25). He first argues why he should worship the Originator (36:22), why he cannot take other gods (36:23), what he would be if he did (36:24), then declares belief (36:25). The sequence is logical: reason, refutation, self-judgment, declaration. It models how faith is articulated under pressure. He does not begin with I believe; he begins with why belief is rational, what alternative would mean, then states belief. That order is persuasive for his people, who are being asked to follow messengers who ask no reward (36:21). By saying I would be clearly astray if I took other gods, he shows he has considered alternative and found it wanting. His faith is not blind; it is reasoned, and its reason is that alternative is manifest error. The narrative then moves quickly to his martyrdom and entry into Paradise (36:26), showing that avoiding manifest error has immediate reward, even if it costs life.

**The Link to the Sūrah's Larger Theme of Error**

The sūrah uses ḍalāl, error, and its derivatives to describe those who take other gods, deny resurrection, and mock messengers. *“Yet he already misled great multitudes of you. Did you not have any sense?”* (36:62) says about Satan, who misleads into error. The believing man's statement that he would be clearly astray if he took other gods aligns him against Satan's misleading. Satan's path is manifest error; the Originator's worship is straight path (36:61). The man's short verse thus places him on straight path and identifies the alternative as error. The sūrah later says *“Indeed, on that Day the residents of Paradise will be busy enjoying themselves”* (36:55), implying they avoided error. The believing man is among them, as his wish *“If only my people knew how my Lord has forgiven me and made me one of the honourable”* (36:26-27) shows. His avoidance of manifest error led to honor, while his people's persistence in it led to being extinguished by a single blast (36:29).

**A Verse That Invites Self-Examination**

For the reader, this short verse invites self-examination: what would make me clearly astray? The man's answer is taking besides God what cannot benefit or save. That test can be applied to many modern attachments. Whatever is taken as ultimate besides God — wealth, power, ideology — that cannot intercede or save when al-Raḥmān intends otherwise, is a candidate for manifest error. The verse does not elaborate; it states consequence plainly. Indeed, I would then be clearly astray. The clarity of the statement matches the clarity of the error it describes. There is no hedging, no perhaps. If I took other gods whose intercession does not benefit and who cannot save, I would be clearly astray. That directness is part of why the sūrah is called heart of Qur'an. It goes to heart of matter without digression. The reader is left with a simple question: am I taking anything besides Him that cannot benefit or save? If so, I am in manifest error, and the believing man's example shows the way out is to declare *“I believe in your Lord, so listen to me”* (36:25).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:25

> I do believe in your Lord, so listen to me.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Public Declaration of Faith**

After reasoning about worshiping the Originator (36:22), rejecting powerless intercessors (36:23), and stating that taking them would be manifest error (36:24), the believing man declares: *“I do believe in your Lord, so listen to me”* (36:25). The declaration is addressed to messengers — your Lord — not to his people directly, though they overhear. Some exegetes read it as declaration to his people: I believe in your Lord, the Lord of messengers, so listen to me, my people. Others read it as appeal to messengers to bear witness: I believe in your Lord, so listen to me and testify for me before Him. Both readings converge: faith is declared publicly, and listening is requested. The verb āmantu, I believe, is in past tense, indicating settled conviction, not tentative inclination. He does not say I am considering; he says I do believe. The immediacy is important because he is in a hostile crowd that just threatened stoning (36:18). Declaring belief in that context is an act of courage that matches his rushing from farthest end (36:20).

**Why Your Lord, Not My Lord**

He says your Lord, rabbikum, addressing messengers, rather than my Lord, though he also believes. The choice shows deference. He attributes lordship to the One messengers call to, acknowledging their authority. It also shows he is joining their community. Previously he was one of the town; now he aligns with messengers by calling their Lord his Lord too. The shift from my people (36:20) to your Lord (36:25) marks his transition. He still calls them my people out of kinship, but his theological allegiance is now with messengers' Lord. The Qur'an records similar shifts: the magicians of Pharaoh, after seeing Mūsā's sign, say *“We believe in the Lord of all worlds, the Lord of Mūsā and Hārūn”* (26:47-48), attributing lordship to the messengers' Lord. The believing man's declaration is of same type: recognition that the Lord messengers call to is true Lord, and his own lordship is included in that.

**So Listen to Me as Request for Testimony**

*“So listen to me”* (36:25), fasmaʿūn, can mean listen to my declaration and accept it, or listen and bear witness for me. If addressed to his people, it means listen to my advice to follow messengers (36:20-21). If addressed to messengers, it means listen to my faith and testify that I believed before God. Both are plausible, and the ambiguity is meaningful. A believer wants both: his people to listen and be guided, and messengers to listen and testify. The Qur'an says *“How will it be when We bring a witness from every community and bring you as witness over these?”* (4:41). Messengers will testify about their communities. The believing man asks messengers to listen so they can testify that he believed despite his people. His request anticipates the scene where he is told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) and wishes his people knew how his Lord forgave him (36:27). His listening request is granted in next life: his testimony is preserved in Qur'an itself, which is recited until Day of Resurrection.

**A Declaration That Costs Life**

According to many reports, after this declaration the man was stoned and killed by his people. Qatādah reports he was praying for God to guide his people even while being stoned. The Qur'an does not detail his killing but implies it by saying he was told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) immediately after his declaration, indicating death and entry. His declaration thus costs him life but gains Paradise. The sūrah presents this as exemplary: true faith is declared even when costly. The earlier verse *“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and are in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him”* (36:11) defines those who benefit from warning as those who fear unseen. This man fears unseen enough to declare belief before a hostile crowd that can see and harm him. His awe of unseen outweighs fear of seen. That is why he is promised forgiveness and honorable reward (36:11) in principle, and entry into Paradise in narrative. His declaration is the lived example of the principle.

**How This Verse Models Daʿwah Under Threat**

The man's sequence models how to call to God under threat: rush from farthest end (36:20), advise to follow those who ask no reward and are guided (36:21), argue from origination and return (36:22), refute powerless intercessors (36:23), state consequence of taking them (36:24), then declare belief and ask for listening (36:25). He does not begin with declaration; he begins with reasoning that his people can follow, then declares. This order respects audience. It also shows that declaration is not mere words; it is conclusion of reasoning. For Mecca, where Prophet faced similar threats, this model was instructive. The Prophet was told *“So do not let their words grieve you”* (36:76) and his duty is only clear delivery (36:17). The believing man's delivery is clear and ends with declaration, even though his people will kill him. The sūrah thus teaches that success of daʿwah is not measured by immediate acceptance but by clarity and sincerity of declaration, and that declaration may be preserved and rewarded beyond life.

**A Short Verse That Holds a Complete Faith**

Four words in Arabic, seven in English, yet this verse holds complete faith: belief, attribution, and request. I believe — internal conviction. In your Lord — correct object of belief, not idols. So listen to me — desire to share and to be witnessed. The three together form a minimal creed that any believer can adopt. It is also a bridge to next verses: after declaration, he is told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26), showing that declaration is accepted. The sūrah then says *“We did not send any soldiers from heavens against his people after his death, nor did We need to”* (36:28), showing that God's response to killing a believer does not require armies, only a single blast (36:29). The man's short declaration thus triggers both his personal reward and his people's punishment, illustrating that individual faith and communal rejection have consequences that are recorded in perfect registry (36:12) and will be manifested on Day when *“they will all be brought before Us”* (36:32). The verse invites every reader to make same declaration, regardless of cost, and to ask others to listen.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:26

> ˹But they killed him, then˺ he was told ˹by the angels˺, “Enter Paradise!” He said, “If only my people knew

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Martyrdom to Paradise**

The translation adds context that the text implies: they killed him, then he was told *“Enter Paradise!”* (36:26). The Arabic qīla udkhul al-jannah, it was said Enter the Garden, is passive, indicating angels or divine command welcoming him immediately after death. The immediacy is striking. No waiting, no intermediate questioning described; entry is direct. The man who rushed from farthest end of city (36:20), advised to follow messengers who ask no reward (36:21), argued from origination and return (36:22), rejected powerless intercessors (36:23), declared himself clearly astray if he took them (36:24), and finally declared *“I believe in your Lord, so listen to me”* (36:25), is now told Enter Paradise. The sequence shows that faith declared under threat is accepted without delay. The Qur'an says *“Do not think those killed in God's path are dead; rather they are alive with their Lord, provided for”* (3:169). This man's entry illustrates that principle. His killing by his people is not end; it is doorway.

**Why Enter Paradise Is Said Immediately**

The command Enter Paradise is given right after his declaration, suggesting that his martyrdom is cause of entry, but also that Paradise is near for the sincere. The sūrah earlier promised *“good news of forgiveness and an honourable reward”* (36:11) for those who follow Reminder and fear Most Compassionate unseen. The believing man fits that description, and his reward is now shown as realized. The immediacy also contrasts with his people's fate, who will be extinguished by a single blast (36:29). One man enters Paradise with a word; a whole town is extinguished with a blast. The asymmetry shows divine justice: individual faith is honored individually, collective rejection is punished collectively, and neither requires armies (36:28). The passive qīla, it was said, also indicates honor; he is addressed by angels, not left alone. The sūrah later describes Paradise residents as busy enjoying themselves, reclining, having fruits, greeted with peace (36:55-58). His entry is entry into that described bliss.

**If Only My People Knew as Ongoing Concern**
