Even in Paradise, his concern remains his people: *“He said, If only my people knew”* (36:26). The wish shows that entry into Paradise does not erase compassion for those left behind. He does not gloat; he wishes they knew what he now knows. The same sentiment appears in other martyrs' wishes, and it models the prophetic concern that persists beyond personal salvation. The Prophet Muḥammad, despite persecution, prayed for his people to be guided. The believing man's wish is of same type. It also shows that knowledge he now has — forgiveness and honor (36:27) — is something his people lack but could have if they followed messengers. His wish is not for revenge but for their guidance, even after they killed him. Qatādah reports he was praying for God to guide his people while being stoned, and his wish in Paradise continues that prayer. The verse thus presents a believer whose concern for his people survives death, which is true sincerity.

**The Connection to Forgiveness and Honor**

The next verse completes his wish: *“of how my Lord has forgiven me, and made me one of the honourable”* (36:27). Forgiveness and honor are exactly what was promised in 36:11: forgiveness and honourable reward. His entry into Paradise is realization of that promise. Forgiveness, maghfirah, indicates his sins are covered; honor, being among mukramīn, indicates his status is elevated. The two together show mercy and generosity. The sūrah earlier said *“Everything is listed in a perfect Record”* (36:12). His forgiveness indicates his record, though containing human faults, has been covered by mercy. His honor indicates his good deed — rushing, advising, believing — has been recorded and rewarded. The verse thus ties narrative to doctrine: record is kept, forgiveness is possible, honor is given. The man's wish that his people knew is wish that they knew forgiveness and honor are available to them too, if they follow messengers.

**How This Verse Consoles the Prophet**

For the Prophet, whose people also threatened and harmed believers, this verse is consolation. A believer from a previous town was killed for his faith and immediately honored. The same could happen to believers in Mecca, and their honor would be similar. The verse also consoles that killing a believer does not end his influence. His words are preserved in Qur'an, recited by millions, and his wish — if only my people knew — continues to call. The sūrah later says *“We did not send soldiers from heavens against his people after his death, nor did We need to”* (36:28), indicating that God defends believers without armies. The man's entry into Paradise without waiting for Day of Resurrection shows that divine reward is not delayed until final accounting; it begins immediately for the sincere. That immediacy encourages perseverance. The path of rushing from farthest end, declaring belief, and being killed is not loss but entry.

**A Verse That Defines Martyrdom as Entry, Not End**

Finally, this verse defines martyrdom as entry, not end. The command Enter Paradise transforms killing from termination to transition. The man's physical life ends, but his true life begins. The Qur'an says martyrs are alive with their Lord (3:169), and this verse shows that aliveness as entry and speech — he speaks, wishing his people knew. His speech in Paradise mirrors his speech in city: there he said follow messengers (36:20), here he says if only my people knew. Both are calls to his people, one before death, one after. The continuity shows that sincere concern transcends death. The verse invites readers to consider what they would wish their people knew if they were told Enter Paradise now. Would they wish for revenge or for guidance? The believing man's wish is for guidance, which is why he is among honourable. His example remains, from farthest end of city to farthest reach of time, as a model of faith that rushes, declares, forgives, and wishes good even for those who kill.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:27

> of how my Lord has forgiven me, and made me one of the honourable.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forgiveness and Honor as Twin Gifts**

This verse completes the believing man's wish: *“of how my Lord has forgiven me, and made me one of the honourable”* (36:27). Two gifts are named: forgiveness, maghfirah, and being made among mukramīn, the honoured. Forgiveness is removal of sin; honor is elevation of status. The first is negative — what is taken away — the second positive — what is given. Together they describe complete salvation. The Qur'an often pairs them: *“For them is forgiveness and an honourable reward”* (36:11, 67:12). The man's wish is that his people knew both, so they would be motivated to seek same. He does not wish they knew his suffering or his people's crime; he wishes they knew God's forgiveness and honor, because that knowledge would attract them to faith. His concern remains evangelical even in Paradise. The wish also shows that forgiveness and honor are from My Lord, rabbī, not from messengers or himself. He attributes both to God, preserving tawḥīd even in gratitude.

**Why Forgiveness Comes First**

Forgiveness is mentioned before honor because forgiveness is prerequisite. A person cannot be among honoured while sins remain unpardoned. The sūrah earlier promised forgiveness and honourable reward for those who follow Reminder and fear Most Compassionate unseen (36:11). The man's entry into Paradise shows that promise fulfilled: forgiveness first, then honor. The Qur'an says *“Whoever is removed from Fire and admitted to Paradise has indeed succeeded”* (3:185). Removal — forgiveness — then admission — honor — is the sequence. The man's sins, whatever they were, are forgiven, and then he is placed among honoured. Honour here likely means among prophets, martyrs, and righteous, or among those near to God, as in *“Indeed, the honoured servants”* (21:26) for angels. The term mukramīn appears for guests of Ibrāhīm who were angels (51:24) and for those in Paradise. Being among honoured means being in noble company, not solitary bliss.

**What His People Did Not Know**

His people did not know that killing him would lead to his forgiveness and honor, nor that their own rejection would lead to a single blast extinguishing them (36:29). They thought stoning would remove bad omen (36:18), but it removed a sincere adviser and brought punishment. The man's wish — if only my people knew — is that they knew the true consequences of their actions. If they knew that belief leads to forgiveness and honor, they might believe. If they knew that killing a believer leads to immediate Paradise for him and swift punishment for them, they might refrain. Knowledge of consequences changes behavior, which is why the Qur'an is described as Reminder. The man's wish is that his people had the knowledge he now has, which is knowledge of unseen that he now sees. His wish mirrors the Prophet's own wish for his people, and it models that true faith wishes guidance for enemies, not just for friends.

**The Relation to the Sūrah's Promise**

The sūrah promised *“good news of forgiveness and an honourable reward”* (36:11) for those who follow Reminder. The believing man's story is the narrative proof of that promise. He followed Reminder — follow messengers (36:20) — feared Most Compassionate unseen — worshiped Originator (36:22) — and now receives forgiveness and honor. The story thus functions as tafsīr of the promise. It shows what following looks like in practice — rushing from farthest end, advising, arguing, declaring — and what reward looks like — immediate entry, forgiveness, honor. The story also shows that forgiveness and honor are not delayed until final Day only; they begin at death for martyrs. The Qur'an says *“Do not say of those killed in God's path, They are dead; rather they are alive, but you perceive not”* (2:154). The man's being among honoured while his people are still alive shows that aliveness.

**How Qatādah's Report Illuminates This Verse**

Qatādah, a Successor, reported that the man was stoned while praying for God to guide his people, and continued praying even as stones struck. That report, though not with a rigorously authenticated chain, captures the spirit of the verse: concern for people persists through suffering. His wish in Paradise — if only my people knew how my Lord forgave me and made me among honoured — is continuation of his prayer during stoning. He prayed for guidance while being killed; he wishes for their knowledge after being honored. The continuity shows that his faith was not transactional — I will pray if you listen — but unconditional — I will pray even if you kill. That unconditional concern is why he is among honoured. The sūrah later says *“Peace will be their greeting from Merciful Lord”* (36:58). His peace includes wishing peace for others, even those who harmed him. That is honourable character.

**A Verse That Defines True Honor**

True honor in Qur'anic vocabulary is not wealth, lineage, or power, but being forgiven and placed among honoured by God. The town's leaders sought honor through stoning messengers and maintaining status, but they were extinguished by a single blast (36:29). The man from farthest end, with no status, sought no reward (36:21) and was made among honoured. The reversal is deliberate. The sūrah says *“Indeed, on that Day residents of Paradise will be busy enjoying themselves”* (36:55), and this man is among them, busy in enjoyment, while his killers are busy being punished. Honor is thus redefined. It is not who has power to stone, but who is forgiven and honoured by God. The man's wish that his people knew this redefinition is still relevant. Many today seek honor through power, wealth, or social media, while Qur'anic honor comes from forgiveness and being among honoured, which requires faith, rushing to truth, and concern for others even when harmed. His short wish holds that redefinition, and his story preserves it.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:28

> We did not send any soldiers from the heavens against his people after his death, nor did We need to

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Army Needed to Punish a Town**

After the believing man's entry into Paradise and his wish, the sūrah says: *“We did not send any soldiers from the heavens against his people after his death, nor did We need to”* (36:28). The verse emphasizes that destroying a town that killed a believer and rejected messengers does not require heavenly armies. A single blast will suffice (36:29). The statement has two purposes: to show God's power — He does not need armies — and to show the town's insignificance — it is not worth an army. The Qur'an says elsewhere *“We sent against them a single blast, and they became like dry twigs of a sheepfold”* (54:31) and *“It was but a single cry, and they were extinguished”* (36:29). The pattern is that great punishment can come through small means when God wills. The town that threatened stoning and painful punishment from itself (36:18) is answered not by angelic legions but by a single sound. The disproportion between threat and punishment shows who truly holds power.

**Why Soldiers From Heavens Are Mentioned**

Jund, soldiers, hosts, from heavens, mina al-samāʾ, suggests angels or heavenly armies. Some exegetes take it as angels of punishment; others as angels of revelation, meaning no new revelation was sent after messengers. The first reading fits context of destruction; second fits warning to Quraysh that no new messengers will come after Muḥammad if they reject. Both are plausible and not mutually exclusive. The phrase after his death, min baʿdih, indicates that punishment came after the believer's death, not immediately during his life, showing divine patience. God allowed the town time after killing a believer, but when punishment came, it was swift and without heavenly army. The statement nor did We need to, wa mā kunnā munzilīn, denies need, not just action. God did not send soldiers because He did not need to. The town's power, which seemed great when it threatened stoning, is revealed as trivial before divine command.

**The Relation to the Sūrah's Theme of Power**

This verse contributes to the sūrah's theme that true power belongs to God alone. The sūrah says *“If We willed, We could drown them, then no one would respond to their cries, nor would they be rescued, except by mercy from Us”* (36:43-44) about ships, and *“Had We willed, We could have blinded their eyes”* (36:66) and *“transfigured them on the spot”* (36:67). All show that God can punish without intermediaries, but He often refrains out of mercy, allowing enjoyment for a little while (36:44). The town that killed the believer received that little while after his death, then a single blast. The verse also contrasts with the believing man's honor. He was made among honoured (36:27) without army; his people were destroyed without army. Both reward and punishment are easy for God. The sūrah's final verse says authority over all things is in God's Hands (36:83), and this verse illustrates that authority: no soldiers needed to enforce it.

**How This Verse Warns Mecca**

For Mecca, which prided itself on military strength and tribal alliances, this verse warns that divine punishment does not require equal military force. The Quraysh had armies and allies; God does not need armies to destroy. The sūrah was revealed when Mecca was persecuting believers, and some believers were killed or tortured. The story of a town that killed a believer and was then destroyed without heavenly army would have resonated. It says God can avenge believers without sending visible armies; a single blast, a single cry, suffices. The verse also says We did not send soldiers, implying that if God had sent soldiers, destruction would have been even more evident, but even without them, destruction came. The warning is that Mecca should not be deceived by absence of visible heavenly intervention. The absence of angels does not mean absence of divine power or concern. The perfect record (36:12) preserves the killing, and authority over all things (36:83) ensures response.

**A Verse That Shows Divine Economy**

The verse shows divine economy: God does not waste means. When a single blast suffices, He does not send hosts. This economy appears throughout Yā Sīn. Dead earth is revived with water, not with elaborate machinery (36:33). Night is stripped of day, not demolished (36:37). Sun runs for fixed term by design (36:38). Each act is efficient and sufficient. The destruction of a town by a single cry is of same type: minimal means, maximal effect, because means are backed by divine command *“Be, and it is”* (36:82). The verse thus teaches that power is not measured by size of army but by authority of command. One command, Be, creates heavens and earth; one blast, a cry, destroys a town. The town that threatened painful punishment from itself (36:18) needed stones and numbers; God needs only a sound. The contrast is intentional and humbling. It tells listeners that true might is not in numbers but in permission from the One in Whose Hands is authority.

**The Link to Mercy and Respite**

Finally, the verse links to mercy. Not sending soldiers immediately after the believer's death is respite, allowing his people time to reflect, though they did not. The sūrah says *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44) about drowning, and same mercy applies here. God did not need to send soldiers, and He did not need to destroy immediately; He allowed a little while. That little while is mercy, giving chance to repent. The town did not take it, so a single blast came. The verse thus holds both power and mercy: power to destroy without army, mercy to delay destruction. For the reader, it is both warning and encouragement. Warning, because destruction can come without warning armies; encouragement, because delay is mercy allowing return. The believing man's entry into Paradise without army and his people's destruction without army show that God's acts, whether honoring or punishing, do not depend on human military logic, but on His own wise design.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:29

> All it took was one ˹mighty˺ blast, and they were extinguished at once

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Blast Ends a Town**

*“All it took was one mighty blast, and they were extinguished at once”* (36:29) describes the town's end. Ṣayḥah wāḥidah, a single cry, blast, shout, is the means of destruction. The same term appears for Thamūd and others: *“We sent against them a single blast, and they became like dry stubble”* (54:31). The blast is not described as fire, flood, or earthquake, but as sound, suggesting that divine command itself is sufficient. The verb khāmidūn, extinguished, is used for fire that goes out. A fire that was burning is suddenly cold, lifeless. The town that was vibrant, threatening stoning (36:18), boasting *“we will certainly stone you”* (36:18), is now extinguished at once, fa-idhā hum khāmidūn. The suddenness, at once, emphasizes that no gradual decline occurred; one moment they were alive and threatening, next they were lifeless. The verse that says *“We did not send soldiers from heavens, nor did We need to”* (36:28) is now explained: a single blast suffices.

**Why Sound as Punishment**

Sound as punishment is fitting for a people who rejected words. Messengers delivered clear words (36:17), a man from farthest end advised with words (36:20-21), yet the town responded with threats of stoning, which is also sound — shouting, collective noise. The divine response is a sound that silences all sounds. The Qur'an says *“Then they will not be able to make a last will, nor can they return to their people”* (36:50) about a future blast that will seize people while disputing. Sound that ends all disputation is appropriate. The blast is also just because it is proportionate to the town's crime of trying to silence truth with noise and stones. God silences them with a sound. The sūrah later says *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will all be brought before Us”* (36:53) for resurrection, showing that a single blast can both destroy and resurrect. One blast extinguishes, another blast brings back. Both are easy for the One who says *“Be, and it is”* (36:82).

**Extinguished as Image of Fire Going Out**

Khāmidūn, extinguished, evokes fire that was burning then goes out, leaving cold ashes. The town's life — markets, disputes, threats — was like fire, active, hot. After blast, it is cold, silent. The image appears elsewhere: *“They were extinguished”* for other destroyed peoples. The choice of fire imagery is apt because the town's punishment in Hereafter will be fire: *“This is Hell you were warned of. Burn in it today”* (36:63-64). Being extinguished in this world as fire goes out prefigures burning in next world as fire that does not go out. The sūrah says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54), so extinguishing here is not wronging but just consequence. The town that threatened to make messengers touch painful punishment from itself (36:18) now touches punishment from God that extinguishes. The reversal is complete: those who threatened to extinguish others are extinguished.

**How This Verse Warns Mecca and All Towns**

For Mecca, which saw itself as secure, well-watered, with trade and allies, the image of a town extinguished at once by a single blast is warning. Security is not protection against divine command. The Qur'an says *“Have they not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back?”* (36:31) right after this story, linking this town to many others. The single blast that extinguished this town is one instance of a pattern: many generations destroyed, none returning (36:31). The warning is that Mecca could be next if it continues rejecting. The sūrah was revealed before Mecca's conquest, when its power seemed intact. Telling of a town extinguished at once says power can be removed instantly. The verse also warns any community that kills a sincere adviser. The believing man was killed, then honored (36:26-27); his killers were extinguished. The Qur'an says *“We did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves”* (42:30). Extinguishing is consequence of self-wronging, not arbitrary.

**The Relation to Resurrection**

The single blast that destroys is paired with single blast that resurrects. Verse twenty-nine says one blast extinguished them; verse fifty-three says *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will all be brought before Us”* (36:53). The same means — blast — has opposite effects depending on divine will. One blast ends worldly life; another begins next life. Both are easy because *“All it takes, when He wills something to be, is simply to say to it: Be! And it is”* (36:82). The sūrah's argument for resurrection uses this ease: if one blast can extinguish, one blast can bring back. The dead earth revived with grain (36:33) is similar: water, a simple means, brings life from death. The town's extinguishing is thus not only punishment but also proof that resurrection is possible. If God can make a vibrant town extinguished at once, He can make extinguished bones alive at once. The verse that seems to be about destruction is also about power that will later be used for resurrection.

**A Verse That Teaches Urgency**

Finally, this verse teaches urgency. All it took was one blast, and they were extinguished at once. No time to make bequest, no time to return to people (36:50). The sūrah says blast will seize people while they are entrenched in worldly disputes (36:49). The town's story is example of that: they were disputing with messengers, threatening stoning, when blast came. The lesson is to not be entrenched in dispute when blast comes. The believing man rushed from farthest end (36:20) and declared belief (36:25) before blast, so he was not among extinguished but among honoured. His rushing is model of urgency that saves. The verse invites reader to rush toward messengers now, before a blast — whether personal death or collective punishment or final Hour — extinguishes opportunity. The town's extinguishing at once is warning that time is short, and that following those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21) is urgent, not optional.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:30

> Oh pity, such beings! No messenger ever came to them without being mocked

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Cry of Pity for Those Who Mock**

*“Oh pity, such beings! No messenger ever came to them without being mocked”* (36:30) is a divine exclamation of sorrow and amazement. Yā ḥasratan ʿalā al-ʿibād, pity upon the servants, can be read as God's lament for what servants do to themselves, or as statement that servants will feel pity and regret on Day of Judgment when they see consequence of mocking. Both are transmitted. The verse follows the town's destruction by a single blast (36:29) and generalizes: this town is not exception; every messenger has been mocked. *“No messenger came to them but they mocked him”* (15:11, 43:7) appears as refrain. The sūrah says *“Have they not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back?”* (36:31) immediately after, linking mocking to destruction. Mockery is not harmless humor; it is rejection that leads to being extinguished. The pity is therefore not sentimental but judicial: pity that beings given signs, messengers, and mercy choose mockery and thus bring punishment upon themselves.

**Why Mockery Is the Universal Response**

Mockery, istihzāʾ, is universal response because it is easy defense against clear delivery (36:17). When messengers say *“Our Lord knows we have been sent”* (36:16) and *“Follow those who ask no reward and are rightly guided”* (36:21), the simplest way to avoid engaging is to mock. Mockery does not require argument; it requires only ridicule. The Qur'an says *“When they see you, they take you only in mockery: Is this the one God has sent as messenger?”* (25:41). Mockery protects ego: if messenger is ridiculous, his message need not be considered. The sūrah's town mocked by calling messengers liars (36:15), bad omens (36:18), and threatening stoning (36:18). Mecca mocked Muḥammad by calling him poet, magician, possessed. The verse says no messenger ever came without being mocked, so Muḥammad's experience is not new; it is pattern. That pattern consoles: if all messengers were mocked, mockery is not evidence against messenger but evidence that audience follows old habit.

**Pity From God or Pity Felt by Servants**

Exegetes debate who feels pity. Some say God expresses pity for servants who wrong themselves: alas for servants, what they do. Others say servants themselves will feel pity on Day when they see truth: alas for us, what we did. The Arabic allows both, and both are true at different times. In this life, God pities servants who mock because He knows what awaits; in next life, servants pity themselves when they see they mocked those who sought to save them. The verse's placement after a town extinguished at once (36:29) supports first reading: God says pity, such beings, after describing their end. The next verse says they never return (36:31), so pity is for those who cannot return to fix mockery. The sūrah later says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54), so pity does not cancel justice; it acknowledges that justice is painful for those who mocked. The exclamation yā ḥasratan is rare and strong, indicating deep regret, not casual observation.

**Mockery as Barrier to Seeing Signs**

Mockery creates barrier described earlier: *“We have placed a barrier before them and behind them and covered them up, so they fail to see”* (36:9). Mocking is a way of covering truth, of veiling. When a person mocks a sign, he prevents himself from seeing it as sign. The sūrah lists signs after this verse — dead earth revived (36:33), gardens and springs (36:34), pairs created (36:36), night stripped of day (36:37), sun and moon in orbits (36:38-40) — all of which are mocked if seen as mere nature. A mocker sees grain but not resurrection, night but not covering, sun but not fixed term. Mockery is thus not only response to messengers but to signs. *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46) follows same pattern. Pity is appropriate because mockers deprive themselves of signs that could guide them. The Qur'an says *“How many signs in heavens and earth they pass by, yet they turn away from them”* (12:105). Mockery is one form of turning away.

**How This Verse Connects to the Prophet's Experience**

For the Prophet, this verse is direct consolation. His people mocked him, and he grieved. The verse says pity, such beings, no messenger ever came without being mocked, telling him his experience is universal among messengers. It also tells him mockery is not final word. The town that mocked was extinguished; the believing man who was mocked was told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26). Mockery's outcome differs for mocker and mocked. The mocker is extinguished; the mocked is honored. The sūrah later says *“So do not let their words grieve you. Indeed, We fully know what they conceal and what they reveal”* (36:76). Mocking words are among those words that should not grieve, because God knows and will judge. The verse also prepares for the next: *“Have they not considered how many generations We destroyed before them?”* (36:31). Mockery leads to destruction, so those who mock now should consider those destroyed before for same reason.

**A Verse That Invites Self-Reflection on Mockery**

For the reader, this verse invites reflection on forms of mockery today. Mockery is not only laughing at messengers; it is dismissing reminders as outdated, calling sincere advisers extremist, or treating resurrection as myth. The sūrah says *“They will cry, Woe to us! Who has raised us from our place of rest? This must be what the Most Compassionate warned us of; the messengers told the truth”* (36:52) on Day of Resurrection, showing that mockery ends in acknowledgment. Pity, such beings, is said before that acknowledgment, as warning that acknowledgment will come too late if mockery persists. The verse's brevity — two clauses — mirrors the brevity of a town's existence that mocks and is extinguished at once (36:29). A community can mock for years, but its end can be a single blast. The pity is that beings given intellect, signs, and messengers choose the one response — mockery — that guarantees they will not benefit from any of them. The verse is thus both lament and warning, inviting the reader to replace mockery with following those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:31

> Have the deniers not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back to life again

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Past Destructions**

*“Have the deniers not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back to life again?”* (36:31) follows the lament over mocking (36:30) and asks those who mock now to look at those who mocked before. The verb yaʿtabir, consider, implies taking lesson, passing from outward sight to inward understanding. The Qur'an frequently asks *“Have they not traveled through the earth and seen how was the end of those before them?”* (12:109, 30:9). Ruins of past peoples were visible to Arabs on trade routes — Thamūd's dwellings in Ḥijr, ʿĀd's remains, Pharaoh's monuments. The question is not whether they know past peoples were destroyed — they do — but whether they consider what that means for themselves. The phrase who never came back, allā yarjiʿūn, means they never returned to this world, not that they will not be resurrected. Their return to worldly life is impossible; their return to God for judgment is certain (36:32). The verse thus distinguishes two returns: return to worldly life, which never happens, and return to God, which always happens.

**Why Past Destruction Matters for Present**

Past destruction matters because it shows pattern: communities that mock messengers and reject signs are destroyed, and destruction is final in worldly terms. The town just described was extinguished at once by a single blast (36:29). That town is one among many. The sūrah says *“Each of them shall be a group arraigned before Us”* (36:32) next, indicating that destroyed peoples are not gone without accounting; they will be arraigned. The question have they not considered is thus both historical and eschatological. Historically, many peoples destroyed; eschatologically, they will be brought before God. For Mecca, which felt secure in its sanctuary and trade, the question is pointed: you are not the first to feel secure, and those before you who felt secure were destroyed. The Qur'an says *“How many towns We destroyed whose people were wrongdoers, so their roofs caved in, and how many abandoned wells and lofty palaces”* (22:45). Abandoned wells and lofty palaces were visible signs of past prosperity now gone.

**Never Coming Back to This Life**

The phrase who never came back to life again, that they do not return to them, has been read in two ways. Most say it means destroyed peoples never return to this world, to their families, to their towns. Their worldly life is over. Others say it means they never return to their deniers, i.e., the destroyed peoples never come back to warn those who remain, so those who remain must learn from ruins, not from returnees. Both readings fit. The first emphasizes finality of worldly destruction; second emphasizes need to learn from signs without expecting the dead to return to warn. The sūrah later says *“They will not be able to make a last will, nor can they return to their own people”* (36:50) about those seized by a blast while disputing, echoing same finality. Death ends opportunity to return and fix. The question have they not considered is therefore urgent: consider now, before you become among those who never come back.

**The Relation to Resurrection**

The statement that destroyed peoples never came back to this life might seem to contradict resurrection, which the sūrah affirms: *“It is certainly We who resurrect the dead”* (36:12) and *“Who will give life to decayed bones?”* (36:78). The distinction is between return to worldly life, rajʿah to dunyā, which never happens, and resurrection to next life, baʿth, which will happen. The destroyed peoples will not return to their towns to continue worldly life, but they will be brought before God (36:32) and will rush from graves to Lord (36:51). The verse clarifies that worldly destruction is not followed by worldly return, so the chance to believe is in this life only. Once destroyed, they do not come back to try again. That makes present consideration crucial. The sūrah's later argument for resurrection from dead earth (36:33) shows that while return to same worldly life does not happen, revival to new life does. The earth that dies in winter does not return to same winter; it returns as spring with new grain.

**How This Verse Warns Against Feeling Secure**

The verse warns against feeling secure because many secure peoples were destroyed. The Qur'an says *“Did We not destroy the former peoples, then make the latter follow them? Thus We deal with the guilty”* (77:16-18). The pattern is that guilt, not lack of power, causes destruction. The town destroyed by a single blast (36:29) was guilty of rejecting messengers and killing a believer. Its destruction required no heavenly soldiers (36:28). The question have they not considered invites Mecca to consider that its own guilt — mocking messenger (36:30), rejecting signs (36:46), refusing to donate (36:47) — fits pattern of those destroyed. Security in sanctuary does not prevent destruction if guilt persists. The verse is also a call to travel and see ruins, which Arabs did on way to Syria. Seeing abandoned dwellings should produce consideration, not just sightseeing. The Qur'an says *“Have they not traveled through the earth and seen how was the end of those before them? They were more powerful than them”* (30:9). More powerful peoples were destroyed; power does not protect.

**A Verse That Prepares for Universal Arraignment**

This verse prepares for next: *“Yet they will all be brought before Us”* (36:32). Destroyed peoples never came back to this life, but they will all be brought before God. The two verses together hold both finality and accountability. Worldly return never happens, but divine arraignment always happens. The consideration requested — have they not considered how many peoples We destroyed — is therefore not only about past but about future. Past destruction is sign of future arraignment. Those who never came back to their towns will come before their Lord. The sūrah later says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged, nor will you be rewarded except for what you used to do”* (36:54). The perfect record (36:12) ensures that what destroyed peoples did is preserved, and being brought before God ensures they will answer. The verse invites present deniers to consider past destroyed as preview of their own future if they continue mocking, and to choose instead to follow those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21) before they become among those who never come back.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:32

> Yet they will all be brought before Us

**Expanded Commentary**

**All Will Be Arraigned**

*“Yet they will all be brought before Us”* (36:32) follows the statement that destroyed peoples never return to this life (36:31) and asserts that they will all be brought before God. Kullun, each, all, emphasizes universality. Every person, whether destroyed in past or alive now, whether believer or denier, will be gathered and presented. The verb muḥḍarūn, brought, arraigned, made present, implies being made to stand, not coming willingly. The Qur'an says *“They will all be brought before Us”* again in *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will all be brought before Us”* (36:53), linking this verse to resurrection. The first says they will all be brought; the second says how — by a single blast. Together they assert that worldly destruction is not escape from accountability. Those who never came back to their towns (36:31) will come before their Lord. The perfect record (36:12) that lists what they sent forth and left behind will be the basis of that arraignment, and *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) will be its principle.

**Why This Verse Follows Destruction**

Placing universal arraignment right after mention of past destructions corrects a possible misunderstanding: that destruction ends the story. It does not. The town extinguished at once (36:29) is not gone without accounting; its people will be brought. The many peoples destroyed before who never returned to worldly life (36:31) will be brought before God. The verse thus connects this world to next. The sūrah's structure moves from town story (36:13-29) to lament over mocking (36:30) to past destructions (36:31) to universal gathering (36:32) to signs of resurrection in dead earth (36:33). The sequence is logical: example of destruction, pattern of destruction, then statement that destruction is followed by gathering. The gathering is not limited to destroyed peoples; it includes present deniers who have not considered past (36:31). Yet in Arabic, kullun can refer to all peoples mentioned or all humanity. Most exegetes take it as all humanity, because other verses say *“We will gather them and not leave any behind”* (18:47). The sūrah's later scene of rushing from graves to Lord (36:51) illustrates this gathering.

**The Meaning of Being Brought Before Us**

Being brought before Us, ladaynā muḥḍarūn, implies presence before God, not merely gathering in a place. Ladaynā, before Us, with Us, indicates nearness to divine presence for judgment. The Qur'an says *“They will be presented before your Lord in rows: You have come to Us as We created you first time”* (18:48). Presentation is humbling because it is before the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things (36:83). The town that said *“You will be touched with painful punishment from us”* (36:18), claiming authority to punish, will itself be brought before the One whose punishment is truly painful. The believing man who was told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) was brought before God in honor; his killers will be brought in disgrace. The same verb muḥḍarūn is used for both, but outcomes differ. The verse is short, but it holds both promise and threat: all will be brought, but not all will be treated same. *“Indeed, on that Day residents of Paradise will be busy enjoying themselves”* (36:55) while others will be told *“Burn in it today for your disbelief”* (36:64).

**How This Verse Relates to the Record**

The verse that everything is listed in a perfect Record (36:12) explains how universal arraignment can be just. If all will be brought, there must be a record by which they are judged. The record contains what they sent forth and what they left behind (36:12), including traces like the town's rejection and the believer's rushing. On Day of arraignment, that record will be presented, and limbs will testify (36:65). The sūrah says *“On this Day We will seal their mouths, their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify”* (36:65), showing that being brought before God involves testimony from one's own body. The town that used hands to stone will have hands speak against it. The believing man whose feet rushed from farthest end (36:20) will have feet testify for him. Universal arraignment is thus not anonymous; it is personal, with personal record and personal witnesses.

**A Verse That Removes Escape**

This verse removes any idea of escape. Some might think that being destroyed and never returning to worldly life (36:31) means escape from judgment. The verse says yet they will all be brought before Us — no escape. Others might think that being many makes individual accountability dilute. The verse says each, kullun, will be brought, emphasizing individual presence within collective gathering. The Qur'an says *“Every soul will come with a driver and a witness”* (50:21), indicating individual escort to judgment. The sūrah later says *“They will cry, Woe to us! Who has raised us from our place of rest?”* (36:52), showing that those brought will be aware and speaking. The verse is therefore both warning and consolation. Warning for those who mock and destroy, because they will be brought; consolation for those who were mocked and killed, like the believing man, because they will also be brought, but to honor. The same gathering has two outcomes, determined by what was sent forth and left behind.

**The Link to the Signs That Follow**

The verse prepares for signs of resurrection that follow. After stating that all will be brought before God, the sūrah gives signs that resurrection is possible and reasonable: dead earth revived with grain (36:33), gardens and springs (36:34), pairs created (36:36), night and day (36:37), sun and moon (36:38-40), ships and cattle (36:41-42, 71-72). Each sign is introduced as a sign for them (36:33, 36:37, 36:41), meaning for those who will be brought. The signs are evidence that the One who can revive dead earth can bring all before Him. The sūrah's argument is cumulative: past destructions show God can destroy, universal arraignment says He will gather, natural signs show He can revive, and the final blast (36:53) shows how He will do it. Verse thirty-two is the hinge between past and future, between destruction and resurrection, stating the principle that all will be brought, which the rest of the sūrah then supports with evidence and illustrates with scenes of Paradise and Hell.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:33

> There is a sign for them in the dead earth: We give it life, producing grain from it for them to eat

**Expanded Commentary**

**Dead Earth as a Sign of Resurrection**

*“There is a sign for them in the dead earth: We give it life, producing grain from it for them to eat”* (36:33) begins the sūrah's catalogue of natural signs. After stories of destroyed towns and universal arraignment (36:31-32), the sūrah turns to creation as evidence that resurrection is possible. Dead earth, al-arḍ al-maytah, is earth that is barren, dry, lifeless, without vegetation. The Qur'an says *“You see the earth barren, but when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells and grows every delightful kind”* (22:5). The sign is that God gives it life, aḥyaynāhā, same verb used for resurrecting dead: *“It is We who resurrect the dead”* (36:12). The parallel is intentional. If God can give life to dead earth, He can give life to dead bodies. The sūrah later asks *“Who will give life to decayed bones?”* (36:78) and answers *“They will be revived by the One who produced them first time”* (36:79). Dead earth is visible proof of that answer, seen every year. The sign is for them, lahum, meaning for the deniers who doubt resurrection and for all who need reminder.

**Why Grain Specifically**

The verse says *“producing grain from it for them to eat”* (36:33). Grain, ḥabb, is staple food, essential for survival, produced from dead earth through water. The mention of eating, yaʾkulūn, connects sign to daily life. Every meal of bread is reminder of resurrection, because grain that is eaten came from earth that was dead. The Qur'an says *“Let man look at his food: We poured water in abundance, then We split the earth, then We grew grain therein”* (80:24-27). Food is not just provision but sign. The sūrah later speaks of gardens of palms and vines and springs gushing in earth so people may eat fruit not made by their hands, and then asks about gratitude (36:34-35). Eating is meant to lead to gratitude, shukr, which is acknowledgment of Giver. A person who eats grain from dead earth and does not consider resurrection or give thanks is heedless (36:6), seeing food but not sign. The verse invites to see food as sign.

**How Dead Earth Is Revived**

The revival of dead earth is described elsewhere with detail: water sent down, earth stirs, seeds split, plants grow, grain produced. The process involves divine act and natural means, but means are under divine command. The sūrah says *“We give it life”* (36:33), attributing act to God, not to nature alone. The Qur'an says *“It is God who sends winds, then they stir clouds, then We drive them to dead land and revive the earth after its death; thus is resurrection”* (35:9). The phrase thus is resurrection, kathalika al-nushūr, explicitly links reviving earth to resurrecting humans. The dead earth is not metaphor; it is model. Just as water revives earth, divine command will revive bodies. The sūrah later says *“He is the One who gives you fire from green trees, and behold, you kindle fire from them”* (36:80), another transformation of one state to opposite — green, wet tree producing fire — showing that God can produce opposite from same. Dead earth producing grain is same type of transformation.

**The Relation to the Perfect Record**

The verse connects to *“Everything is listed in a perfect Record”* (36:12) because grain that grows from dead earth is recorded as provision, and eating it is recorded as act that should lead to thanks. The sūrah says *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35) after mentioning fruit that they had no hand in making. Gratitude is expected response to sign. If gratitude is not given, ingratitude is recorded. The perfect record includes both provision and response. The dead earth sign is thus not only proof of power to resurrect but also test of gratitude. Those who eat grain and give thanks are among those who follow Reminder (36:11); those who eat and do not give thanks are among heedless. The sūrah later says *“They derive from them other benefits and drinks. Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:73) about cattle, repeating same question. Gratitude is central ethic that links eating to worship.

**Why This Sign Is Placed Here**

Placing dead earth sign after mention of universal arraignment (36:32) is strategic. All will be brought before God (36:32); is that possible? Yes, because dead earth is brought to life every year. The sign answers doubt before it is fully articulated. The sūrah later articulates doubt explicitly: *“Who will give life to decayed bones?”* (36:78), but it has already answered with dead earth, gardens, night and day, sun and moon. The placement also follows the town story. A town extinguished at once (36:29) seems final, but dead earth shows that extinguishing is not final; revival is possible. The town's people will be brought before God (36:32) just as dead earth is brought to life. The sign is therefore both consolation for believers who die — they will be revived like earth — and warning for deniers who think death ends accountability — they will be revived for judgment. The sūrah says *“Have they not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back?”* (36:31), but dead earth shows that while they never come back to worldly life, they will come back to next life.

**A Sign That Is Seen Daily Yet Missed**

Dead earth revived is seen daily, yet many miss it as sign because heedlessness (36:6) makes them see only agriculture, not resurrection. The verse says there is a sign for them in dead earth, implying sign is present but they may not see it as sign because barriers before and behind (36:9) veil them. The sūrah's purpose is to remove veil by pointing: look at dead earth, We give it life. The act of pointing — this is a sign — is itself mercy, because it teaches how to read creation. The Qur'an says *“We will show them Our signs in horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is truth”* (41:53). Dead earth is sign in horizon; grain eaten is sign within self, because it becomes part of body that will be resurrected. The verse invites reader to look at food on table and see resurrection, to eat with gratitude and awareness that the One who gave life to earth will give life to dead, and that everything is listed in perfect Record (36:12) for a Day when no soul will be wronged (36:54).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:34

> And We have placed in it gardens of palm trees and grapevines, and caused springs to gush forth in it

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gardens and Springs From Dead Earth**

This verse continues the sign of dead earth revived: *“And We have placed in it gardens of palm trees and grapevines, and caused springs to gush forth in it”* (36:34). After grain for eating (36:33), gardens and springs are mentioned as further provision and beauty. Palm trees, nakhl, and grapevines, aʿnāb, were the most valued fruits for Arabs, providing staple and luxury, shade and sweetness. Gardens of them indicate abundance, not single tree. Springs gushing, fujjarnā, indicates water emerging from earth itself, not just rain from sky, showing that life comes from within dead earth when God commands. The Qur'an says *“We made therein gardens of palms and grapevines and caused springs to gush forth”* as part of same sign. The placement — in it, fīhā — refers to earth, showing that dead earth, once revived, produces not only grain but gardens and springs. The sign is thus of transformation from barren to fruitful, from lifeless to life-giving. The same transformation will happen to decayed bones (36:78-79).

**Why Palms and Grapevines Specifically**

Palms and grapevines are specified because they were best of fruits to Arabs and because they represent different types of growth: palm is tall, perennial, provides dates, fiber, shade; grapevine is low, seasonal, provides fruit and drink. Together they cover variety. The Qur'an often mentions them together: *“Gardens of palms and grapevines”* (23:19, 2:266). Their mention also hints at Paradise, which is described as having palms, vines, and springs. The sūrah later says Paradise residents will have fruits and whatever they desire (36:57). Gardens in this world are foretaste of gardens in next. The verse thus links provision in this life to promise in next, encouraging gratitude now and hope for more later. The springs gushing in dead earth also prefigure springs in Paradise. The sign is not only proof of power to resurrect but also preview of reward for those who believe. The earth that was dead producing gardens is like a believer whose heart was dead being revived to produce good deeds.

**Springs as Sign of Hidden Power**

Springs gushing from earth show hidden power. Water is stored within earth, unseen, then caused to gush by divine command. Similarly, life is stored within dead bones, unseen, and will be caused to emerge by divine command. The Qur'an says *“We sent down water from sky in measure, then We caused it to settle in earth, and indeed We are able to take it away”* (23:18). Water's presence in earth is by measure and permission. Springs are not random; they are placed. The verse says We have placed gardens and caused springs to gush, attributing both to divine act. The sūrah emphasizes We throughout for acts only God does — We placed barriers, We resurrect, We placed gardens. The attribution teaches tawḥīd: gardens and springs are not from earth itself but from God who placed them in earth. The question that follows — *“so people might eat fruit not made by their hands, and be moved to gratitude”* (36:35) — makes explicit that fruit is not made by human hands. Gratitude is due to Placer, not to earth.

**The Relation to Gratitude**

The purpose clause in next verse — so that they may eat from its fruit — shows that gardens and springs are for eating and enjoyment, not just display. Eating is meant to lead to thanks. The sūrah repeats question of thanks twice: *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73). Repeating question indicates importance. Gratitude, shukr, is acknowledgment that provision comes from God and use of provision in obedience. Ingratitude, kufr, is opposite, covering favor and using provision in disobedience. The town that was extinguished (36:29) was ungrateful for messengers; those who do not give thanks for gardens are similar. The sign of gardens and springs is thus test: will they eat and give thanks, or eat and forget? The perfect record (36:12) records both eating and thanks or lack thereof. The sūrah says *“If you give thanks, I will surely increase you”* (14:7) elsewhere, linking thanks to increase. Gardens and springs can increase if thanks given; they can be removed if not.

**How This Verse Fits the Resurrection Argument**

Gardens and springs from dead earth strengthen resurrection argument. If dead earth can produce gardens of palms and vines and springs, then decayed bones can produce living humans. The transformation from dead to fruitful is more impressive than from fruitful to fruitful. The Qur'an says *“Look at the effects of God's mercy, how He revives earth after its death; indeed, that same One will revive the dead”* (30:50). The effects of mercy include gardens and springs. The sūrah later says *“He is the One who gives you fire from green trees, and behold, you kindle fire from them”* (36:80), another transformation of opposite from same — green, wet tree producing fire. Gardens from dead earth is same type: life from death, fruit from barren. The argument is cumulative: dead earth to grain (36:33), dead earth to gardens and springs (36:34), night to day (36:37), sun to fixed term (36:38), moon to phases (36:39). Each shows transformation that makes resurrection reasonable.

**A Sign That Is Both Provision and Beauty**

Finally, gardens and springs are both provision and beauty. Grain is provision for survival; gardens are provision plus delight, shade, beauty. The sūrah mentions both to show that God's provision is not minimal but abundant and beautiful. Paradise is described as having fruits and whatever they desire, shade, couches (36:56-57). Gardens in this world are sample. The verse invites reader to see gardens not just as agriculture but as sign of God's generosity and power to beautify what was dead. A dead earth that becomes garden is like a heedless heart that becomes mindful, producing good deeds that are beautiful. The sūrah earlier said *“You can only warn those who follow the Reminder and are in awe of Most Compassionate unseen”* (36:11), and following Reminder revives heart as water revives earth. The gardens and springs are thus outward sign of inward revival that is possible for anyone who gives thanks.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:35

> so that they may eat from its fruit, which they had no hand in making. Will they not then give thanks

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fruit Not Made by Human Hands**

*“So that they may eat from its fruit, which they had no hand in making. Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35) concludes the sign of dead earth, gardens, and springs. The purpose clause so that they may eat shows that provision is for human benefit, and the parenthetical which they had no hand in making clarifies that fruit is not human product. The Arabic wa mā ʿamilat-hu aydīhim can be read as which their hands did not make, meaning fruit is from God, not from human creation, or as and from what their hands have made, meaning they eat fruit and also what they make from it like juice, oil, etc. Both readings are transmitted, and both point to divine provision as source. Even what hands make — pressing oil, making wine, weaving palm fiber — depends on fruit that hands did not create. The verse thus removes any claim that humans produce their own sustenance independently. The Qur'an says *“Is it you who make it grow, or are We the Grower?”* (56:64) about plants. The answer is We. Eating fruit that hands did not make should lead to thanks.

**Why the Question Will They Not Give Thanks**

The rhetorical question *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35) is repeated later for cattle: *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:73). Repetition shows that gratitude is central response expected to signs. Shukr, thanks, in Qur'anic usage is not mere words but acknowledgment with heart, tongue, and limbs, using provision in obedience. The town that was extinguished (36:29) failed to give thanks for messengers; those who eat fruit without thanks fail similarly. The question is not informational but exhortative: it urges thanks. The Qur'an says *“If you give thanks, I will increase you, but if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe”* (14:7). Thanks leads to increase; ingratitude leads to loss. The sūrah's signs — dead earth revived, gardens, springs, night and day, sun and moon — are all favors that should produce thanks. The fact that many do not give thanks is part of heedlessness (36:6) and leads to barriers (36:9) and equality of warning and non-warning (36:10).

**The Relation to the Perfect Record and Gratitude**

The perfect record (36:12) that lists what they send forth and leave behind includes thanks or lack thereof. Gratitude is recorded as good deed; ingratitude as bad. The sūrah says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged, nor will you be rewarded except for what you used to do”* (36:54). Thanks is part of what you used to do. The verse also links to resurrection argument. If fruit that hands did not make can be eaten now, then bodies that hands did not make can be revived later. The One who creates fruit without human hands can create humans again without human hands. The sūrah later says *“Does man not see that We created him from a sperm-drop, then behold, he openly challenges?”* (36:77), arguing from creation without human hands to resurrection. Fruit that hands did not make is similar argument on smaller scale: daily creation without human agency proves power to create again.

**How Eating Should Lead to Thanks**

Eating is daily act that can be done heedlessly or mindfully. Heedless eating sees only taste; mindful eating sees Giver. The Prophet taught to say bismillah before eating and alhamdulillah after, making eating an act of remembrance. The verse invites that mindfulness: so that they may eat from its fruit, which they had no hand in making, will they not then give thanks? The sequence is eat, recognize, give thanks. Recognition that hands did not make fruit is key middle step. If a person thinks his hands made fruit — by planting, watering, harvesting — he may attribute provision to self and not give thanks. The verse corrects by saying which they had no hand in making, reminding that planting and watering are means, but growth is from God. The Qur'an says *“Do you see what you sow? Is it you who make it grow, or are We the Grower? If We willed, We could make it debris”* (56:63-65). The possibility of making it debris shows that fruit is not guaranteed by human effort; it is gift.

**The Connection to Gardens of Paradise**

Fruit that hands did not make in this world prefigures fruit in Paradise that will be given without toil. The sūrah says Paradise residents will have fruits and whatever they desire (36:57). In this world, fruit requires planting, watering, waiting, though growth itself is from God; in Paradise, fruit will be without toil, pure gift. Eating fruit here with thanks prepares for eating fruit there with enjoyment. The verse thus links present provision to future reward, encouraging thanks now as preparation for enjoyment later. The sūrah says residents of Paradise will be busy enjoying themselves (36:55), and part of enjoyment is fruits. Those who gave thanks for fruit in this world will enjoy fruit in next without fear of loss. Those who did not give thanks will be told *“Burn in it today for your disbelief”* (36:64), and will not have fruits. The question will they not give thanks is therefore also warning: thanks now leads to fruits later; ingratitude now leads to deprivation later.

**A Verse That Defines True Ownership**

Finally, this verse defines true ownership. Humans do not own fruit by creating it; they own it by permission to eat, which is from God. True owner is God who placed gardens and caused springs to gush (36:34). Humans are eaters, not makers. That definition humbles and liberates. It humbles because it removes arrogance of self-sufficiency; it liberates because it removes anxiety of having to create sustenance. The One who created fruit without human hands can provide without human hands. The sūrah later says *“Do they not see that We singlehandedly created for them cattle which are under their control?”* (36:71), again emphasizing singlehanded creation without human partnership. The verse invites reader to eat with awareness that fruit is gift, to give thanks with heart, tongue, and use of provision in obedience, and to see in daily eating a sign of resurrection and a preview of Paradise, where fruits will be abundant for those who gave thanks.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:36

> Glory be to the One Who created all ˹things in˺ pairs—˹be it˺ what the earth produces, their genders, or what they do not know

**Expanded Commentary**

**Glory to the Creator of Pairs**

*“Glory be to the One who created all things in pairs — be it what the earth produces, their genders, or what they do not know”* (36:36) is tasbīḥ, declaration of God's transcendence, after listing signs of dead earth, grain, gardens, springs. Subḥāna, glory be, indicates God is beyond what idolaters associate and beyond imperfection, and expresses amazement at creation. The verse says He created all things in pairs, azwāj, which can mean pairs, kinds, species. The Qur'an says *“And of all things We created pairs, that you may remember”* (51:49). Pairs include male and female, sweet and bitter, night and day, sun and moon, believer and disbeliever, this world and next. The verse specifies three categories: what earth produces — plants in pairs; their genders — humans and animals in male and female; what they do not know — pairs beyond human knowledge, perhaps in unseen realm or in creation not yet discovered. The statement is both scientific and theological: creation is paired, indicating Creator is single, not paired.

**Why Pairs Indicate Oneness**

Pairs indicate oneness of Creator because pairing implies need for counterpart, while God has no counterpart. If all things are in pairs, the One who created pairs is not Himself paired. The verse thus argues for tawḥīd from observation of duality. The sūrah earlier said *“We have placed in it gardens of palm trees and grapevines”* (36:34) — palms have male and female, vines have varieties. The sūrah later says sun and moon each in orbit (36:40) — a pair of lights. The verse generalizes: all things are in pairs, so the Creator of pairs is One. The Qur'an says *“He creates the two mates — male and female”* (53:45) and *“From every fruit He made two mates”* (13:3). Pairing is not limitation but sign. It shows that creation is relational, needing other, while Creator is independent. The verse ends with what they do not know, indicating that pairing extends beyond known, so even unknown creation is paired, and Creator remains beyond pairing. Glory be to Him who is beyond pairing yet creates pairing.

**What Earth Produces in Pairs**

What earth produces in pairs includes plants that have male and female parts, or that grow as pairs of sweet and bitter, edible and inedible, etc. Early exegetes noted that many plants have male and female, or that fruits come in pairs. Modern knowledge adds that plants have sexual reproduction with male and female organs, often in same flower or separate. The Qur'an's statement that what earth produces is in pairs was not obvious to all at time of revelation, yet it is accurate. The verse does not detail biology; it states principle that earth produce is paired. That principle invites reflection: if plants are paired, their pairing is not accidental but created. The sūrah says *“so that they may eat from its fruit, which they had no hand in making”* (36:35) — fruit from paired plants is gift, not human creation. Pairing also ensures reproduction and continuation, showing wisdom in design. A single plant alone would not reproduce; pairs allow continuation, which is mercy.

**Their Genders and What They Do Not Know**

Their genders, anfusihim, themselves, refers to humans and perhaps animals, created male and female. The Qur'an says *“O mankind, We created you from male and female”* (49:13) and *“He created you from single soul, then made from it its mate”* (39:6). Gender pairing is fundamental to human existence and continuation. What they do not know, mimmā lā yaʿlamūn, extends pairing beyond known. It can refer to unseen creatures, to forces like positive and negative, to types of creation not yet discovered, or to spiritual pairs. The phrase acknowledges limit of human knowledge while asserting principle that all creation, known and unknown, is paired. This humility — we do not know all pairs — is part of tasbīḥ. Glory be to One who knows what we do not know and creates what we do not know. The sūrah later says *“He has perfect knowledge of every created being”* (36:79), so what we do not know is known to Him.

**How This Verse Fits Resurrection Argument**

Pairs argument supports resurrection. If God created all things in pairs, then this world and next are a pair, death and life are a pair, and creation first time and second time are a pair. The Qur'an says *“And of all things We created pairs, that you may remember”* (51:49) — remember that this world has counterpart. The sūrah argues for resurrection from dead earth (36:33), from green trees giving fire (36:80), from creation of heavens and earth (36:81). Pairs adds another argument: just as there is male and female, there is first creation and second creation, which are paired. The verse's placement after grain, gardens, springs shows that those provisions are from paired creation and point to paired world. The sūrah says *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things, and to Whom you will all be returned”* (36:83) at end, which is also about pairing: authority over all things in His Hands, return to Him — two aspects of same sovereignty. Glory be in 36:36 and glory be in 36:83 form inclusio, both declaring transcendence.

**A Verse That Invites Wonder**

Finally, this verse invites wonder, which is purpose of tasbīḥ. Subḥāna is said when seeing something amazing that indicates God's transcendence. The creation of all things in pairs — what earth produces, genders, what they do not know — is amazing when considered. A single earth produces countless paired kinds, each with role, each reproducing, each beneficial. The sheer variety of pairs — sweet and sour fruits, male and female animals, night and day — shows generosity and wisdom. The verse asks reader to glorify Creator, not just acknowledge. Glorification is active response: to declare God free from imperfection and partner. The sūrah's earlier question *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35) is answered by *“Glory be to the One”* (36:36) — thanks leads to glorification. The believer who eats fruit that hands did not make (36:35) and sees pairs in creation says glory be to Creator of pairs. That glorification is itself recorded in perfect Record (36:12) and will be rewarded with fruits and whatever they desire in Paradise (36:57), where pairing continues but with perfect harmony.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:37

> There is also a sign for them in the night: We strip from it daylight, then—behold!—they are in darkness

**Expanded Commentary**

**Night as a Sign**

*“There is also a sign for them in the night: We strip from it daylight, then behold, they are in darkness”* (36:37) presents night as sign, after earth, gardens, and pairs. The sūrah says *“a sign for them”* (36:33, 36:37, 36:41) repeatedly, indicating that signs are for the audience that doubts resurrection and neglects thanks. Night is sign because it is daily transformation that resembles death and resurrection. Daylight is stripped, naslakha, verb originally used for skinning an animal — removing skin from flesh. Daylight is like skin covering night; when stripped, night appears as underlying reality, and people are in darkness, muẓlimūn. The image is vivid: day is covering that is removed, revealing darkness beneath. The Qur'an says *“He rolls night into day and day into night”* (39:5) and *“We made night and day as two signs; We erased sign of night and made sign of day visible”* (17:12). The Book states shows that light is gift, not permanent possession, and darkness is original state that returns.

**Why Stripping Is the Chosen Verb**

Naslakha, to strip, to skin, is strong verb. It implies daylight is not merely absent but removed, taken off. The removal is divine act: We strip. The sūrah attributes act to God, as with dead earth revived (36:33) and gardens placed (36:34). The stripping is daily, yet many do not see it as act because it is regular. The verse says behold, fa-idhā, then suddenly, they are in darkness, emphasizing immediacy. One moment daylight, next darkness, as if skin removed quickly. The same immediacy appears in town extinguished at once (36:29) and blast seizing while disputing (36:49). The Qur'an often uses fa-idhā to show suddenness of divine acts. Night's suddenness after stripping is sign of how quickly conditions can change, and how quickly resurrection can come. The sūrah later says *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will all be brought before Us”* (36:53), using same at once, fa-idhā, for resurrection. Night's daily at once prefigures final at once.

**Darkness as Original and Reminder**

Darkness after stripping is described as they are in darkness, muẓlimūn, meaning they enter darkness, become darkened. Darkness is not just absence of light but state people enter. The Qur'an says *“We made night for rest and day for seeing”* (10:67). Night is for rest, but also reminder of death, of grave, of need for light. The sūrah's audience, who doubt resurrection, are told to look at night: you enter darkness daily, yet you are brought out to daylight daily; similarly, you will enter darkness of death and be brought out to light of resurrection. The verse also connects to earlier barriers: *“We placed a barrier before them and behind and covered them up, so they fail to see”* (36:9). Covering, aghshaynā, and darkness, ẓulmah, are related. Those with barriers fail to see truth; those in night fail to see world. Both need light from God to see. The sūrah says *“We have not taught him poetry, nor is it fitting for him. This Book is only Reminder and clear Qur'an”* (36:69), indicating that Qur'an is light that removes darkness.

**How Night Relates to Day and Sun**

This verse prepares for sun and moon verses that follow. Scripture notes leads to *“The sun travels for its fixed term”* (36:38) and moon phases (36:39). Night and day are pair, created in pairs (36:36), each with orbit, each not overtaking other (36:40). The sūrah says *“It is not for sun to catch up with moon, nor does night outrun day. Each travelling in orbit”* (36:40). Night and day are thus ordered, not chaotic, each with term. The stripping of day from night is part of that order. The Qur'an says *“He made sun and moon subservient, each running for term appointed”* (39:5). Night as sign shows that order includes darkness as well as light, rest as well as work. The alternation is mercy: *“Out of His mercy He made for you night and day, that you may rest therein and seek His bounty”* (28:73). It is reported is mercy for rest, but also sign that rest is temporary and will be followed by awakening, just as sleep is followed by waking and death by resurrection.

**A Sign That Is Seen Yet Missed**

Night is seen by all, yet many miss it as sign because heedlessness (36:6) makes them see night as mere absence, not as stripping. The verse says there is a sign for them in night, implying sign is present but they may not see it as sign because they are in darkness, both literal and metaphorical. The sūrah says *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46). Night comes daily, yet they turn away from it as sign. The verse's purpose is to point: look, We strip daylight, behold, you are in darkness. The pointing is itself mercy, teaching how to read creation. The Qur'an says *“In creation of heavens and earth and alternation of night and day are signs for people of understanding”* (3:190). The Book states is one such alternation. For people of understanding, it is sign of God's power to remove and restore, to darken and lighten, to make dead earth alive and alive earth dead. For heedless, it is just night.

**The Link to Resurrection and Gratitude**

Finally, night as sign links to resurrection and gratitude. Resurrection is like stripping night to reveal day, or stripping day to reveal night, then restoring. The One who strips daylight and brings darkness can strip darkness and bring daylight, and can strip life and bring death, then bring life again. The sūrah asks *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) for provision. Night is provision for rest, yet many do not give thanks for it. The sūrah later says Paradise residents will be in cool shade, reclining (36:56), which is like pleasant night without darkness, rest without fear. Those who gave thanks for night and saw it as sign will enjoy shade in Paradise. Those who did not will be in darkness of Hell, told *“Burn in it today for your disbelief”* (36:64). Scripture notes is thus both sign of power and test of gratitude, inviting reader to see daily darkness as reminder of final darkness and final light, and to prepare for the Day when *“Peace will be their greeting from Merciful Lord”* (36:58) for those who saw signs.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:38

> The sun travels for its fixed term. That is the design of the Almighty, All-Knowing

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sun on a Fixed Course**

*“The sun travels for its fixed term. That is the design of the Almighty, All-Knowing”* (36:38) continues the celestial signs after night stripped of day (36:37). The sun, shams, runs, tajrī, for a fixed term, mustaqarr, which can mean dwelling place, resting place, appointed time, or fixed course. The verb tajrī implies flowing, running, like water or ship, indicating continuous movement, not static. The Qur'an says *“Sun and moon run for term appointed”* (13:2, 31:29, 35:13, 39:5). The fixed term, mustaqarr, has been interpreted as its resting place under Throne where it prostrates, as in hadith of Abū Dharr where Prophet said sun goes till it prostrates under Throne and asks permission to rise, and one day permission will be denied and it will rise from west. That hadith is recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3199 and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 159. Others interpret mustaqarr as its appointed time, its term, i.e., it runs until Day of Judgment, or its fixed course, its orbit, or its solstice points where it appears to stop then reverse. All point to same: sun's movement is not random but fixed by decree.

**Why Fixed Term Matters**

Fixed term, mustaqarr, indicates that sun's movement has limit, place, and time set by God. It does not wander; it travels to appointed destination. That is sign of design, not chance. The sūrah says *“That is the design of the Almighty, All-Knowing”* (36:38), attributing sun's course to two names: al-ʿAzīz, Almighty, who has power to set course, and al-ʿAlīm, All-Knowing, who knows its course. The pairing appears throughout Qur'an for natural phenomena: *“That is design of Almighty, All-Knowing”* for sun and moon. Might sets course, knowledge measures it. The fixed term also implies end. Sun runs for term, then term ends. That end is Day when sun and moon are brought together (75:9) and light is folded. The verse thus contains reminder of temporality. Sun that seems permanent has fixed term; so does human life. The sūrah says *“If We willed, We could have blinded their eyes”* (36:66) and *“transfigured them”* (36:67), showing that God can end terms early if He wills. Sun's term is fixed, but its daily rising is mercy allowing enjoyment for a little while (36:44).

**The Relation to Night and Day**

Sun's travel for fixed term follows night stripped of daylight (36:37) because sun is cause of day. When sun runs, day comes; when sun sets, night comes after stripping. The sūrah says *“It is not for sun to catch up with moon, nor does night outrun day. Each travelling in orbit”* (36:40), showing that sun, moon, night, day each have orbit and term, none overtaking other. The order is precise, not chaotic. The Qur'an says *“He made sun and moon subservient, each running for term appointed”* (13:2). Subservience, taskhīr, means they serve humans by providing light, heat, timekeeping. Sun's fixed term serves humans: it allows calculation of years and reckoning (10:5), determines prayer times, provides energy for grain and gardens (36:33-34). The sign is thus both proof of power and provision for gratitude. The question *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) applies to sun as well. Sun that runs for fixed term provides light without human effort, yet many do not give thanks.

**How Early Commentators Understood Mustaqarr**

Early commentators differed on mustaqarr. Some said it is sun's resting place under Throne, based on hadith of prostration. Others said it is its appointed time, i.e., Day of Resurrection when its course ends. Others said it is its fixed course, its orbit, or its solstice, where it reaches northernmost or southernmost point then reverses, appearing to stop — solstice from Latin solstitium, sun-stopping. Another reading, lā mustaqarra lahā, it has no fixed dwelling, was attributed to Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās, meaning sun has no rest, always moving. All readings agree that sun's movement is by decree and has limit. Al-Ṭabarī records both and notes that sun's prostration under Throne is not visible to humans but is true in unseen. The variety of interpretations shows that verse's wording allows multiple layers: physical course, temporal term, and metaphysical resting. The core is that sun runs, not stands, and its running is designed by Almighty, All-Knowing.

**A Sign That Supports Resurrection**

Sun's fixed term supports resurrection argument. If sun runs for fixed term and then will be folded, then human life running for fixed term and then ending is similar. The One who sets term for sun can set term for resurrection and can bring sun back after its term, or bring humans back after theirs. The sūrah later says *“Can the One who created heavens and earth not easily resurrect these deniers? Yes, He can! For He is Master Creator, All-Knowing”* (36:81). Creation of heavens and earth includes sun, and Master Creator, Khallāq, All-Knowing, includes knowledge of sun's term. If He can create sun and set its term, He can resurrect decayed bones. The verse also shows that fixed terms are not to be feared but recognized as design. Sun's term is design of Almighty, All-Knowing, so it is wise and powerful, not random. Human term is similarly designed, with wisdom and knowledge, so death is not accident but appointment, and resurrection is not impossible but designed.

**A Verse That Invites Reflection on Time**

Finally, sun's travel for fixed term invites reflection on time. Every day sun rises and sets, marking days, months, years. The Qur'an says *“He made sun a radiance and moon a light and determined for it phases, that you may know number of years and reckoning”* (10:5). Timekeeping depends on sun's fixed course. If sun had no fixed term, time could not be measured, and religious rites that depend on time — prayer, fasting, pilgrimage — could not be performed.
