Sun's fixed term is thus mercy that enables worship. The sūrah says *“We have not taught him poetry, nor is it fitting for him. This Book is only Reminder and clear Qur'an”* (36:69), and Reminder includes reminding of time's passage and fixed terms. The reader who sees sun travelling for fixed term is invited to see his own life travelling for fixed term, to give thanks for light and time, and to prepare for the Day when sun will be brought together with moon (75:9) and all will be brought before God (36:32), where *“no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) and where design of Almighty, All-Knowing will be fully manifest.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:39

> As for the moon, We have ordained ˹precise˺ phases for it, until it ends up like an old, curved palm stalk

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moon in Phases**

*“As for the moon, We have ordained precise phases for it, until it ends up like an old, curved palm stalk”* (36:39) follows sun's fixed term (36:38) with moon's measured stations. Qaddarnā, We have ordained, measured, decreed, indicates precise determination. Manāzil, phases, stations, are twenty-eight stations moon passes through in lunar month, each night in different position, allowing timekeeping. The Qur'an says *“He made sun a radiance and moon a light and determined for it phases, that you may know number of years and reckoning”* (10:5) and *“They ask you about new moons. Say, They are markers of time for people and for pilgrimage”* (2:189). Moon's phases are thus mercy for calculation and worship. The comparison to old, curved palm stalk, ʿurjūn qadīm, describes crescent at end of month: thin, curved, yellowish, like dried date stalk that held cluster then withered, curved. The image is familiar to Arabs who saw palm stalks daily. Moon that was full becomes thin like old stalk, then disappears, then returns.

**Why Moon's Phases Are a Sign**

Moon's phases are sign because they show precise ordaining and transformation. Moon does not stay full; it waxes and wanes in fixed pattern, never skipping, never random. That precision indicates design by Almighty, All-Knowing (36:38). The transformation from full to thin stalk and back shows ability to bring from one state to opposite, which supports resurrection. Dead earth to grain (36:33), night to day (36:37), sun to fixed term (36:38), moon to old stalk — all are transformations that make resurrection reasonable. The sūrah later says *“He is the One who gives you fire from green trees, and behold, you kindle fire from them”* (36:80), another transformation of opposite from same. Moon's phases also show that decrease is not end; after becoming like old stalk, moon returns to new crescent. Similarly, human decrease to decayed bones is not end; return will come. The verse thus teaches that cycles in creation point to cycle of death and life.

**The Old Palm Stalk Image**

Old, curved palm stalk, ʿurjūn qadīm, is precise image. ʿUrjūn is stalk of date cluster, which when fresh is green, thick, holding dates, then after dates removed, it dries, becomes thin, curved, yellowish-brown, like crescent moon. Qadīm, old, indicates it has been there long, withered. The comparison captures shape, color, and thinness of waning crescent. It also captures idea of something that held fruit then became empty, like moon that was full then becomes thin. The image is humble, taken from daily life, not from astronomy, making sign accessible. Arabs knew palm stalks; they could see moon and compare. The Qur'an often uses such familiar images: *“like an old palm stalk”* for moon, *“like wool fluffed”* for mountains on Day of Judgment. Familiarity makes sign clear. The moon that ends like old stalk will be renewed, just as palm produces new stalks. The cycle is ongoing, not one-time.

**How Moon's Phases Relate to Timekeeping**

Moon's phases were essential for Arabs and for Islam for timekeeping, especially for pilgrimage and fasting. *“They are markers of time for people and for pilgrimage”* (2:189). Lunar calendar determines Ramadan, Hajj, etc. Ordaining precise phases is thus mercy enabling worship. The sūrah says *“We have not taught him poetry, nor is it fitting for him. This Book is only Reminder and clear Qur'an”* (36:69), and Reminder includes reminding of times for worship through moon phases. The verse says We have ordained phases, attributing act to God, not to moon itself. Moon does not ordain its own phases; it is ordained. The sūrah emphasizes We for acts only God does — We revive earth, We placed gardens, We strip daylight, We ordained moon phases. The attribution teaches tawḥīd: moon is servant, not deity, ordained by God. Those who worship moon or sun are reminded that both are ordained, running for fixed term, not independent.

**The Connection to Sun and Night**

Moon's phases follow sun's fixed term and night stripped of day, forming celestial trio: night, sun, moon. The next verse says *“It is not for sun to catch up with moon, nor does night outrun day. Each travelling in orbit”* (36:40), showing that sun, moon, night, day each have orbit and do not overtake. Moon's phases are part of that order. Sun has fixed term, moon has phases, night and day have alternation, all designed. The order is sign of One Designer. If sun and moon were independent deities, they might clash; they do not, because One designs both. The verse also shows that decrease — moon to old stalk — is part of order, not defect. Decrease allows measurement of time and shows that creation is not static but dynamic, with appointed stages. Human life similarly has stages: *“We reverse them in development”* (36:68) for those granted long life, showing decrease in strength. Moon's decrease to old stalk mirrors human decrease in old age, both ordained.

**A Sign That Invites Gratitude and Reflection**

Finally, moon's phases invite gratitude and reflection. Gratitude because phases allow timekeeping, navigation, and beauty; reflection because phases show precise ordaining and ability to transform. The sūrah asks *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) for provision. Moon's phases are provision for timekeeping, which enables religious rites, which are provision for soul. The verse invites reader to look at moon, see old palm stalk shape at end of month, and remember that just as moon returns after becoming thin, so will humans return after becoming dust. The One who ordains phases can ordain resurrection. The sūrah later says *“Can the One who created heavens and earth not easily resurrect these deniers? Yes, He can! For He is Master Creator, All-Knowing”* (36:81). Moon is part of heavens, its phases part of creation, and its ordaining part of knowledge. Seeing moon as ordained, not random, prepares heart to see resurrection as ordained, not impossible.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:40

> It is not for the sun to catch up with the moon, nor does the night outrun the day. Each is travelling in an orbit of their own

**Expanded Commentary**

**Order in Heavens**

*“It is not for the sun to catch up with the moon, nor does the night outrun the day. Each is travelling in an orbit of their own”* (36:40) summarizes celestial order after detailing night (36:37), sun (36:38), moon (36:39). The verse denies two possibilities: sun overtaking moon, and night outrunning day. Lā yanbaghī, it is not for, it is not fitting, it does not behoove, indicates that such overtaking would be improper, contrary to design. Sun and moon have separate orbits, falak, and separate times; sun appears by day, moon by night, and they do not collide or overtake. Night and day also have order; night does not outrun day to come before its time, nor does day outrun night. The Qur'an says *“He made sun and moon subservient, each running for term appointed”* (13:2) and *“Each glides in an orbit”* (21:33). The image is of precise, non-colliding movement, like ships in sea each in course. The verse emphasizes that order is maintained by design, not chance.

**Why Sun Cannot Catch Moon**

Sun catching moon would mean both appearing together in same position, or sun's light overwhelming moon's role, or collision. In physical terms, sun and moon have different orbits and speeds; sun appears to move slower, moon faster, but they never catch in sense of one eliminating other. In Qur'anic cosmology, sun and moon are both signs, each with role, and their distinctness is preserved. Sun is radiance, moon is light, as in *“He made sun a radiance and moon a light”* (10:5). If sun caught moon, distinction would be lost, and timekeeping by moon phases would be impossible. The verse thus shows wisdom in separation. The sūrah says *“That is design of Almighty, All-Knowing”* (36:38) for sun, and moon phases are ordained (36:39); not catching is part of that design. The One who designs knows how to keep them separate. The verse also refutes those who worship sun and moon as deities that might compete; they do not compete because One orders both.

**Night Not Outrunning Day**

Nor does night outrun day, meaning night does not come before its time, nor does day. Their alternation is fixed, measured. The Qur'an says *“He rolls night into day and day into night”* (39:5), indicating smooth transition, not abrupt outrunning. The alternation allows rest and work, as in *“Out of His mercy He made for you night and day, that you may rest therein and seek His bounty”* (28:73). If night outran day, rest would overtake work prematurely; if day outran night, work would overtake rest. Balance is preserved. The sūrah earlier said night is sign where daylight is stripped and they are in darkness (36:37). That stripping happens at fixed time, not random. The verse says each travelling in orbit, kullun fī falakin yasbaḥūn, each swimming, gliding, in orbit. Yasbaḥūn, swimming, indicates smooth, continuous motion, like swimming in water, not jerky. The celestial bodies swim in space as ships swim in sea, which is next sign (36:41-42).

**Each in Orbit as Sign of Design**

Each in orbit, kullun fī falakin, is phrase that appears in *“Each glides in an orbit”* (21:33) for sun and moon. Falak, orbit, originally means round, like whirl of spindle, indicating circular course. The verse says each is in orbit of its own, indicating individual course, not shared. Sun has orbit, moon has orbit, night and day have alternation that can be seen as orbit of light and darkness. The individuality of orbits shows that each is subservient to command, not independent. The Qur'an says *“Sun and moon run for term appointed”* (13:2). Term appointed and orbit are two aspects of same design: time and space both fixed. The verse thus argues for tawḥīd: many bodies, one orderer. If there were many gods, orbits would clash; since they do not, there is One. The sūrah says *“Glory be to the One who created all things in pairs”* (36:36), and sun-moon, night-day are pairs that do not transgress bounds, unlike humans who transgress (36:19).

**How This Verse Supports Resurrection**

Order in heavens supports resurrection argument. If sun, moon, night, day each travel in orbit without overtaking, then universe is ordered, not chaotic, and its orderer can order resurrection. The sūrah later says *“Can the One who created heavens and earth not easily resurrect these deniers? Yes, He can! For He is Master Creator, All-Knowing”* (36:81). Heavens and earth include sun, moon, night, day, and their orbits. If He can create and maintain such precise orbits, He can resurrect decayed bones. The verse also shows that fixed terms and orbits end. Sun runs for fixed term (36:38), moon has phases ending like old stalk (36:39), night and day alternate until Day when sun and moon are brought together (75:9). The orbits are not eternal; they have term, after which new order comes. That new order is resurrection, when all will be brought before God (36:32, 36:53). The present order is sign that orderer can bring new order.

**A Verse That Invites Trust in Divine Order**

Finally, this verse invites trust in divine order. The reader who sees sun not catching moon and night not outrunning day daily is invited to trust that his own life, with its night and day, has order too, and that God's promise of resurrection will not be overtaken or delayed. The sūrah says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54), indicating that judgment will be as precise as celestial orbits, with no overtaking or outrunning. The verse also invites gratitude: order that allows timekeeping, rest, work, navigation, is mercy. The question *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) applies here too. Sun, moon, night, day travelling in orbits provide light, time, rest, all without human effort, yet many do not give thanks. Seeing their precise, non-colliding orbits should lead to glorification: *“Glory be to the One who created all things in pairs”* (36:36) and *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things”* (36:83). The verse is short, but it holds a complete cosmology of order, design, and trust.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:41

> Another sign for them is that We carried their ancestors ˹with Noah˺ in the fully loaded Ark

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ark as Sign for Those Who Travel**

*“Another sign for them is that We carried their ancestors with Noah in the fully loaded Ark”* (36:41) moves from celestial signs — night, sun, moon — to human history and travel. The sūrah says *“a sign for them”* (36:33, 36:37, 36:41) again, indicating this is another proof of God's power and mercy. The Ark, fulk mashḥūn, fully loaded Ark, refers to Noah's Ark, which carried believers, animals, and provision, preserving humanity. Most commentators identify ancestors as those saved with Noah, by whom all later humanity is descended. The Qur'an says *“We carried their progeny in the laden Ark”* in some readings, meaning descendants were carried as potential in loins of those saved. The Ark is sign because it shows salvation through obedience, carriage through water that drowned others, and preservation of life. The sūrah later says *“If We willed, We could drown them, then no one would respond to their cries, nor would they be rescued, except by mercy from Us”* (36:43-44), linking Ark to present ships that can also drown if God wills, but are saved by mercy.

**Why Ancestors in Ark Matters**

Carrying ancestors in Ark matters because it shows that present people exist because past people were saved. If Ark had not been carried, humanity would not continue. The verse reminds audience that their own existence is due to divine rescue of ancestors. The Qur'an says *“We carried the children of Israel in the Ark”* in some interpretations, but majority says Noah's Ark, by which one generation was preserved and thereby all humanity. The phrase fully loaded, mashḥūn, indicates Ark was filled with needed things — people, animals, food — not empty. It was prepared, loaded, then carried. Similarly, present ships are loaded and carried. The sūrah says *“and created for them similar things to ride in”* (36:42) next, linking Ark to ships, camels, and other mounts. The sign is thus of carriage: God carries humans through water, through desert, through time. The Ark carried ancestors through flood; ships carry descendants through sea; cattle carry through land. All are signs of same mercy that carries.

**How Ark Relates to Resurrection**

Ark as sign supports resurrection argument. If God could carry ancestors in Ark through water that drowned all else, He can carry humans through death to life. The flood was like death — water covering earth, destroying — and Ark was like resurrection — preservation through water, emergence to new life. The Qur'an says *“We saved him and those with him in the laden Ark, and We made them successors and drowned those who denied Our signs”* (10:73). Salvation and drowning are paired, like life and death. The sūrah's earlier sign of dead earth revived (36:33) is similar: earth drowned with water then revived with grain. Ark carried through drowning water to new earth is same pattern. The verse thus shows that God can save through what destroys, which is exactly what resurrection is: saving life through death. The sūrah later says *“Can the One who created heavens and earth not easily resurrect these deniers?”* (36:81). Creating Ark and carrying ancestors is part of that creation and carrying power.

**The Relation to Ships and Modern Transport**

The next verse says *“and created for them similar things to ride in”* (36:42), which many interpret as ships similar to Ark, or camels called ships of desert, or all beasts of burden. The Ark is prototype of all transport that carries humans. The Qur'an says *“It is He who made for you ships and cattle to ride”* (43:12). Ships and cattle both carry, both can drown or stumble, both are saved by mercy. The verse invites reflection: just as ancestors were carried in Ark by divine mercy, you are carried in ships and on animals by same mercy. If God willed, He could drown you (36:43), but He allows enjoyment for a little while (36:44) out of mercy. The sign is thus of dependence. Humans think they control ships, but carriage is by God's permission. The sūrah says *“If We willed, We could drown them”* (36:43), removing illusion of control. The Ark reminds that first carriage was purely divine rescue, not human technology, so all later carriage is also by permission.

**A Sign That Is Historical and Present**

Ark is historical sign — ancestors carried — and present sign — ships carrying now. The sūrah links past and present: ancestors carried, you are carried now in similar things. The link shows continuity of mercy. The same God who carried ancestors carries descendants. The verse also links to perfect record (36:12): carrying ancestors is recorded, and being carried now is recorded. The sūrah says *“Everything is listed in a perfect Record”* (36:12). The Ark's story is listed, and present voyages are listed. The sign invites gratitude: ancestors were carried, you are carried, will you not give thanks? The question *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) applies here too. Carriage through water is favor that should produce thanks, not heedlessness. The town that was extinguished (36:29) failed to give thanks for messengers; those who travel in ships and do not give thanks for carriage are similar. The verse warns that carriage can be withdrawn — drowning — if thanks not given, but mercy allows enjoyment for a little while (36:44) to give chance to thank.

**How This Verse Prepares for Warning About Drowning**

Finally, this verse prepares for warning about drowning (36:43-44). After mentioning Ark and similar things to ride, the sūrah says if We willed, We could drown them, with no one to respond to cries nor rescued except by mercy. The Ark story shows that drowning is possible — flood drowned all except those in Ark — and that rescue is possible — Ark saved. Present ships are same: they can drown, but mercy saves. The verse thus holds both power and mercy, like earlier pairing of Almighty, Most Merciful (36:5). The Ark is sign of both: power to drown all, mercy to save some in fully loaded Ark. The reader who travels by ship or remembers ancestors in Ark is invited to see carriage as mercy, to give thanks, and to prepare for return to God (36:83), because just as ancestors were carried through water to new earth, all will be carried through death to new life, and all will be brought before God (36:32).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:42

> and created for them similar things to ride in

**Expanded Commentary**

**Similar Things to Ride**

*“And created for them similar things to ride in”* (36:42) continues the sign of the Ark (36:41). After carrying ancestors in fully loaded Ark, God created for descendants similar things to ride: ships, camels, horses, and by extension all transport. The verse says We created for them, attributing creation of transport to God, not to human invention alone. Human ingenuity builds ships, but materials, buoyancy, wind, and guidance are from God. The phrase like thereof, mithlahu, indicates similarity to Ark in function — carrying — not necessarily in form. Many commentators take it as ships similar to Noah's Ark, which is plausible because Ark is prototype of ship. Others say camels, called ships of desert, safīnat al-barr, because they carry through desert as ships carry through sea. Others say all beasts of burden and modern vehicles, because all serve same purpose. The Qur'an says *“We made for you ships and cattle to ride”* (43:12) and *“He creates that which you do not know”* (16:8), indicating that transport includes what was known and what would be known later. The sign is thus of ongoing carriage.

**Why Creation of Transport Is a Sign**

Creation of transport is sign because it shows mercy that enables movement, trade, and seeking provision, and power that can withdraw that ability. The sūrah says next that if divine will decreed sinking, no helper would answer and no deliverance would happen except through mercy (36:43-44). Ships that carry can also drown if God wills; camels that carry can stumble; vehicles that carry can crash. Carriage is by permission, not guarantee. The verse reminds that being carried is favor, not right. The Qur'an says *“Do they not see that We singlehandedly created for them cattle which are under their control?”* (36:71) about cattle, emphasizing singlehanded creation without partner. Similar things to ride are also singlehandedly created, subjected to humans, yet humans do not control their ultimate safety. The sign invites gratitude: you are carried, will you not give thanks? The question *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) applies to transport as well.

**The Link to Ark and to Mercy**

Linking similar things to Ark shows continuity of mercy from ancestors to descendants. Ancestors carried in Ark through flood that drowned others; descendants carried in ships through sea that could drown. Both are mercy. The Ark was fully loaded, mashḥūn (36:41), indicating preparation and abundance; similar things are also loaded with people and goods. The sūrah emphasizes We for both: We carried ancestors, We created similar things. The same We that placed barriers (36:9) and resurrects dead (36:12) also carries. The verse also prepares for warning that carriage can end: drowning possible, rescue only by mercy (36:43-44). The Ark story already showed both drowning and rescue; present transport shows same duality. The reader who travels is invited to remember Ark and to see his own travel as continuation of that first rescue, and to give thanks rather than be heedless (36:6).

**How This Verse Expands the Concept of Carriage**

This verse expands carriage from sea to land and beyond. Ark carried through water; similar things carry through water and land. The Qur'an mentions ships, cattle, horses, mules, donkeys as transport, and says *“He creates that which you do not know”* (16:8), which many modern exegetes take as including later inventions like trains, cars, planes. The principle is that all that carries is created and subjected by God. The verse is short — seven words in Arabic — but its scope is broad. It says We created for them similar things to ride in, indicating that whatever humans ride, its creation and subjection are from God. The sūrah later says *“We have subjected these animals to them, so they may ride some and eat others”* (36:72), showing subjection as divine act. Subjection means animal or ship obeys, carries, does not resist beyond measure, allowing human use. That obedience is not from animal or ship itself but from God who subjected.

**A Sign That Is Seen Daily Yet Missed**

Transport is seen daily, yet many miss it as sign because heedlessness (36:6) makes them see only technology, not mercy. The verse says there is a sign for them in Ark and similar things, implying sign is present but may be missed. The sūrah says *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46). Ships and mounts come daily, yet many turn away from seeing them as signs. The verse's purpose is to point: look, We created for you similar things to ride. The pointing is mercy, teaching how to read creation. The Qur'an says *“Do they not see that We have made for them ships and cattle?”* (36:71) as question inviting seeing. Seeing transport as created and subjected leads to gratitude and to trust in One who carries, and to preparation for final carriage — being brought before God (36:32) and returned to Him (36:83). The One who carries through sea and land can carry through death to life.

**The Connection to Resurrection and Return**

Finally, carriage in Ark and similar things points to final carriage: return to God. The sūrah ends *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things, and to Whom you will all be returned”* (36:83). Return is final carriage. Just as ancestors were carried in Ark to new earth, and descendants are carried in ships to new lands, all will be carried through death to new life and returned to God. The One who creates things to ride in this world can create resurrection as ride to next world. The verse invites reader to see daily carriage as rehearsal for final return, to give thanks for being carried now, and to prepare for being returned later. The perfect record (36:12) lists carriage and thanks or ingratitude, and *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) will be based on that record. The short verse thus holds a complete theology of transport as mercy, sign, and preview of return.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:43

> If We willed, We could drown them: then no one would respond to their cries, nor would they be rescued—

**Expanded Commentary**

**Power to Drown Those Carried**

*“If We willed, We could drown them: then no one would respond to their cries, nor would they be rescued”* (36:43) follows the sign of Ark and similar things to ride (36:41-42) with warning that carriage can be withdrawn. The verse says law shaʾnā, if We willed, indicating that drowning is not inevitable but possible by divine will. The same We that carried ancestors in Ark (36:41) and created similar things to ride (36:42) can drown those who ride if He wills. The conditional shows that safety in ships is not guarantee but permission. The Qur'an says *“When they board ships, they call upon God, sincere in religion, but when He delivers them to land, they associate others”* (29:65) and *“When harm touches you at sea, those you call upon besides Him vanish, except Him”* (17:67). Drowning is moment when false gods disappear and only God remains. The verse says then no one would respond to cries, fa-lā ṣarīkha lahum, no helper to answer scream, and nor would they be rescued, wa lā hum yunqadhūn, nor saved. Both help and rescue are denied except by mercy (36:44).

**Why Cries Would Have No Responder**

Ṣarīkh, responder to cry, is one who hears scream and comes to help. The verse says no responder, meaning no one would hear or would be able to help. At sea, cries for help may not be heard; even if heard, rescuers may not be able to reach. The verse emphasizes human helplessness at sea, which is experience many know. The Qur'an says *“It is He who enables you to travel on land and sea, until when you are in ships and they sail with them with good wind and they rejoice therein, there comes a stormy wind and waves come from every place and they think they are encompassed, they call upon God”* (10:22). The call upon God at sea shows that humans recognize at that moment that no human responder suffices. The verse says no responder and no rescue, indicating total helplessness, which is true of drowning. The sūrah's earlier image of shackles (36:8) and barriers (36:9) also shows helplessness, but here helplessness is in midst of provision that was supposed to carry. The One who carries can also leave without carrier.

**The Relation to Mercy and Respite**

The next verse says *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44), showing that non-drowning is by mercy, not entitlement. The conditional if We willed shows that will to drown is restrained by mercy. The sūrah says *“That is design of Almighty, All-Knowing”* (36:38) for sun, indicating power and knowledge; here power to drown is restrained by mercy, allowing enjoyment for a little while. That little while, ḥīn, is worldly life, temporary enjoyment that is *“naught but enjoyment of delusion”* (3:185) compared to next life. The respite is mercy giving chance to repent and give thanks. The sūrah says *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) for provision. Being carried in ships without drowning is provision that should produce thanks. If thanks not given, drowning remains possible, either literally at sea or metaphorically in sins. The verse thus holds both warning and mercy: warning that drowning possible, mercy that it is not immediate.

**How This Verse Connects to Ark**

The Ark story (36:41) already showed drowning and rescue: flood drowned all except those in Ark. Present ships are same: they can drown, but mercy rescues. The verse says if We willed, We could drown them, echoing flood where God did will to drown disbelievers and did will to save believers in Ark. The difference is that flood was general; present drowning would be specific to those in ships. Both show that water, which is mercy — rain reviving dead earth (36:33), springs gushing (36:34) — can also be punishment — flood, drowning. Water is both mercy and might, like the pairing Almighty, Most Merciful (36:5). The verse invites reflection: you are carried by water that could drown you, yet you are saved by mercy. That should produce humility, not arrogance. The town that threatened stoning (36:18) thought it had power to punish; this verse says true power to drown belongs to God, and He refrains out of mercy.

**A Verse That Teaches Dependence**

This verse teaches dependence. Humans depend on ships and similar things to ride (36:42), but ships depend on God's will not to drown them. Dependence is layered: you depend on ship, ship depends on sea remaining calm, sea depends on God's will. The Qur'an says *“He it is who made sea subservient that you may eat fresh meat therefrom and bring out ornaments to wear, and you see ships plowing therein”* (16:14). Subservience of sea is by divine act; if subservience withdrawn, drowning follows. The verse says no responder and no rescue, emphasizing that at moment of drowning, no human help suffices; only divine mercy saves. That truth is known to those who have been at sea in storm; they call upon God alone. The sūrah says *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away”* (36:46), but drowning is sign that cannot be turned away from when it comes; it forces recognition. The verse is thus both warning and invitation to recognize dependence before forced to.

**The Link to Final Return**

Finally, drowning and lack of responder prefigure Day of Resurrection when *“no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) and *“hands will speak and feet testify”* (36:65). On that Day, there will also be no responder for those who denied, except mercy from God. The sūrah says *“They cannot help the pagans, even though they serve idols as dedicated guards”* (36:75) about idols, showing that false helpers will not respond. The verse about drowning is early version of that: at sea, no responder; on Day, no responder except mercy. The reader is invited to seek mercy now, while enjoyment for a little while (36:44) remains, by giving thanks for carriage and by following those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21). The One who could drown but instead grants mercy and enjoyment for a little while is Most Compassionate, whose greeting to Paradise residents will be *“Peace”* (36:58). Seeking that peace now is wise, before cry has no responder.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:44

> except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a ˹little˺ while

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mercy That Allows Enjoyment for a Little While**

*“Except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44) completes the warning about drowning (36:43). If God willed, He could drown those in ships with no responder and no rescue, except by mercy from Us, illā raḥmatan minnā, and enjoyment for a little while, wa matāʿan ilā ḥīn. The exception shows that non-drowning, safety at sea and on land, is by mercy, not entitlement. Raḥmah, mercy, is from Us, minnā, emphasizing divine source. The sūrah opened with revelation from Almighty, Most Merciful (36:5), pairing might and mercy; here might to drown is restrained by mercy to allow enjoyment. The enjoyment is for a little while, ilā ḥīn, temporary, not permanent. The Qur'an says worldly enjoyment is *“naught but enjoyment of delusion”* (3:185, 57:20) compared to Hereafter, and *“Whatever you have been given is enjoyment of worldly life, but what is with God is better and more lasting”* (28:60). Little while indicates that mercy gives time, not eternity in this world, time to repent, give thanks, and prepare for return (36:83).

**Why Mercy and Enjoyment Are Paired**

Mercy and enjoyment for a little while are paired because mercy manifests as allowing enjoyment. God could punish immediately for ingratitude, but He allows enjoyment for a little while, giving chance to thank and turn. The sūrah says *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) after provision; allowing enjoyment for a little while is opportunity to answer that question with thanks. The Qur'an says *“Let not those who disbelieve think that respite We grant them is good for them; We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin, and theirs will be humiliating punishment”* (3:178) for those who use respite to increase sin, but for those who use respite to repent, it is good. The verse thus holds both possibilities: enjoyment for a little while can be used for thanks or for heedlessness. The sūrah says *“Whenever a sign comes to them, they turn away”* (36:46), showing that many use respite to turn away. The mercy that allows enjoyment is thus test as well as gift.

**The Relation to Respite and Istidrāj**

Allowing enjoyment for a little while relates to concept of istidrāj, gradual drawing, where gifts continue while warnings are ignored, leading person to think silence is approval. The Qur'an says *“When they forgot what they were reminded of, We opened for them gates of everything, until when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly”* (6:44). Enjoyment for a little while can become istidrāj if used heedlessly. The sūrah warns about single blast seizing while disputing (36:49) and not being able to make bequest or return (36:50), showing that little while ends suddenly. The mercy that allows enjoyment is therefore not to be mistaken for endorsement. It is time to prepare, not time to be complacent. The sūrah says *“Beware of what is ahead of you and what is behind you, so you may be shown mercy”* (36:45) next, indicating that awareness of before and behind is needed to receive mercy, not just enjoyment.

**How This Verse Connects to Ark and Ships**

The Ark (36:41) and similar things to ride (36:42) are enjoyed by mercy; if God willed, He could drown, but mercy allows enjoyment for a little while. The Ark's occupants enjoyed new earth after flood for a little while that became long while for humanity; present ship travelers enjoy voyage for a little while that ends at shore. Both enjoyments are temporary and by mercy. The verse also connects to dead earth revived (36:33) and gardens and springs (36:34-35): grain, fruit, gardens are enjoyment for a little while, by mercy, not permanent. The sūrah says *“They derive from them other benefits and drinks. Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:73) about cattle, again enjoyment that should lead to thanks. The pattern is consistent: provision, enjoyment for a little while, call to thanks, warning that enjoyment ends. The reader is invited to see every enjoyment — food, gardens, transport, cattle — as mercy allowing enjoyment for a little while, and to use little while for thanks.

**A Verse That Defines Worldly Life**

This verse defines worldly life as enjoyment for a little while by mercy. It is not ultimate purpose but temporary enjoyment. The Qur'an says *“Worldly life is but play and amusement, but Hereafter is better for those who are mindful”* (6:32). Little while, ḥīn, is indefinite but short compared to eternity. The sūrah later describes Paradise residents as busy enjoying themselves (36:55), having fruits and whatever they desire (36:57), greeted with peace from Merciful Lord (36:58) — enjoyment not for little while but forever. The contrast is intentional: worldly enjoyment for little while by mercy, otherworldly enjoyment forever by mercy. The first is test, second is reward. The verse invites reader to not mistake first for second, to not cling to little while as if permanent, but to use it to seek permanent. The sūrah says *“So do not let their words grieve you. Indeed, We fully know what they conceal and reveal”* (36:76), indicating that worldly words and enjoyments are temporary and known to God, while perfect record (36:12) preserves response.

**The Link to Return and Authority**

Finally, enjoyment for a little while ends with return to God (36:83), when authority over all things will be manifest. The sūrah ends *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things, and to Whom you will all be returned”* (36:83). Return ends little while. The mercy that allows enjoyment for a little while is from the One to Whom return is, so using little while to prepare for return is wise. The verse is short, but it holds theology of time: worldly time is little while by mercy, given to prepare for eternal. Those who use little while to give thanks, follow Reminder (36:11), follow those who ask no reward (36:21), worship Originator (36:22), will find that mercy that allowed little while also allows entry into Paradise where enjoyment is not little but abundant and where greeting is peace from Merciful Lord (36:58). Those who use little while heedlessly will find that little while ends with single blast (36:49) and no chance to make bequest (36:50).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:45

> ˹Still they turn away˺ when it is said to them, “Beware of what is ahead of you ˹in the Hereafter˺ and what is behind you ˹of destroyed nations˺ so you may be shown mercy.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Call to Beware of Before and Behind**

*“Still they turn away when it is said to them, Beware of what is ahead of you in the Hereafter and what is behind you of destroyed nations so you may be shown mercy”* (36:45) describes response to warning. The translation adds still they turn away, which is implied from next verse (36:46) where turning away is explicit. The call is ittaqū, be mindful, beware, fear, protect yourselves, of what is before you and behind you. Before you, mā bayna aydīkum, can mean Hereafter that is ahead, or punishment that is coming, or what is in front in space. Behind you, mā khalfakum, can mean this world that is behind, or destroyed nations behind in time, or sins committed. Exegetes give several readings: before is Hereafter to come, behind is worldly life that deludes; before is what is apparent, behind is hidden; before is sins to come, behind is sins past. All point to same: be mindful of past and future, visible and hidden, so you may receive mercy. The sūrah says *“Have they not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back?”* (36:31) — behind is destroyed nations. It says *“They will all be brought before Us”* (36:32) — before is Hereafter.

**Why Before and Behind Are Paired**

Before and behind are paired to indicate totality. A person should be mindful of what is ahead — Hereafter, judgment, return to God (36:83) — and what is behind — world, destroyed nations, past sins — so that he is aware in both directions. The Qur'an says *“Have they not considered what is before them and behind them of heaven and earth? If We willed, We could cause earth to swallow them”* (34:9). Awareness of before and behind produces humility and mercy-seeking. The sūrah says *“so you may be shown mercy”* (36:45), laʿallakum turḥamūn, indicating that mindfulness of before and behind leads to mercy. The town that was extinguished (36:29) was not mindful of before and behind; it threatened messengers without considering past destroyed or future judgment. The believing man was mindful — he worshiped Originator (36:22) and remembered return (36:22) — and was shown mercy: *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) and forgiveness and honor (36:27).

**Turning Away Despite Call to Mercy**

The verse says still they turn away when told to be mindful so they may receive mercy. Turning away, iʿrāḍ, is refusal to face, to consider. The sūrah says next *“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46), showing that turning away is habitual. The call to be mindful of before and behind is sign, and they turn away from it. The Qur'an says *“Who does greater wrong than one who is reminded of signs of his Lord then turns away from them?”* (18:57, 32:22). Turning away is greater wrong because it rejects mercy that is offered. The verse says so you may be shown mercy, indicating that mercy is available if mindfulness is practiced, but they turn away, so mercy is not received. The sūrah earlier said *“We have placed a barrier before them and behind them and covered them up, so they fail to see”* (36:9). Barriers before and behind prevent seeing before and behind. The call to beware of before and behind is attempt to remove barriers, but they turn away, keeping barriers.

**How This Verse Relates to Respite and Enjoyment**

This verse follows *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44), which is mercy giving time. The call to beware of before and behind is use of that time. Enjoyment for little while is time to be mindful of before and behind so mercy may be shown. If time is used to turn away, mercy is not received and little while ends with blast seizing while disputing (36:49). The sūrah says *“They must be awaiting a single Blast, which will seize them while they are entrenched in worldly disputes”* (36:49). Those entrenched in disputes are not mindful of before and behind; they are absorbed in present dispute. The call to beware of before and behind is call to lift gaze from present dispute to past destroyed and future Hereafter. The believing man did that: he remembered origination and return (36:22) and was mindful of before and behind, so he was shown mercy.

**The Meaning of Mercy Here**

Mercy, raḥmah, that may be shown if mindful, is same mercy that allows enjoyment for little while (36:44) and that greets Paradise residents with peace (36:58). Mercy is both respite now and reward later. The verse says so you may be shown mercy, implying that mindfulness is condition for receiving mercy, though mercy itself is gift. The Qur'an says *“My mercy encompasses all things, so I will decree it for those who are mindful”* (7:156). Mindfulness of before and behind is part of being mindful. The town that killed believer was not mindful, so it was not shown mercy but extinguished (36:29). The believer who was mindful was shown mercy: forgiveness and honor (36:27). The verse is thus both warning and invitation: be mindful of what is ahead and behind, so mercy may be shown. The invitation is open, but response described is turning away, which is why next verse says whenever a sign comes, they turn away (36:46).

**A Verse That Defines Mindfulness**

Finally, this verse defines mindfulness as awareness of before and behind. Mindfulness is not only ritual but temporal awareness: remembering past destroyed nations as lesson, anticipating Hereafter as destination, and living present in light of both. The sūrah says *“It is certainly We who resurrect the dead, and write what they send forth and leave behind”* (36:12), indicating that what is before and behind is recorded. Being mindful of before and behind is being mindful of record. The perfect record contains past deeds and future recompense. The call to beware is call to live as if record is being written and will be read when all are brought before God (36:32). The verse invites reader to consider what is before — Hereafter, judgment, Paradise and Hell described later (36:55-64) — and what is behind — destroyed peoples, personal sins, worldly delusions — and to seek mercy now while enjoyment for little while remains. Those who turn away will have no responder when drowned (36:43) and no bequest when blast seizes (36:50); those who are mindful will have forgiveness and honorable reward (36:11).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:46

> Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it

**Expanded Commentary**

**Habitual Turning Away From Signs**

*“Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it”* (36:46) generalizes the response described in previous verse. The sūrah has listed many signs — dead earth revived (36:33), gardens and springs (36:34), pairs created (36:36), night stripped of day (36:37), sun for fixed term (36:38), moon phases (36:39), sun and moon not overtaking (36:40), Ark and similar things to ride (36:41-42), power to drown and mercy to allow enjoyment (36:43-44) — and says whenever a sign comes, they turn away. The Arabic kullamā, whenever, indicates repetition, habit. It is not that they turned away once, but whenever sign comes, they turn away. The Qur'an says similar in *“Never did a sign come to them from signs of their Lord but they turned away from it”* (6:4), which is almost identical. Turning away, iʿrāḍ, is not inability to see but refusal to face. The sūrah earlier described barriers before and behind and covering (36:9) that cause failure to see, but here turning away is active choice that reinforces barriers. The verse shows that signs are abundant, but response is consistent turning away, which is why they remain heedless (36:6) and why decree is justified (36:7).

**Why Signs Are Turned Away From**

Signs are turned away from because they demand change. Dead earth revived demands belief in resurrection; gardens and springs demand thanks; night and sun and moon demand recognition of design; Ark and ships demand gratitude for carriage. Each sign demands response — belief, thanks, mindfulness — that heedless do not want to give. Turning away avoids demand. The Qur'an says *“They know outward aspect of worldly life, but of Hereafter they are heedless”* (30:7). They know outward — grain, gardens, sun, ships — but turn away from inward — sign-character. The sūrah says *“We have placed a barrier before them and behind and covered them, so they fail to see the truth”* (36:9). Turning away is both cause and effect of barrier: they turn away, so barrier placed; barrier placed, so they fail to see and turn away more. The cycle deepens. The verse says from their Lord, min āyāti rabbihim, indicating signs come from Lord who is also Most Merciful (36:5) and who allows enjoyment for little while (36:44) out of mercy, so turning away from signs is turning away from Lord's mercy.

**The Relation to Previous Call to Beware**

This verse follows call to beware of before and behind so mercy may be shown (36:45). The call to beware is itself a sign, and they turn away from it. The sūrah says still they turn away when told to beware (36:45), then whenever a sign comes they turn away (36:46), showing that turning away is response to both warning and sign. The pattern is consistent: warning given, sign shown, turning away follows. The town that rejected messengers (36:13-18) turned away from clear delivery (36:17); Mecca that mocked messenger (36:30) turned away from wise Qur'an (36:2). The verse universalizes: whenever sign comes, they turn away, indicating that problem is not lack of signs but attitude to signs. The Qur'an says *“How many signs in heavens and earth they pass by, yet they turn away from them”* (12:105). Passing by and turning away is image of traveler who sees signpost but ignores it, continuing wrong road.

**How Turning Away Leads to Other Sins**

Turning away from signs leads to other sins described next: refusing to donate (36:47) and mocking promise of resurrection (36:48). If signs are turned away from, then gratitude for provision — which should lead to donating — is not felt, and warning about Hereafter — which should lead to preparation — is mocked. The sūrah's sequence is logical: signs shown (36:33-44), call to be mindful of before and behind (36:45), but they turn away whenever sign comes (36:46), so when told to donate from what God provided, they say why should we feed whom God could feed (36:47), and when told about threat of Hour, they say when will this threat come (36:48). Turning away from signs is root; refusal to give and mocking promise are fruits. The verse thus diagnoses root cause of later sins. The perfect record (36:12) records both root and fruits, and *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) will be based on that record.

**A Verse That Defines Disbelief as Avoidance**

This verse defines disbelief not as lack of evidence but as avoidance of evidence. Evidence is abundant — dead earth, gardens, night, sun, moon, Ark, ships — yet they turn away whenever it comes. The Qur'an says *“Even if We opened for them a gate in heaven and they continued to ascend through it, they would say Our eyes are dazzled, rather we are bewitched”* (15:14-15), showing that even extraordinary sign would be turned away from by those habituated to turning away. The sūrah says *“It is the same whether you warn them or not, they will never believe”* (36:10) for those who have become such that warning does not benefit. Turning away whenever sign comes is how a person becomes such. Each turning away hardens heart a little, until barriers before and behind (36:9) are complete and warning makes no difference (36:10). The verse is therefore warning to not turn away from first sign, lest habit form.

**The Link to Mercy and to Final Scenes**

Finally, turning away from signs prevents receiving mercy that is offered. The previous verse said be mindful so you may be shown mercy (36:45); this verse says whenever sign comes they turn away, so mercy not received. The sūrah later describes Paradise residents who did not turn away but followed Reminder and feared Most Compassionate unseen (36:11), and who will be busy enjoying, reclining, having fruits, greeted with peace from Merciful Lord (36:55-58). Those who turned away will be told *“Step away this Day, O wicked ones”* (36:59), *“Did I not command you not to follow Satan?”* (36:60), *“This is Hell you were warned of”* (36:63), *“Burn in it today”* (36:64). The two outcomes stem from two responses to signs: following and turning away. The verse invites reader to examine own response to daily signs — grain, gardens, night, sun, moon, ships — and to not turn away whenever they come, but to see them as from Lord, give thanks, be mindful of before and behind, and seek mercy while enjoyment for little while remains (36:44).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:47

> And when it is said to them, “Donate from what Allah has provided for you,” the disbelievers say to the believers, “Why should we feed those whom Allah could have fed if He wanted to? You are clearly astray!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Refusal to Donate and Its Justification**

*“And when it is said to them, Donate from what Allah has provided for you, the disbelievers say to the believers, Why should we feed those whom Allah could have fed if He wanted to? You are clearly astray”* (36:47) describes response to call to charity. After turning away from signs (36:46), they are told to donate from what Allah provided — grain, gardens, springs, ships, cattle — all provision mentioned earlier. Their response is to question why they should feed those whom Allah could feed if He wanted, implying that poverty is God's will, so feeding poor opposes divine will. This argument is recorded as coming from Quraysh to Abū Bakr, who used to feed destitute Muslims, and they said if God can feed them, why does He not? He replied some are tried with poverty, others with wealth; poor commanded to patience, wealthy to give. They called him clearly astray. The argument is sophistry that uses divine power to excuse human stinginess. The Qur'an says *“Whatever you have been given is provision from God”* and donation is test of gratitude.

**Why This Argument Is Flawed**

The argument is flawed because it confuses divine ability with human responsibility. God can feed poor directly, but He has made feeding poor a means for wealthy to show gratitude and for poor to be tested with patience. The Qur'an says *“We have made some of you a trial for others, will you be patient?”* (25:20). Wealth and poverty are both trials. The wealthy are tried with whether they give; poor with whether they are patient and grateful. Saying God could feed if He wanted ignores that God wants wealthy to feed as act of worship. The sūrah earlier said *“so that they may eat from its fruit, which they had no hand in making. Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35) and *“They derive from them other benefits and drinks. Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:73) about cattle. Thanks includes donating from provision. Refusing to donate is ingratitude. The disbelievers' argument also contradicts their own belief: they say Allah could feed if He wanted, acknowledging divine power, yet they do not worship Him alone but take other gods hoping to be helped (36:74).

**You Are Clearly Astray as Reversal**

They say to believers *“You are clearly astray”* (36:47), using same phrase believing man used for himself if he took other gods: *“Indeed, I would then be clearly astray”* (36:24). The disbelievers reverse label, calling believers astray for asking to feed poor. This reversal is common: those who are astray call guided astray. The sūrah says *“Yet he already misled great multitudes of you. Did you not have any sense?”* (36:62) about Satan misleading. Those misled think they are guided and call guided astray. The Qur'an says *“When it is said to them, Do not cause corruption on earth, they say We are only reformers. Unquestionably, it is they who are corrupters, but they perceive not”* (2:11-12). Calling believers clearly astray for enjoining charity shows corruption of values. Charity is not astray; it is straight path. The sūrah says worshiping God alone is straight path (36:61), and charity is part of worship.

**The Relation to Shackles and Barriers**

Refusal to donate relates to earlier image of shackles around necks up to chins (36:8), which prevents extending hands to give. Hands shackled to necks cannot donate. The disbelievers' argument — why should we feed those whom Allah could feed — is intellectual shackle that binds hands from giving. The sūrah says *“Do not shackle your hand to your neck”* (17:29) as warning against miserliness. The disbelievers have shackled hands by reasoning that excuses miserliness. The perfect record (36:12) records both provision and refusal to donate, and *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) will be based on that. The sūrah later says *“They cannot help the pagans, even though they serve idols as dedicated guards”* (36:75), showing that those who refuse to help poor end up serving idols that cannot help them. Refusal to help leads to serving what cannot help.

**How This Verse Connects to Mercy and Enjoyment**

This verse follows *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44) and call to beware of before and behind so mercy may be shown (36:45). Donating from what Allah provided is way to show gratitude for enjoyment for little while and to seek mercy. Refusing to donate is using enjoyment heedlessly, which leads to being among those who turn away from signs (36:46). The sūrah says *“Will they not then give thanks?”* (36:35, 36:73) after provision; donating is thanks. The disbelievers' refusal is ingratitude. The verse also shows that disbelievers understand provision comes from Allah — they say from what Allah has provided — yet they refuse to share. Acknowledging provision without sharing is incomplete thanks. The believing man who rushed from farthest end (36:20) gave his life, not just wealth, showing complete thanks. His reward was forgiveness and honor (36:27). Those who refuse to give will be told *“Burn in it today for your disbelief”* (36:64), showing that ingratitude has consequence.

**A Verse That Defines True Guidance in Wealth**

Finally, this verse defines true guidance in wealth: donate from what Allah provided. True guidance is not to question why God does not feed poor directly, but to feed them as God commanded, recognizing that wealth is test and provision is from God. The Qur'an says *“And in their wealth there is right for the beggar and deprived”* (51:19). Right, ḥaqq, indicates that poor have right in wealth of rich, not that rich give charity as favor. Refusing to give violates right. The verse invites reader to see wealth as provision from Allah, to donate from it, and to not be among those who say why should we feed those whom Allah could have fed, for that argument is clearly astray, as believing man said about taking other gods (36:24). The straight path (36:61) includes giving thanks through donating, and following those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21) includes following their generosity. The short verse thus holds complete ethic of wealth as provision to be shared, not hoarded, and warns that refusing to share is sign of being clearly astray.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:48

> And they ask ˹the believers˺, “When will this threat come to pass, if what you say is true?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mocking Question About When Threat Will Come**

*“And they ask the believers, When will this threat come to pass, if what you say is true?”* (36:48) records another response of disbelievers after refusing to donate (36:47). They ask about threat, waʿd, promise, which includes Resurrection, Hour, punishment. The question is not sincere inquiry about timing but mockery: when will this threat you warn about happen, if you are truthful? Similar questions appear throughout Qur'an: *“When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?”* (10:48, 21:38, 27:71, 34:29, 67:25). The disbelievers expect prophet to know exact time of Hour, thinking that if he does not know, he is not truthful. The Qur'an answers that knowledge of Hour is with God alone: *“They ask you about the Hour, when it will occur. Say, Knowledge of it is only with my Lord. None will reveal its time except Him”* (7:187). The question is thus based on false expectation that messenger must know future. Messenger's duty is only clear delivery (36:17), not knowledge of unseen timing.

**Why Threat Is Mocked**

Threat is mocked because it is unseen and delayed. The sūrah says *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44), indicating that punishment is delayed out of mercy, giving time. Delay makes mockers think threat will never come. The Qur'an says *“Those who do not believe in it seek to hasten it, but those who believe are fearful of it and know it is truth”* (42:18). Disbelievers hasten threat as mockery; believers fear it as truth. The town that was extinguished at once by single blast (36:29) also mocked before blast; blast came while they disputed (36:49). Mocking question when will threat come is asked while enjoying little while, not realizing little while ends suddenly. The sūrah says *“What awaits them is a single mighty blast seizing them while absorbed in disputes”* (36:49) next, answering their question: threat will come as single blast seizing while disputing, not at time they choose.

**If What You Say Is True as Condition**

The condition *“if what you say is true”* (36:48) implies doubt about truthfulness of believers and messengers. They call messengers liars (36:15) and believers clearly astray (36:47), so they doubt threat. The condition is rhetorical: they do not actually think believers are truthful; they challenge them to prove truthfulness by producing threat. The Qur'an says *“They say, When will this promise be, if you are truthful? Say, You have appointment for a Day which you cannot delay by an hour nor advance”* (34:29-30). The appointment is fixed, not subject to human hastening or delay. The disbelievers' question assumes that truthfulness requires ability to produce threat on demand, which is false. Truthfulness is about message content, not timing control. The sūrah says *“Our Lord knows that we have truly been sent to you”* (36:16) — truthfulness is known to God, not dependent on producing threat when asked.

**How This Verse Relates to Previous Refusal to Donate**

This verse follows refusal to donate (36:47) because both are forms of turning away from signs (36:46). Refusing to donate turns away from sign of provision; asking when threat will come turns away from sign of warning. Both show heedlessness (36:6) and barriers (36:9). The sūrah's sequence shows progression: signs shown (36:33-44), call to be mindful (36:45), turning away from signs (36:46), refusal to donate (36:47), mocking threat (36:48), then blast seizing while disputing (36:49). The progression is from ignoring provision to mocking promise to being seized. The perfect record (36:12) records each step, and *“On that Day no soul will be wronged”* (36:54) will be based on that. The verse also shows that disbelievers address believers, not just messengers, asking when threat will come. Believers are asked because they also warn, following messengers who ask no reward (36:21). Mocking believers is extension of mocking messengers.

**The Answer That Follows: Single Blast**

The next verse answers their question: *“What awaits them is a single mighty blast seizing them while absorbed in disputes”* (36:49). The answer is not date but description: threat will come as single blast seizing while disputing, without time to make bequest or return (36:50). That is how Hour comes — suddenly, while people are heedless. The Qur'an says *“It will not come upon you except suddenly”* (7:187). The disbelievers ask when, expecting to prepare; answer is it will come while they are disputing, so preparation must be now, not later. The sūrah later says *“The Trumpet will be blown a second time, then behold, they will rush from graves to their Lord”* (36:51), showing that threat includes resurrection blast as well. The single blast that seizes while disputing (first blast) and trumpet that raises from graves (second blast) are two threats that answer their question.

**A Verse That Defines Proper Attitude to Unseen Threat**

Finally, this verse defines proper attitude to unseen threat: belief, fear, preparation, not mocking question about timing. The Qur'an says *“Those who believe are fearful of it and know it is truth”* (42:18). Proper attitude is to fear threat, know it is truth, and prepare by following Reminder (36:11), donating from provision (36:47), worshiping Originator (36:22), and not taking other gods (36:23). Mocking question when will threat come if you are truthful is improper because it shifts focus from preparation to timing, which is with God alone. The verse invites reader to not ask when threat will come, but to ask whether he is prepared when it comes, because it will come as single blast seizing while disputing, with no time to make bequest (36:50). The believing man who rushed from farthest end (36:20) and declared belief (36:25) was prepared, so he was told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) before blast extinguished his people (36:29). His preparation saved him; their mocking question did not.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:49

> They must be awaiting a single Blast, which will seize them while they are ˹entrenched˺ in ˹worldly˺ disputes

**Expanded Commentary**

**Single Blast Seizing While Disputing**

*“They must be awaiting a single Blast, which will seize them while they are entrenched in worldly disputes”* (36:49) answers the mocking question when will threat come (36:48). The answer is not date but description: threat will come as single blast, ṣayḥah wāḥidah, seizing them while they dispute, yakhiṣṣimūn, entrenched in worldly disputes. The same phrase single blast appears for town extinguished at once (36:29) and for resurrection when all will be brought before Us (36:53). One blast can destroy, another can resurrect. The blast here is first blast, ṣaʿqah, that ends worldly life, seizing people unexpectedly. The Book states *“It will not come upon you except suddenly”* (7:187) about Hour, and *“The Hour will come upon them suddenly while they perceive not”* (12:107). Seizing while disputing shows suddenness and heedlessness: they are disputing about provision, threat, messengers, while blast comes. The sūrah says *“Then no final will can be made nor return to family possible”* (36:50) next, showing that blast leaves no time.

**Why Disputes Are Mentioned**

Disputes, khusūmah, worldly disputes, indicate absorption in present argument — about wealth, status, provision, threat — that distracts from preparation. The disbelievers who said *“Why should we feed those whom Allah could have fed?”* (36:47) and *“When will this threat come?”* (36:48) are examples of disputing. They dispute about donating and about timing of threat, while blast awaits. Scripture notes *“They await not but a single blast that will seize them while they dispute”* (36:49), implying that disputing is what they are doing when seized, so disputing does not prevent blast; it is context in which blast comes. The believing man rushed from farthest end (36:20) and declared belief (36:25) instead of disputing, so he was not among those seized while disputing; he was told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26) before. The verse thus contrasts two ways of spending little while allowed by mercy (36:44): disputing or rushing to truth. One leads to being seized, other to being honored.

**Single Blast as Divine Power**

Single blast shows divine power that does not need armies (36:28). One blast extinguished town (36:29); one blast will seize all while disputing (36:49); one blast will bring all before God (36:53). The same means — sound, cry — has different effects by divine will. It is reported *“All it takes, when He wills something to be, is simply to say to it: Be! And it is”* (36:82). Blast is like Be — command that creates effect instantly. The town's threat *“we will certainly stone you and you will be touched with painful punishment from us”* (36:18) required many stones and many people; God's blast requires one sound. The disproportion shows true power. The sūrah says *“If We willed, We could drown them”* (36:43) and *“Had We willed, We could have blinded their eyes”* (36:66) and *“transfigured them on the spot”* (36:67), all showing that God can punish in many ways, but chooses single blast as means that is sudden and all-encompassing.

**How This Verse Warns Against Procrastination**

This verse warns against procrastination. Disputing while awaiting blast is procrastination, thinking threat is distant, so disputing can continue. The verse says they must be awaiting single blast, mā yanẓurūna illā ṣayḥatan wāḥidah, they await not but single blast, meaning blast is what is actually coming, not what they dispute about. They think they await answer to when threat will come; in fact they await blast itself. The Qur'an says *“Do they await except that angels come to them or your Lord comes or some signs come? On Day when some signs come, belief will not benefit soul that did not believe before”* (6:158). Awaiting without preparing is futile. The verse invites to prepare now, before blast seizes while disputing, because after seizing, no bequest can be made nor return to people (36:50). The believing man's rushing (36:20) is model of not awaiting but acting.

**The Link to Resurrection Blast**

Single blast that seizes while disputing is first blast; trumpet blown second time that makes them rush from graves to Lord (36:51) is second blast. The two blasts are paired: first ends worldly life, second begins next life. The sūrah says *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will all be brought before Us”* (36:53) for second. Both are single, both at once, both bring all before God. The first blast answers question when will threat come — it will come suddenly while disputing; second blast answers doubt about resurrection — *“Who will give life to decayed bones?”* (36:78) — it will come as blast bringing from graves. The two blasts thus frame worldly life and next life. The verse about blast seizing while disputing is warning that little while of enjoyment (36:44) ends with blast, so use little while to give thanks, donate, follow Reminder, worship Originator, before blast.

**A Verse That Defines Urgency**

Finally, this verse defines urgency. They must be awaiting single blast seizing while disputing — that is what is actually coming, so urgency is needed. The Qur'an says *“The Hour is near”* (54:1) and *“What will make you know? Perhaps Hour is near”* (42:17). Nearness is not measured by years but by suddenness: it can seize while disputing, without warning. The verse invites reader to not be among those entrenched in worldly disputes when blast comes, but among those who follow those who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21), who worship Originator (36:22), who declare belief (36:25), and who are told *“Enter Paradise”* (36:26). The believing man's story shows that one who rushes from farthest end and declares belief is not seized while disputing but honored. The verse is short, but it holds complete warning: single blast awaits, it seizes while disputing, so stop disputing and start following, before no bequest can be made (36:50).
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:50

> Then they will not be able to make a ˹last˺ will, nor can they return to their own people

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Time for Bequest or Return**

*“Then they will not be able to make a last will, nor can they return to their own people”* (36:50) continues description of single blast seizing while disputing (36:49). The blast is so sudden that those seized cannot make bequest, tawṣiyah, last will, advice, or disposition of affairs, nor return to their people, ahl, family. The verse shows totality of seizure: no time for worldly arrangement, no time for farewell. The Qur'an says *“When death comes to one of them, he says My Lord, send me back, that I may do righteous deeds in what I left behind”* (23:99-100), but return is denied. The blast that seizes while disputing leaves no time for such request; even bequest, which is minimal act, cannot be made. The sūrah earlier said *“Have they not considered how many peoples We destroyed before them who never came back?”* (36:31) — no return to worldly life. This verse adds no bequest either, showing that worldly ties are cut instantly. The perfect record (36:12) that lists what they sent forth and left behind will be what remains; no new bequest can add to it after blast.

**Why Bequest and Return Are Mentioned**

Bequest, waṣiyyah, is final act of disposing wealth, advising family, seeking forgiveness. Return to people, rajʿ ilā ahl, is final act of rejoining family, saying farewell, fixing relations. Both are things people think they will have time for at end. The verse says they will not, because blast seizes suddenly while disputing, not while preparing. The Qur'an says *“Death will seize you wherever you are, even if you are in lofty towers”* (4:78) and *“When their term comes, they cannot delay by an hour nor advance”* (16:61). The blast is term that cannot be delayed. The sūrah says *“They must be awaiting a single Blast, which will seize them while they are entrenched in worldly disputes”* (36:49) — disputing, not preparing, so no bequest, no return. The believing man who rushed from farthest end (36:20) and declared belief (36:25) made his bequest in advance — follow messengers (36:20-21) — and his return was to Paradise (36:26), not to people. He prepared before blast, so his bequest remains.

**The Relation to Worldly Disputes**

Being unable to make bequest or return is consequence of being entrenched in worldly disputes (36:49). Those absorbed in disputes about provision (36:47) and timing of threat (36:48) think they have time to continue disputing, but blast ends disputing and leaves no time for bequest or return. The verse thus warns against absorption in disputes that distract from preparation. The Qur'an says *“Let not worldly life delude you”* (35:5). Disputing about worldly matters is delusion that there is time. The sūrah says *“except by mercy from Us, allowing them enjoyment for a little while”* (36:44) — little while is time to prepare bequest and return to God, not time to dispute. The verse says no bequest, no return, showing that little while ends without warning. The perfect record (36:12) will show whether little while was used for disputing or for following Reminder (36:11) and giving thanks (36:35).

**How This Verse Connects to Resurrection**

No bequest and no return in this world prefigure no bequest and no return in next world without divine permission. On Day of Resurrection, when trumpet blown second time and they rush from graves to Lord (36:51), they will also not be able to make bequest or return to people; they will be brought before God (36:32, 36:53). The sūrah says *“On that Day no soul will be wronged, nor will you be rewarded except for what you used to do”* (36:54) — what was sent forth before blast is what counts; no new bequest after. The verse also connects to *“They will cry, Woe to us! Who has raised us from our place of rest?”* (36:52) — they will wish to return to place of rest, but cannot; they must go to Lord. The inability to make bequest or return at blast is preview of inability to change record at resurrection. Preparation must be before.

**A Verse That Teaches Urgency of Preparation**

This verse teaches urgency of preparation. Since blast can seize while disputing with no time for bequest or return, preparation must be now. The Qur'an says *“O you who believe, fear God and let every soul look to what it has sent forth for tomorrow”* (59:18). What is sent forth (36:12) is what remains after blast, not bequest made after. The believing man's bequest — follow messengers who ask no reward and are rightly guided (36:21) — was made before his death, and it remains as example. His return was to Paradise (36:26), not to people, because he sought return to Originator (36:22). The verse invites reader to make bequest now — donate from what Allah provided (36:47), follow Reminder (36:11), worship Originator (36:22) — and to return to God now, before forced return (36:83) when no bequest or return to people possible.

**The Link to Mercy and Authority**

Finally, no bequest and no return show that authority over all things is in God's Hands (36:83). Humans think they control bequest and return, but blast shows they do not. The sūrah says *“So glory be to the One in Whose Hands is authority over all things, and to Whom you will all be returned”* (36:83). Return to Him is final return, after which no return to people. Bequest in this world is limited; true bequest is what is sent forth to next world (36:12). The verse is short, but it holds complete warning: single blast seizes while disputing, leaving no time for last will or return, so prepare now by following those who ask no reward, worshiping Originator, giving thanks for provision, and seeking mercy while enjoyment for little while remains (36:44), before blast that will not allow bequest or return.
## Sūrah Yā-Sīn 36:51

> The Trumpet will be blown ˹a second time˺, then—behold!—they will rush from the graves to their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**Second Blast and Rushing From Graves**

*“The Trumpet will be blown a second time, then behold, they will rush from the graves to their Lord”* (36:51) describes resurrection after first blast that seized while disputing (36:49-50). The trumpet, ṣūr, is blown by angel Isrāfīl. First blow ends worldly life; second begins next life. The Qur'an says *“The Trumpet will be blown, and all who are in heavens and earth will swoon except whom God wills, then it will be blown again, and behold, they will stand looking”* (39:68). The interval between blows is said to be forty years in some reports. The verb yansilūn, rush, from nasala, to run quickly, indicates hasty movement, like rushing from farthest end of city (36:20) and rushing from graves. The sūrah says *“They will all be brought before Us”* (36:32, 36:53), and rushing from graves is how. The phrase to their Lord, ilā rabbihim, shows destination: return to Originator (36:22) and to One in Whose Hands is authority (36:83). Resurrection is not random scattering but directed rushing to Lord.

**Why Rushing Is Used**

Rushing indicates urgency and inevitability. Those in graves do not walk slowly; they rush, as if pulled. The Qur'an says *“On Day when they will rush from graves as if rushing to a goal”* (70:43). The goal is Lord. The rushing also mirrors worldly rushing: the believing man rushed from farthest end to support messengers (36:20); now all rush from graves to Lord. One rushing was voluntary toward guidance; other rushing is compulsory toward judgment. The sūrah says *“Indeed, on that Day residents of Paradise will be busy enjoying themselves”* (36:55), while others will rush in terror. The verb yansilūn suggests continuous, flowing rush, like water. The graves, ajdāth, are places of rest, marqad (36:52), from which they are raised. The raising is described as *“Who has raised us from our place of rest?”* (36:52), showing that graves were rest, now ended. The second blast ends rest and begins accountability.

**The Relation to First Blast**

First blast seized while disputing and left no time for bequest or return (36:49-50). Second blast raises from graves and gives no time to remain. Both are sudden, both at once, fa-idhā, behold. The first ends worldly disputes; second begins final judgment. The sūrah says *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will all be brought before Us”* (36:53), which can refer to second blast. The two blasts frame human existence: one ends it, one restarts it. The One who says *“Be, and it is”* (36:82) can end and restart with single command. The trumpet is means, but command is divine. The sūrah emphasizes We for acts only God does — We revive earth, We carry ancestors, We resurrect dead — and blowing trumpet is by God's command. The rushing from graves to Lord shows that return to God (36:83) is not optional; it is destination of rushing.

**How This Verse Supports Resurrection Argument**

This verse supports resurrection argument that runs through sūrah: dead earth revived (36:33), night stripped of day (36:37), sun fixed term (36:38), moon phases (36:39), fire from green trees (36:80), creation of heavens and earth (36:81). Rushing from graves is final proof that resurrection happens, not just possible. The sūrah earlier said *“It is certainly We who resurrect the dead, and write what they send forth and leave behind”* (36:12) as assertion; now it describes how — trumpet blown, rushing from graves. The description is vivid, meant to make resurrection imaginable. The Qur'an says *“They will come forth from graves as if they were scattered locusts, rushing to the caller”* (54:7-8). The image of rushing from graves is repeated to emphasize that resurrection is collective, swift, and directed.
